\section{Topology}
\label{sec_topology}

Needs: \cref{sec_imports}\\

Contains:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Contains the specialized lemmas that we need many times but that do not appear in a convenient way in mathlib
\end{itemize}

\begin{leanrmk}
    Some basic topology theorems we use over and over:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \verb|IsClosedMap.closure_image_eq_of_continuous| shows that $\overline{fA} = f\overline{A}$ as long as $f: X \to Y$ is a closed map
        
        \item \verb|IsCompact.image| the continuous image of a compact set is compact

        \item \verb|IsProperMap.isClosedMap| shows that a proper map is closed

        \item \verb|Continuous.isProperMap| shows that a continuous function from a compact space to a T2 space is proper.

        \item \verb|mem_closure_iff|: a point $x$ is in $\overline{A}$ if and only if for all open $U \ni x$, $U \cap \overline{A} \neq \emptyset$.

        \item \verb|IsClosed.isCompact|: a closed subset of a compact space is compact

        \item \verb|IsCompact.isClosed|: in a Hausdorff space, a compact set is closed

        \item \verb|isClosed_closure|: the closure of a set is closed
    \end{itemize}
\end{leanrmk}

Here's a result we use several times that we'll need ready access to in Lean.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image}
 \lean{imageClosureIsClosureImage}
 \leanok{}
    Let $f: X \to Y$ be a continuous map of compact Hausdorff spaces.  For all $A \subseteq X$, $\overline{fA} = f \overline{A}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    By \verb|Continuous.isProperMap|, $f$ is proper. Therefore, by \verb|IsProperMap.isClosedMap|, $f$ is closed. Therefore, by \verb|IsClosedMap.closure_image_eq_of_continuous|, $\overline{fA} = f \overline{A}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{leanrmk}
    Basic topology structures already in lean:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Hausdorff (T2) spaces: \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Topology/Separation/Hausdorff.html}

        \item Compact spaces: \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Topology/Defs/Filter.html} To go from a compact, Hausdorff space to a uniform space, use \verb|uniformSpaceOfCompactT2|. That this uniformity is unique is \verb|unique_uniformity_of_compact|.

        \item Uniform spaces: \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Topology/UniformSpace/Defs.html}

        \item Regular space:  \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Topology/Separation/Regular.html} A regular space is one in which closed sets and points are separable by disjoint open sets. \verb|UniformSpace.to_regularSpace| gives that a uniform space is regular.

        \item \verb|nhds| is the filter of neighborhoods of a point $x$ in a topological space $X$.  A ``neighborhood'' is a set which contains an open subset of $x$.
    \end{itemize}
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{leanrmk}
    Here are some useful facts about entourages in lean:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Entourages are elements of the filter in ``uniformity'' field in the uniform space class. But careful: in this file, we've been implicitly assuming an entourage is an open set. We would need to make this explicit when working in lean, since elements of the entourage filter are not necessarily open.  

        \item \verb|UniformSpace.ball|, given a point $x$ and an entourage $\alpha$, gives $\alpha[x]$, the open ball about $x$ according to the entourage.  This is all points $y$ so $(x,y) \in \alpha$.

        \item \verb|comp_open_symm_mem_uniformity_sets| shows that all entourages contain a symmetric entourage

        \item We frequently want to apply $\pi \otimes \pi$.  The structure for this in lean is \verb|Prod.map|.  See also \verb|Prod.map_apply| for a shortcut
    \end{itemize}
\end{leanrmk}

In what follows, we will need to consider systems whose phase spaces are non-metrizable.  All phase spaces will be compact Hausdorff spaces.  We may have luck finding results in mathlib concerning uniform spaces.  The two are connected as follows.

\begin{theorem}
    A compact Hausdorff space is a uniform space with a unique uniformity given by the open neighborhoods of the diagonal.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    This is \verb|unique_uniformity_of_compact| in mathlib.

    (\verb|nhdsSet_diagonal_eq_uniformity| will be easier to use.)
\end{proof}

We can make our definitions in the appropriate generality by making them for uniform spaces.  The disadvantage then is if we want to specialize to compact Hausdorff spaces and the previous theorem is not in mathlib, we'd need to prove it.

\begin{leanrmk}
    Baire space in Mathlib:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \verb|BaireSpace| is a class that says a topology has the Baire property

        \item \verb|BaireSpace.of_t2Space_locallyCompactSpace| is an instance from which we can get a BaireSpace type from a compact Hausdorff space.  To do this, in a proof, one can write
        \begin{center}\verb|have : BaireSpace X := by infer_instance|\end{center}
    \end{itemize}
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_entourage_regularity_at_a_point}
    \label{lemma_closed_nbhd_prod_in_diag}
    \lean{openClosureProductInEntourage}
    \leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a uniform space, $x \in X$, and $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ be an entourage.  There exists an open set $U \ni x$ such that $\overline{U} \times \overline{U} \subseteq \alpha$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Put the following things in order:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \verb|UniformSpace.ball x \alpha| will give the element of \verb|nbhd x| corresponding to $\alpha$
        
        \item Since $X$ is uniform, by \verb|UniformSpace.to_regularSpace|, it is regular
        
        \item \verb|exists_mem_nhds_isClosed_subset| gives that since $X$ is regular, that there is a closed neighborhood $V$ of $x$ contained in \verb|UniformSpace.ball x \alpha|
    \end{itemize}
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Substitute for semiopenness of a factor map]
    \label{lemma_image_of_entourage_somewhere_dense_along_diagonal}
    \lean{openProductInEntourageImage}
    \leanok{}
    Let $\pi: X \to Y$ be a surjection of compact Hausdorff spaces. For all entourages $\alpha \subseteq X^2$, there exists a non-empty, open set $V \subseteq Y$ such that
    \[V \times V \subseteq (\pi \otimes \pi) \alpha.\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_entourage_regularity_at_a_point}
\leanok{}
    Let $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ be an entourage.  For all $x \in X$, the set $\alpha[x]$ is an open neighborhood of $x$.  By \cref{lemma_entourage_regularity_at_a_point}, there exists a non-empty, open set $U_x \subseteq X$ containing $x$ such that $\overline{U_x} \times \overline{U_x} \subseteq \alpha$.

    By compactness, there exists a finite set $F \subseteq X$ such that $\cup_{x \in F} U_x = X$. Thus $\cup_{x \in F} \overline{U_x} = X$.  Since $\pi$ is surjective, $\cup_{x \in F} \pi \overline{U_x} = Y$. 
    Since $\overline{U_x}$ is compact and $\pi$ is continuous, $\pi \overline{U_x}$ is closed for all $x$.  Since $Y$ is compact, Hausdorff, it is a Baire space, so there exists $x \in F$ such that $\pi \overline{U_x}$ has non-empty interior.  Let $V \subseteq Y$ be non-empty, open such that $V \subseteq \pi \overline{U_x}$.  Then
    \[V \times V \subseteq \pi \overline{U_x} \times \pi \overline{U_x} = (\pi \otimes \pi) (\overline{U_x} \times \overline{U_x}) \subseteq (\pi \otimes \pi)\alpha,\]
    as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_open_nbhd_of_fiber_contains_open_preimage}
\lean{existOpenNeighborhoodPreImageContainedIn}
\leanok{}
    Let $\pi: X \to Y$ be a continuous map between compact Hausdorff spaces. (not necessarily surjective).  For all $y \in Y$ and open $W \subseteq X$ such that $\pi^{-1} y \subseteq W$, there exists open $U \subseteq Y$ with $y \in U$ such that $\pi^{-1} U \subseteq W$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    By compactness and the fact that $\pi$ is a surjection, $\pi (X \setminus W)$ is closed and does not contain $y$.  Set $U = Y \setminus \pi(X \setminus W)$, and check that $y \in U$ and $\pi^{-1} U \subseteq W$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_commuting_map_algebra}
    \lean{preimageOfCommutingMapsCommutes}
    Let $X$ be a set and $f, g : X \to X$ be commuting maps.  For all $A \subseteq X$, $f g^{-1} A \subseteq g^{-1} f A$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Note $g f g^{-1} A = f g g^{-1} A \subseteq f A$, whereby $f g^{-1} A \subseteq g^{-1} f A$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_quotient_is_compact_hausdorff}
\lean{quotientByCERIsCompactHausdorff}
    Let $X$ be a compact Hausdorff space, and let $R \subseteq X^2$ be a closed equivalence relation.  The quotient $X/R$, endowed with the quotient topology, is a compact Hausdorff space.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Since the quotient map $\pi : X \to X/R$ is continuous and $X$ is compact, we have that $X/R$ is compact.  To see that $X/R$ is Hausdorff, let $y, z \in X/R$ be distinct. Since $R$ is closed, the sets $\pi^{-1}\{y\}$ and $\pi^{-1}\{z\}$ are disjoint closed subsets of $X$.
    Because $X$ is compact Hausdorff, it is a normal space. Thus there exist disjoint open sets $U, V \subseteq X$ such that $\pi^{-1}\{y\} \subseteq U$ and $\pi^{-1}\{z\} \subseteq V$.

    Let $\widetilde{U} = \pi(U^c)^c$ and $\widetilde{V} = \pi(V^c)^c$. It is easy to see that $\widetilde{U} \cap \widetilde{V} = \emptyset$, that $y \in \widetilde{U}$, and that $z \in \widetilde{V}$. It remains to show $\widetilde{U}$ and $\widetilde{V}$ are open. For this, it suffices to show $\pi$ is a closed map, but it is a standard fact that continuous maps of compact Hausdorff spaces are closed.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_uniform_near_diag}
\lean{nbhdOfDiagForcesOtherSetContainment}
\leanok{}
Let $X$ be a compact Hausdorff space.  Let $y \in X$ and $U \subseteq X$ be open and contain $y$.  There exists an open set $V \subseteq X$ containing $y$ and an open set $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ that contains the diagonal such that for all $x, z \in X$, if $(x,z) \in \alpha$ and $z \in V$, then $x \in U$.    
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    By the regularity of $X$, there exists an open set $V \subseteq X$ containing $y$ and such that $\overline{V} \subseteq U$.  Put $K = X \setminus U$, and define
    \[\alpha = X^2 \setminus (K \times \overline{V}).\]
    The set $\alpha$ is clearly open, and it contains the diagonal since $K \cap \overline{V} = \emptyset$.  Finally, suppose $x, z \in X$ are such that $(x,z) \in \alpha$ and $z \in V$.  It must be that $x \in X \setminus K = U$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\section{Semigroups}
\label{sec_semigroups}

Needs: \cref{sec_imports}

Contains:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Contains semigroup algebra: ideals, idempotency, etc...
\end{itemize}

\begin{leanrmk}
    In mathlib:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Semigroup: \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Algebra/Group/Defs.html}

        \item Results around subsemigroups appear in Mathlib.Algebra.Group.Subsemigroup.  Some useful things:
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item Definition: \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Algebra/Group/Subsemigroup/Defs.html}

            \item Subsemigroup closure (the subsemigroup generated by a set): \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Algebra/Group/Subsemigroup/Basic.html}
        \end{enumerate}

        \item Idempotents: \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Algebra/Group/Idempotent.html}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{leanrmk}
    Missing from Mathlib are definitions around ideals in semigroups.
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{definition}[{Left ideal, \cite[Def. 1.26]{hindman_strauss_book_2012}}]
\lean{SemigroupLeftIdeal}
\leanok{}
    A left ideal of semigroup $S$ is a non-empty subset $L \subseteq S$ such that for all $s \in S$, $s L \subseteq L$.
\end{definition}



\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_intersection_of_ideals_is_ideal}
    \lean{nonemptyInterOfLeftIdealsIsLeftIdeal}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, and let $L_i$ be a collection of left ideals of $S$.  The intersection $\bigcap_i L_i$, if non-empty, is a left ideal of $S$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Suppose $\bigcap_i L_i$ is non-empty.  We need only show that for all $s \in S$, $s\bigcap_i L_i \subseteq \bigcap_i L_i$.  Let $i \in I$.  Since $L_i$ is a left ideal,
    \[s \bigcap_i L_i \subseteq s L_i \subseteq L_i,\]
    as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[{Minimal left ideal, \cite[Def. 1.27]{hindman_strauss_book_2012}}]
    \lean{isMinLeftIdeal}
    \leanok{}
    A minimal left ideal of semigroup $S$ is a left ideal $L$ with the property that for all left ideals $J \subseteq S$ with $J \subseteq L$, $J = L$.  (That is, $L$ is a minimal element by containment amongst left ideals.)
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Idempotent]
    \lean{IsIdempotentElem}
    \mathlibok{}
    An element $s$ of a semigroup $S$ is idempotent if $ss = s$.
\end{definition}


\section{Family algebra}
\label{sec_family_algebra}

Needs: \cref{sec_imports}

Contains:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Contains the abstract Furstenberg family algebra definitions and results
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Definitions}

Let $S$ be a set.

\begin{definition}[Collection]
    A \emph{collection} of subsets of $S$ is an element of $\collections{S}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Nonempty collection]
    A collection $\family \in \families{S}$ is \emph{nonempty} if $\family \neq \emptyset$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Proper collection]
    A collection $\family \in \families{S}$ is \emph{proper} if $\family \neq \powerset{S}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Upward closed collection]
    A collection of sets $\family \in \collections{S}$ is \emph{upward closed} if for all $A, B \subseteq S$, (($A \in \family$ and $A \subseteq B$) implies $B \in \family$).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Family of subsets of $S$]
    A collection of sets $\family \in \collections{S}$ is a \emph{family} if it is upward closed.  We denote the set of families on $S$ by $\families{S}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Partition regular family]
    A family $\family \in \families{S}$ is \emph{partition regular family} if for all $A, B \subseteq S$, $A \cup B \in \family$ implies $A \in \family$ or $B \in \family$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Filter family]
    A family $\family \in \families{S}$ is a \emph{filter family} if for all $A, B \subseteq S$, $A \in \family$ and $B \in \family$ implies $A \cap B \in \family$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Ultrafilter family]
    A family $\family \in \families{S}$ is an \emph{ultrafilter family} if it is both a partition regular family and a filter family.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Set lattice}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\cup: \families{S}^2 \to \families{S}$
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The union of two families is a family
        \item The union of two filters is not necessarily a filter
        \item The union of two partition regular families is partition regular
    \end{enumerate}
    \item $\cap : \families{S}^2 \to \families{S}$
        \begin{enumerate}
        \item The intersection of two families is a family
        \item The intersection of two filters is a filter
        \item The intersection of two partition regular families is not necessarily partition regular
    \end{enumerate}
    \item $(\families{S}, \cup, \cap)$ is a sub-lattice of $(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(S)),\cup,\cap)$
    \item By \cref{thm_de_morgan_union_v1} and \cref{thm_de_morgan_union_v2}, I believe that $(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(S)),\cup,\cap,*)$ is something like an involutive lattice
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Dual families}

\begin{definition}[Dual map]
    The \emph{dual} map $*: \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(S)) \to \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(S))$ is defined by 
    \[\family^* = \{A \subseteq S : \forall B \in \family, \ A \cap B \neq \emptyset\}.\]
    For $\family \in \collections{S}$, its image $\family^*$ is the \emph{dual} of $\family$.
\end{definition}

\vskip0.5cm

Facts on the level of collections:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $*$ is antitone: for collections $\family$ and $\familytwo$, if $\family \subseteq \familytwo$, then $\familytwo^* \subseteq \family^*$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_dual_is_antitone}
    \end{itemize}

    \item For all collections $\family$ and $\familytwo$, $(\family \cup \familytwo)^* = \family^* \cap \familytwo^*$ and $(\family \cap \familytwo)^* = \family^* \cup \familytwo^*$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_de_morgan_union_v1} and \cref{thm_de_morgan_union_v2}
    \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\vskip0.5cm

Facts on the level of families:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $*$ maps families to families, that is, $*: \families{S} \to \families{S}$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam}
        \item Note that the edge cases $\emptyset^* = \mathcal{P}(S)$ and $\mathcal{P}(S)^* = \emptyset$ hold by strict interpretation of the definition.  Thus, in the theorems proving facts about the dual, there are no separate edge cases to consider.
        \item There is a more general statement here we might favor: $*$ takes $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(S))$ to $\families{S}$.
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item Useful equivalent formulation: for all families $\family$,
    \[\family^* = \{A \subseteq S : S \setminus A \not\in \family\}\]
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_equiv_fam_dual_formulation}
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item $*$ is an involution on families: for all families $\family$, $(\family^*)^* = \family$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
        \item There is a more general statement we might favor: $(\family^*)^*$ is the upward-closure of $\family$.
    \end{itemize}

    \item $*: \families{S} \to \families{S}$ is a bijection
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_dual_is_bijection_on_fams}
    \end{itemize}

    \item For all families $\family$, $\family$ is partition regular if and only if $\family^*$ is a filter
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_pr_iff_dual_is_filter}
    \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\clearpage

\subsection{Family join}

\begin{definition}[Family join]
    \dgg{Updated!} The \emph{family join} map $\classcap: \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(S))^2 \to \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(S))$ is defined by
    \[\family \classcap \familytwo = \big\{C \subseteq S : \exists A \in \family, \ \exists B \in \familytwo, \ A \cap B = C \big\}\]
    % OLD \[\family \classcap \familytwo = \begin{cases} \{A \cap B : A \in \family, \ B \in \familytwo\} & \text{if $\family \neq \emptyset$ and $\familytwo \neq \emptyset$} \\ \familytwo & \text{if $\family = \emptyset$} \\ \family & \text{if $\familytwo = \emptyset$} \end{cases}\]
    The (family) join of collections $\family, \familytwo \in \collections{S}$ is $\family \classcap \familytwo$.
\end{definition}

Note that if $\family = \emptyset$ or $\familytwo = \emptyset$, then by definition, $\family \classcap \familytwo = \emptyset$.

\vskip0.5cm

Facts on the level of collections:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \dgg{Updated!} if $\familytwo \neq \emptyset$, then $\family \subseteq \family \classcap \familytwo$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcap_contains_union}
    \end{itemize}

    \item $\classcap$ is commutative: $\family \classcap \familytwo = \familytwo \classcap \family$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcap_commutative}
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item $\classcap$ is associative: $\family \classcap (\familytwo \classcap \familythree) = (\family \classcap \familytwo) \classcap \familythree$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcap_associative}
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item $\classcap$ is monotone: if $\family_1 \subseteq \family_2$ and $\familytwo_1 \subseteq \familytwo_2$, then $\family_1 \classcap \familytwo_1 \subseteq \family_2 \classcap \familytwo_2$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcap_monotone}
    \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\vskip0.5cm

Facts on the level of families:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item The map $\classcap$ takes pairs of families to families, that is, $\classcap: \families{S}^2 \to \families{S}$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_family_classcap_is_family}
    \end{itemize}

    \item \dgg{New!}  $\family \classcap \familytwo \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$ iff $\family \subseteq \familytwo^*$; in particular, $\family \classcap \family^* \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcap_full_condition}
    \end{itemize}

    \item $\family \classcap \family^*$ is partition regular
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_family_classcap_dual_is_partition_regular}
    \end{itemize}

    \item $\family \classcap \family = \family$ if and only if $\family$ is a filter
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcap_idempotent_at_filters}
    \end{itemize}

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Family meet}

\begin{definition}[Family meet]
    \dgg{Updated!} The \emph{family meet} map $\classcapdual: \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(S))^2 \to \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(S))$ is defined by
    \[\family \classcapdual \familytwo = \big\{A \subseteq S : \forall B \in \family^*, \ A \cap B \in \familytwo \big\}.\]
    % \[\family \classcapdual \familytwo = \begin{cases} \big\{A \subseteq S : \forall B \in \family^*, \ A \cap B \in \familytwo \big\} & \text{if $\family \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$ and $\familytwo \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$} \\ \familytwo & \text{if $\family = \mathcal{P}(S)$} \\ \family & \text{if $\familytwo = \mathcal{P}(S)$} \end{cases}\]
    The (family) meet of collections $\family, \familytwo \in \collections{S}$ is $\family \classcapdual \familytwo$.
\end{definition}

\vskip0.5cm

Facts on the level of collections:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \dgg{Updated!} If $\familytwo \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$, then $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \subseteq \family$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcapdual_contained_in_intersection}
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item $\classcapdual$ is commutative
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcapdual_commutative}
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item $\classcapdual$ is associative
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcapdual_associative}
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item $\classcapdual$ is monotone
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcapdual_monotone}
    \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\vskip0.5cm

Facts on the level of families:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item The map $\classcapdual$ takes pairs of families to families, that is, $\classcapdual: \families{S}^2 \to \families{S}$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_family_classcapdual_is_family}
    \end{itemize}

    \item \dgg{New!} $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \neq \emptyset$ iff $\family^* \subseteq \familytwo$. In particular, $\family \classcapdual \family^* \neq \emptyset$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcapdual_empty_condition}
    \end{itemize}

    \item $\family \classcapdual \family^*$ is a filter
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_family_classcapdual_dual_is_filter}
    \end{itemize}

    \item $\family \classcapdual \family = \family$ if and only if $\family$ is partition regular
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_classcapdual_idempotent_at_pr_families}
    \end{itemize}    
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Algebra involving both meet and join}

\dgg{These identities are more clearly spelled out in the ``Delta'' paper.}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item DeMorgan-type laws: for all collections $\family$, $\familytwo$, $(\family \classcap \familytwo)^* = \family^* \classcapdual \familytwo^*$ and $(\family \classcapdual \familytwo)^* = \family^* \classcap \familytwo^*$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_de_morgan_v1} and \cref{thm_de_morgan_v1_dual}
    \end{itemize}

    \item (Partial modularity) $\family \classcap (\familytwo \classcapdual \familythree) \subseteq (\family \classcap \familytwo) \classcapdual \familythree$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: Let $A \in \family \classcap (\familytwo \classcapdual \familythree)$.  Then there is $B \in \family$ and $C \in \familytwo \classcapdual \familythree$ such that $A = B \cap C$.  Let $D \in \familythree^*$.  We need $A \cap D \in \family \classcap \familytwo$.  But $A \cap D = B \cap D \cap C$, where $B \in \family$ and, since $C \in \familythree \classcapdual \familytwo$ and $D \in \familythree^*$, $D \cap C \in \familytwo$.

        \item Dual: $(\family \classcapdual \familytwo) \classcap \familythree \subseteq \family \classcapdual (\familytwo \classcap \familythree)$

        \item Equality does not hold in dual statement, eg: $(\syndetic \classcapdual \thick) \classcap \dcsyndetic = \dcsyndetic$, whereas $\syndetic \classcapdual (\thick \classcap \dcsyndetic)$ is strong central.
    \end{itemize}

    \item (Partial distributivity) $(\family \classcapdual \familytwo) \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familythree) \subseteq \family \classcapdual (\familytwo \classcap \familythree)$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: $(\family \classcapdual \familytwo) \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familythree) \subseteq (\family \classcapdual \familytwo) \classcap \familythree \subseteq \family \classcapdual (\familytwo \classcap \familythree)$, where the second containment comes from the previous point.

        \item Equality does not hold in general: set $\family = \syndetic$, $\familytwo = \thick$, and $\familythree = \syndetic$.

        \item Dual: (write it)
    \end{itemize}

    \item $\family^* \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familytwo) \subseteq \familytwo$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: If $A \in \family \classcapdual \familytwo$ and $B \in \family^*$, then $B \cap A \in \familytwo$.

        \item Dual: $\family \classcapdual (\family^* \classcap \familytwo) \supseteq \familytwo$

        \item Corollary: $\family \classcapdual (\family^* \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familytwo)) = \family \classcapdual \familytwo$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item Proof: From previous point, $\family^* \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familytwo) \subseteq \familytwo$, so by monotonicity, $\family \classcapdual (\family^* \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familytwo)) \subseteq \family \classcapdual \familytwo$.  Conversely, from previous point dual,  $\family \classcapdual (\family^* \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familytwo)) \supseteq \family \classcapdual \familytwo$.  
        \end{itemize}

    \end{itemize}
    
    \item $\family^* \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familytwo) \subseteq \familythree$ if and only if $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \subseteq \family \classcapdual \familythree$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: (quick to check by defs)

        \item Alternative proof: Use the previous point to apply $\family \classcapdual$ to go in one direction.  Apply $\family^* \classcap$ to go in the other.

        \item Related: $\family^* \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familytwo) \supseteq \familythree$ if and only if $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \supseteq \family \classcapdual \familythree$

        \item \dgg{To do: use the previous point to prove this via some sort of cancellativity?}
    \end{itemize}

    \item (Partial absorption) $\family \classcapdual (\family \classcap \familytwo) \subseteq \family$
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: $\family \classcapdual (\family \classcap \familytwo) \subseteq \family \cap (\family \classcap \familytwo) \subseteq \family$

        \item There is not equality in general.  Take, eg, $\family$ and $\familytwo$ to be two distinct, proper, non-ultrafilters filters.  Then $\family \classcapdual (\family \classcap \familytwo)$ is trivial.

        \item Lack of absorption shows that $(\families{S}, \classcap, \classcapdual)$ is not a lattice.

        \item Dual: $\family \classcap (\family \classcapdual \familytwo) \supseteq \family$
    \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Proofs}

Throughout this section, $S$ is a fixed set, and all families are families of subsets of $S$.

\subsubsection{Set lattice facts}

\dgg{I'm waiting to include anything here because it may depend on what is available already in lean.  For example, will lean let us write that the union of two collections is a collection with no issue, or will we need to state and prove that?}

\subsubsection{Dual facts}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam}
    \lean{Family.famDual}
    If $\family$ is a family, then $\family^*$ is a family.  That is, $\cdot^*: \families{S} \to \families{S}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    By the definition of family, we need only check that $\family^*$ is an upward closed collection of subsets of $S$.  It is, by definition, a collection of subsets of $S$.  If $A \in \family^*$ and $A \subseteq A' \subseteq S$, to show that $A' \in \family^*$, we need only to show that for all $B \in \family$, $A' \cap B \neq \emptyset$.  Since $A \in \family^*$, we have that $\emptyset \neq A \cap B \subseteq A' \cap B$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_equiv_fam_dual_formulation}
    \leanok{}
    \lean{famDualAlt}
    For all families $\family$, $\family^* = \{A \subseteq S \mid S \setminus A \not\in \family\}$
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
In the following list, each statement is equivalent to the one that follows it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $A \in \family^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}
        \item for all $B \in \family$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] set theory: for $X, Y \subseteq Z$, $X \cap Y = \emptyset$ iff $Y \subseteq Z \setminus X$
        \end{itemize}
        \item for all $B \in \family$, $B \not\subseteq S \setminus A$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] logic: negation of a universal quantifier
        \end{itemize}
        \item not ( there exists $B \in \family$, $B \subseteq S \setminus A$ )
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] $\family$ is upward closed
        \end{itemize}
        \item not ( $S \setminus A \in \family$ )
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{leanrmk}
    In lean: \verb|Function.Involutive|
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
    \lean{dualIsInvolutionOnFamilies}
    $*$ is an involution on the set families: $(\family^*)^* = \family$
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam,thm_equiv_fam_dual_formulation}
    \leanok{}
    By \cref{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam}, it suffices to show that for all $\family \in \families{S}$, $(\family^*)^* = \family$.
    In the following list, each statement is equivalent to the one that follows it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $A \in (\family^*)^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] \cref{thm_equiv_fam_dual_formulation}
        \end{itemize}
        \item $S \setminus A \not\in \family^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] \cref{thm_equiv_fam_dual_formulation}
        \end{itemize}
        \item $S \setminus (S \setminus A) \in \family$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] set theory: for $X \subseteq Z$, $Z \setminus (Z \setminus X) = X$
        \end{itemize}
        \item $A \in \family$
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{leanrmk}
    In lean: \verb|Function.Bijective|
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_dual_is_bijection_on_fams}
    \lean{dualIsBijectionOnFamilies}
    $\cdot^*: \families{S} \to \families{S}$ is a bijection
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
    This follows from \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams} and the general fact that involutions are always bijective.  \dgg{Can we find this general fact in the lean library?}
\end{proof}
% By \cref{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam}, $\cdot^*: \families{S} \to \families{S}$, so we need only show that it is surjective and injective.
% $*$ is surjective: Let $\family$ be a family.  By \cref{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam}, $\family^*$ is a family and by \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}, $(\family^*)^* = \family$, so $\family$ is in the range of $*$.
% $*$ is injective: Suppose $\family^* = \family$

\begin{theorem}[Dual is antitone]
    \label{thm_dual_is_antitone}
    \leanok{}
    \lean{dualIsAntitone}
    For all collections $\family, \familytwo \in \collections{S}$, if $\family \subseteq \familytwo$, then $\familytwo^* \subseteq \family^*$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{leanrmk}
    In Mathlib.Order.Monotone, see \verb|Antitone (f : a → b)|
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{proof}
    Let $\family, \familytwo \in \collections{S}$. In the following list, each statement implies the following one.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $A \in \familytwo^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}
        \item for all $B \in \familytwo$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] $\family \subseteq \familytwo$
        \end{itemize}
        \item for all $B \in \family$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}
        \item $A \in \family^*$
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}
% Suppose $\family \subseteq \familytwo$.  Let $A \in \familytwo^*$.  To see that $A \in \family^*$, we must show that for all $B \in \family$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$.  Let $B \in \family$.  Since $\family \subseteq \familytwo$, $B \in \familytwo$.  Since $A \in \familytwo^*$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$, as desired.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_pr_iff_dual_is_filter}
    \label{thm_family_pr_iff_dual_is_filter}
    \lean{familyIsPRIffDualIsFilter}
    $\family$ is a partition regular family if and only if $\family^*$ is a filter
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam,thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams,thm_equiv_fam_dual_formulation}
    By \cref{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam} and \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}, $\family$ is family if and only if $\family^*$ is a family.  Then, in the following list, each statement is equivalent to the one that follows it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\family$ is a partition regular family
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}

        \item for all $A, B \subseteq S$, if $A \cup B \in \family$, then $A \in \family$ or $B \in \family$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
        \end{itemize}

        \item for all $A, B \subseteq S$, if $A \cup B \in (\family^*)^*$, then $A \in (\family^*)^*$ or $B \in (\family^*)^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] \cref{thm_equiv_fam_dual_formulation}
        \end{itemize}

        \item for all $A, B \subseteq S$, if $S \setminus (A \cup B) \not\in \family^*$, then $S \setminus A \not\in \family^*$ or $S \setminus B \not\in \family^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] set theory: for $X, Y \subseteq Z$, $Z \setminus (X \cup Y) = (Z \setminus X) \cap (Z \setminus Y)$
        \end{itemize}

        \item for all $A, B \subseteq S$, if $(S \setminus A) \cap (S \setminus B) \not\in \family^*$, then $S \setminus A \not\in \family^*$ or $S \setminus B \not\in \family^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] logic: ``if not $p$, then not $q$ OR not $r$'' is equivalent to ``if $q$ and $r$, then $p$''
        \end{itemize}

        \item for all $A, B \subseteq S$, if $S \setminus A \in \family^*$ and $S \setminus B \in \family^*$, then $(S \setminus A) \cap (S \setminus B) \in \family^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] write $C = S \setminus A$ and $D = S \setminus B$ and use set theory: for $X \subseteq Z$, $Z \setminus (Z \setminus X) = X$
        \end{itemize}

        \item for all $C, D \subseteq S$, if $C \in \family^*$ and $D \in \family^*$, then $C \cap D \in \family^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}

        \item $\family^*$ is a filter
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

% \label{thm_family_equal_to_dual_iff_ultrafilter}
%     $\family = \family^*$ if and only if $\family$ is an ultrafilter family
% \end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Set DeMorgan 1]
    \label{thm_de_morgan_union_v1}
    \leanok{}
    \lean{familySetDeMorganLaw1}
    For all collections $\family$, $\familytwo$, $(\family \cup \familytwo)^* = \family^* \cap \familytwo^*$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\family, \familytwo \in \collections{S}$. In the following list, each statement is equivalent to the one that follows it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $A \in (\family \cup \familytwo)^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}

        \item for all $B \in \family \cup \familytwo$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] logic
        \end{itemize}

        \item for all $B \in \family$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$ and for all $B \in \familytwo$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}

        \item $A \in \family^*$ and $A \in \familytwo^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] logic
        \end{itemize}

        \item $A \in \family^* \cap \familytwo^*$
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Set DeMorgan 2]
    \label{thm_de_morgan_union_v2}
    \leanok{}
    \lean{familySetDeMorganLaw2}
    For all collections $\family$, $\familytwo$, $(\family \cap \familytwo)^* = \family^* \cup \familytwo^*$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_de_morgan_union_v1,thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
    Apply \cref{thm_de_morgan_union_v1} to $\family^*$ and $\familytwo^*$ to see that $(\family^* \cup \familytwo^*)^* = (\family^*)^* \cap (\familytwo^*)^*$.  By \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}, we have that $(\family^* \cup \familytwo^*)^* = \family \cap \familytwo$.  Taking the dual and again and using \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams} again, we see $\family^* \cup \familytwo^* = (\family \cap \familytwo)^*$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Family join}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_family_classcap_is_family}
% Does this already have a name in lean?
    If $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are families, then $\family \classcap \familytwo$ is a family.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \dgg{Updated!} By definition, $\family \classcap \familytwo$ is a collection of subsets of $S$.  We need to show that it is upward closed.

    % If $\family = \emptyset$ or $\familytwo = \emptyset$, then $\family \classcap \familytwo$ is a family, and hence is upward closed.  Assume $\family \neq \emptyset$ and $\familytwo \neq \emptyset$.  We will show that $\family \classcap \familytwo$ is upward closed.

    Let $A \in \family \classcap \familytwo$ and $A \subseteq A' \subseteq S$.  We must show that $A' \in \family \classcap \familytwo$.  By definition, there exists $B \in \family$ and $C \in \familytwo$ such that $A = B \cap C$.  Define $B' = B \cup A'$ and $C' = C \cup A'$.

    We claim that
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $B' \in \family$,
        \item $C' \in \familytwo$,
        \item $B' \cap C' = A'$.
    \end{enumerate}
    If we show all three, then we are done, since it will follow that $A' \in \family \classcap \familytwo$.
    
    (1) Since $B \in \family$, $\family$ is upward closed, and $B \subseteq B' \subseteq S$, we have that $B' \in \family$.

    (2) Since $C \in \familytwo$, $\familytwo$ is upward closed, and $C \subseteq C' \subseteq S$, we have that $C' \in \familytwo$.

    (3) From set theory, union distributes over intersection ($X \cup (Y \cap Z) = (X \cup Y) \cap (X \cup Z)$).  We see
    \[B' \cap C' = (B \cup A') \cap (C \cup A') = (B \cap C) \cup A' = A \cup A'.\]
    Since $A \subseteq A'$, $A \cup A' = A'$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_classcap_contains_union}
    \lean{familyJoinContainsUnion}
    \dgg{Updated!} If $\familytwo \neq \emptyset$, then $\family \subseteq \family \classcap \familytwo$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    % If $\family = \emptyset$ or $\familytwo = \emptyset$, this is immediate by definition.
    
    % Assume $\family \neq \emptyset$ and $\familytwo \neq \emptyset$. We will show $\family \subseteq \family \classcap \familytwo$ and $\familytwo \subseteq \family \classcap \familytwo$.

    \dgg{Updated!} Let $A \in \family$.  Since $\familytwo \neq \emptyset$ and $\familytwo$ is upward closed, $S \in \familytwo$.  By definition $A = A \cap S \in \family \classcap \familytwo$.  Therefore, $\family \subseteq \family \classcap \familytwo$.
\end{proof}
    % Let $B \in \familytwo$.  Since $\family \neq \emptyset$ and $\family$ is upward closed, $S \in \family$.  By definition $B = S \cap B \in \family \classcap \familytwo$. Therefore, $\familytwo \subseteq \family \classcap \familytwo$.


\begin{theorem}[$\classcap$ is commutative]
    \label{thm_classcap_commutative}
    \leanok{}
    \lean{thmfamilyJoinIsCommutative}
    For all $\family, \familytwo \in \collections{S}$,
    \[\family \classcap \familytwo = \familytwo \classcap \family.\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \dgg{Updated!}
    Let $\family, \familytwo \in \collections{S}$.  We must show that
    \[\family \classcap \familytwo = \familytwo \classcap \family.\]
    
    % If $\family = \emptyset$ or $\familytwo = \emptyset$, this is immediate by definition.  Assume $\family \neq \emptyset$ and $\familytwo \neq \emptyset$ so that
    % \[\family \classcap \familytwo = \{A \cap B : A \in \family, \ B \in \familytwo\}.\]
    Let $X \subseteq S$. Each line is equivalent to the one that follows it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $X \in \family \classcap \familytwo$
        \item there exists $A \in \family, \ B \in \familytwo$ such that $X = A \cap B$
        \item there exists $A \in \family, \ B \in \familytwo$ such that $X = B \cap A$
        \item $X \in \familytwo \classcap \family$
    \end{enumerate}
    Therefore, $\family \classcap \familytwo = \familytwo \classcap \family$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[$\classcap$ is associative]
    \label{thm_classcap_associative}
    \lean{familyJoinIsAssociative}
    For all $\family, \familytwo, \familythree \in \collections{S}$,
    \[(\family \classcap \familytwo) \classcap \familythree = \family \classcap (\familytwo \classcap \familythree).\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \dgg{Updated!} Let $\family, \familytwo, \familythree \in \collections{S}$.  We must show that
    \[(\family \classcap \familytwo) \classcap \familythree = \family \classcap (\familytwo \classcap \familythree).\]
    % If $\family = \emptyset$ or $\familytwo = \emptyset$ or $\familythree = \emptyset$, it is quick to check that this equality holds.  Therefore, we will assume $\family, \familytwo, \familythree \neq \emptyset$.

    % Since $\family, \familytwo \neq \emptyset$,
    % \[\family \classcap \familytwo = \{A \cap B : A \in \family, \ B \in \familytwo\}.\]
    % Similarly, since $\familytwo, \familythree \neq \emptyset$,
    % \[\familytwo \classcap \familythree = \{A \cap B : A \in \familytwo, \ B \in \familythree\}.\]

    Let $X \subseteq S$. Each line is equivalent to the one that follows it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $X \in (\family \classcap \familytwo) \classcap \familythree$
        \item there exists $A \in (\family \classcap \familytwo)$ and $B \in \familythree$ such that $X = A \cap B$
        \item there exists $C \in \family, \ D \in \familytwo$, and $B \in \familythree$ such that $X = (C \cap D) \cap B$
        \item there exists $C \in \family, \ D \in \familytwo$, and $B \in \familythree$ such that $X = C \cap (D \cap B)$
        \item there exists $C \in \family$ and $E \in \familytwo \classcap \familythree$ such that $X = C \cap E$
        \item $X \in \family \classcap (\familytwo \classcap \familythree)$
    \end{enumerate}
    Therefore, $(\family \classcap \familytwo) \classcap \familythree = \family \classcap (\familytwo \classcap \familythree)$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[$\classcap$ is monotone]
    \label{thm_classcap_monotone}
    \lean{familyJoinIsMonotone}
    For all $\family_1, \family_2, \familytwo_1, \familytwo_2 \in \collections{S}$, if $\family_1 \subseteq \family_2$ and $\familytwo_1 \subseteq \familytwo_2$, then $\family_1 \classcap \familytwo_1 \subseteq \family_2 \classcap \familytwo_2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_classcap_contains_union}
    % If $\family_1 = \emptyset$, then by the definition of $\classcap$ and \cref{thm_classcap_contains_union},
    % \[\family_1 \classcap \familytwo_1 = \familytwo_1 \subseteq \family_2 \cup \familytwo_2 \subseteq \family_2 \classcap \familytwo_2.\]
    % By the analogous argument, we reach the same conclusion if $\familytwo_1 = \emptyset$.

    % If $\family_2 = \emptyset$ or $\familytwo_2 = \emptyset$, then $\family_1 = \emptyset$ or $\familytwo_1 = \emptyset$, and we reach the desired conclusion by the previous paragraph.

    % Therefore, we may assume that none of $\family_1, \family_2, \familytwo_1, \familytwo_2$ are empty.
    \dgg{Updated!} Let $X \subseteq S$.  Each line follows from the previous one:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $X \in \family_1 \classcap \familytwo_1$
        \item there exists $A \in \family_1$ and $B \in \familytwo_1$ such that $X = A \cap B$
        \item there exists $A \in \family_2$ and $B \in \familytwo_2$ such that $X = A \cap B$
        \item $X \in \family_2 \classcap \familytwo_2$
    \end{enumerate}
    Therefore, $\family_1 \classcap \familytwo_1 \subseteq \family_2 \classcap \familytwo_2$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_family_classcap_dual_is_partition_regular}
    \lean{joinOfFamilyAndDualIsPR}
    $\family \classcap \family^*$ is partition regular
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_family_classcapdual_dual_is_filter,thm_pr_iff_dual_is_filter,thm_de_morgan_v1_dual,thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams,thm_classcap_commutative}
    Let $\family$ be a family. Applying \cref{thm_family_classcapdual_dual_is_filter}, we see that $\family \classcapdual \family^*$ is a filter.  Applying \cref{thm_pr_iff_dual_is_filter}, we see that $(\family \classcapdual \family^*)^*$ is partition regular.  By \cref{thm_de_morgan_v1_dual}, we get that $\family^* \classcap (\family^*)^*$ is partition regular.  Applying \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}, we see that $\family^* \classcap \family$ is partition regular.  Applying \cref{thm_classcap_commutative}, we have that $\family \classcap \family^*$ is partition regular, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_classcap_idempotent_at_filters}
    \lean{familyIsIdempotentForJoinIffFilter}
    For all families $\family$, $\family \classcap \family = \family$ if and only if $\family$ is a filter.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_classcap_contains_union}
    \dgg{Updated!} In the following list, each statement is equivalent to the following one.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\family \classcap \family = \family$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] If $\family = \emptyset$, this holds.  Otherwise, it holds by \cref{thm_classcap_contains_union}.
        \end{itemize}
        \item $\family \classcap \family \subseteq \family$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}
        \item for all $A, B \in \family$, $A \cap B \in \family$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] families are upward closed, and definition of filter
        \end{itemize}
        \item $\family$ is a filter
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_classcap_full_condition}
    \lean{familyJoinFullCondition}
    \dgg{New!} For all families $\family, \familytwo$, we have that $\family \classcap \familytwo \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$ if and only if $\family \subseteq \familytwo^*$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \dgg{New!} Each line is equivalent to the one following it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\family \classcap \familytwo \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] $\family \classcap \familytwo$ is upward closed
        \end{itemize}
        \item $\emptyset \not\in \family \classcap \familytwo$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition of family join
        \end{itemize}
        \item for all $A \in \family$ and $B \in \familytwo$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$.
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition of dual
        \end{itemize}
        \item $\family \subseteq \familytwo^*$
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Family meet}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_family_classcapdual_is_family}
% Does this already have a name in lean?
    If $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are families, then $\family \classcapdual \familytwo$ is a family.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_family_classcap_is_family,thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam,thm_de_morgan_v1,thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
    Let $\family$ and $\familytwo$ be families.  Applying \cref{thm_family_classcap_is_family} to the families $\family^*$ and $\familytwo^*$, we get that $\family^* \classcap \familytwo^*$ is a family.  By \cref{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam}, $(\family^* \classcap \familytwo^*)^*$ is a family.  By \cref{thm_de_morgan_v1},
    \[(\family^* \classcap \familytwo^*)^* = (\family^*)^* \classcapdual (\familytwo^*)^*.\]
    By \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}, we have
    \[(\family^* \classcap \familytwo^*)^* = \family \classcapdual \familytwo.\]
    It follows that $\family \classcapdual \familytwo$ is a family.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_classcapdual_contained_in_intersection}
    \lean{familyMeetContainedInIntersection}
    \dgg{Updated!} For all families $\family$ and $\familytwo$, if $\familytwo \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$, then $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \subseteq \family$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_classcap_contains_union,thm_dual_is_antitone,thm_de_morgan_union_v1,thm_de_morgan_v1,thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
    \dgg{Updated!} Let $\family$ and $\familytwo$ be families.  Applying \cref{thm_classcap_contains_union} to $\family^*$ and $\familytwo^*$, we see that $\family^* \subseteq \family^* \classcap \familytwo^*$. Taking the dual and applying \cref{thm_dual_is_antitone}, we have that $(\family^*)^* \supseteq (\family^* \classcap \familytwo^*)^*$.  Applying \cref{thm_de_morgan_union_v1} and \cref{thm_de_morgan_v1}, we have that $(\family^*)^* \supseteq (\family^*)^* \classcapdual (\familytwo^*)^*$.  By \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}, $\family \supseteq \family \classcapdual \familytwo$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_classcapdual_commutative}
    \lean{familyMeetIsCommutative}
    $\classcapdual$ is commutative
\end{theorem}

\dgg{Careful: the proof below works only for families -- not more generally for collections -- because it relies on \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}.}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_classcap_commutative,thm_de_morgan_v1,thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
    Let $\family$ and $\familytwo$ be families.  Applying \cref{thm_classcap_commutative} to the families $\family^*$ and $\familytwo^*$, we get that
    \[\family^* \classcap \familytwo^* = \familytwo^* \classcap \family^*.\]
    Applying the dual and using \cref{thm_de_morgan_v1}, we see
    \[(\family^*)^* \classcapdual (\familytwo^*)^* = (\familytwo^*)^* \classcapdual (\family^*)^*.\]
    By \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}, we have
    \[\family \classcapdual \familytwo = \familytwo \classcapdual \family.\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_classcapdual_associative}
    \lean{familyMeetIsAssociative}
    $\classcapdual$ is associative
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    (Don't prove from scratch.  If possible, refer to dual statement.)

    \label{thm_classcapdual_monotone}
    \label{thm_classcapdual_is_monotonic}
    \lean{familyMeetIsMonotonic}
    $\classcapdual$ is monotone
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    (Don't prove from scratch.  If possible, refer to dual statement.)

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_family_classcapdual_dual_is_filter}
    \lean{meetOfFamilyAndDualIsFilter}
    $\family \classcapdual \family^*$ is a filter
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_family_classcapdual_is_family}
    % Let $\family$ be a family.  If $\family = \mathcal{P}(S)$ or $\family^* = \mathcal{P}(S)$, then $\family \classcapdual \family^* = \emptyset$, which is a filter.
    
    % Assume $\family \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$ and $\family^* \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$.
    \dgg{Updated!} By \cref{thm_family_classcapdual_is_family}, $\family \classcapdual \family^*$ is a family.  We need only to show that if $A, B \in \family \classcapdual \family^*$, then $A \cap B \in \family \classcapdual \family^*$.

    Let $A, B \in \family \classcapdual \family^*$.  To see that $A \cap B \in \family \classcapdual \family^*$, by definition, we must show that for every $C \in \family^*$, the set $A \cap B \cap C$ belongs to $\family^*$.

    Let $C \in \family^*$.  Since $A \in \family \classcapdual \family^*$, $A \cap C \in \family^*$.  Since $B \in \family \classcapdual \family^*$, the set $A \cap B \cap C \in \family^*$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_classcapdual_idempotent_at_pr_families}
    \lean{familyIsIdempotentForMeetIffPR}
    \leanok{}
    For all families $\family$, $\family \classcapdual \family = \family$ if and only if $\family$ is partition regular.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_classcap_idempotent_at_filters,thm_pr_iff_dual_is_filter,thm_de_morgan_v1,thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
    Let $\family$ be a family.  By \cref{thm_classcap_idempotent_at_filters} applied to $\family^*$, we have that $\family^* \classcap \family^* = \family^*$ if and only if $\family^*$ is a filter.  By \cref{thm_pr_iff_dual_is_filter}, we have that $(\family^* \classcap \family^*)^* = (\family^*)^*$ if and only if $(\family^*)^*$ is partition regular.  By \cref{thm_de_morgan_v1}, we have that $(\family^*)^* \classcapdual (\family^*)^* = (\family^*)^*$ if and only if $(\family^*)^*$ is partition regular.  By \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}, we have that $\family \classcapdual \family = \family$ if and only if $\family$ is partition regular, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_classcapdual_empty_condition}

    \dgg{New!} For all families $\family, \familytwo$, we have that $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \neq \emptyset$ if and only if $\family^* \subseteq \familytwo$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_classcap_full_condition}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_de_morgan_v1}
    \lean{familyDeMorgan1}
    For all collections $\family$, $\familytwo$, $(\family \classcap \familytwo)^* = \family^* \classcapdual \familytwo^*$
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    % We consider three cases.
    
    % Case 1: $\family = \emptyset$. In this case,
    % \begin{itemize}
    %     \item $\family \classcap \familytwo = \familytwo$, by definition

    %     \item $\family^* \classcapdual \familytwo^* = \mathcal{P}(S) \classcapdual \familytwo^* = \familytwo^*$, by definition
    % \end{itemize}
    % Therefore,
    % \[(\family \classcap \familytwo)^* = \familytwo^* = \family^* \classcapdual \familytwo^*,\]
    % as desired.

    % Case 2: $\familytwo = \emptyset$.  In this case, we follow the analogous argument.  (Alternatively, we could use the fact that $\classcap$ and $\classcapdual$ are commutative.)
    
    % Case 3: $\family \neq \emptyset$ and $\familytwo \neq \emptyset$.
    
    \dgg{Updated!} In the following list, each statement is equivalent to the one that follows it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $A \in (\family \classcap \familytwo)^*$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}
        \item for all $B \in \family \classcap \familytwo$, $A \cap B \neq \emptyset$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}
        \item for all $C \in \family$ and $D \in \familytwo$, $A \cap C \cap D \neq \emptyset$
        \begin{itemize}
            \item[--] definition
        \end{itemize}
        \item for all $C \in \family$, $A \cap C \in \familytwo^*$
        \begin{itemize}

        \end{itemize}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_de_morgan_v1_dual}
    \lean{familyDeMorgan2}
    For all families / collections (see note immediately following), $(\family \classcapdual \familytwo)^* = \family^* \classcap \familytwo^*$
\end{theorem}

\dgg{I believe this is true on the level of collections.  But to prove it by using \cref{thm_de_morgan_v1} and the dual, we'd only get the statement for families (since the dual is not an involution on the level of collections...}

\begin{proof}[Families proof]
\uses{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam,thm_de_morgan_v1,thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}
    Let $\family$ and $\familytwo$ be families.  By \cref{thm_dual_of_fam_is_fam}, $\family^*$ and $\familytwo^*$ are families.  By \cref{thm_de_morgan_v1} with the families $\family^*$ and $\familytwo^*$, we have that
    \[(\family^* \classcap \familytwo^*)^* = (\family^*)^* \classcapdual (\familytwo^*)^*.\]
    By \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams}, this is
    \[\family \classcapdual \familytwo = (\family^* \classcap \familytwo^*)^*.\]
    Taking duals and using \cref{thm_dual_is_involution_on_fams} again, we see
    \[(\family \classcapdual \familytwo)^* = \family^* \classcap \familytwo^*,\]
    as desired.
\end{proof}
