\dgg{This is a pool of potential results.  These can be safely ignored for now.}

Dual families
\begin{enumerate}
    \item For all families $\family$, $\family^* = \family$ if and only if $\family$ is an ultrafilter
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof: \cref{thm_family_equal_to_dual_iff_ultrafilter}
    \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

Family join
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Subsets of $\families{S}$ invariant under $\classcap$:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Filters
        \item Partition regular families (grills)
        \item non-empty families
        \item (and, by extension, all intersections of combinations of these)
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item Note that $\classcap$ is not idempotent.  Indeed, if $\family$ contains two sets that are disjoint, then $\family \classcap \family = \mathcal{P}(S)$.  This shows that $(\families{S},\subseteq,\classcap,\classcapdual)$ is not a lattice.
    
    \item $\family \classcap \familytwo \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$ iff $\family \subseteq \familytwo^*$  \dgg{seems to fail when $\family = \emptyset$ and $\familytwo = \mathcal{P}(S)$.  Perhaps this is the only exception to the statement.}
        
    \item On filters and partition regular families
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item If $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are filters, $\family \classcap \familytwo$ is the smallest filter containing both $\family$ and $\familytwo$.

        \item $\classcap$ is not an interesting operation on partition regular families.  If either $\family$ or $\familytwo$ is equal to $\emptyset$, then $\family \classcap \familytwo \in \{\family, \familytwo\}$.  Otherwise, if $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are non-empty and partition regular and $\family \classcap \familytwo \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$, then $\family = \familytwo$ is an ultrafilter and $\family \classcap \familytwo = \family$.

        \item The previous implies that if $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are partition regular, then $\family \classcap \familytwo$ is partition regular, but for a silly reason: it is equal to $\family$, $\familytwo$, $\emptyset$, or $\mathcal{P}(S)$.

        \item (For a proof, see the family meet section where the dual statement is proven.)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}

Family meet
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Subsets of $\families{S}$ invariant under $\classcapdual$:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Filters
        \item Partition regular families (grills)
        \item families not equal to $\mathcal{P}(S)$
        \item (and, by extension, all intersections of combinations of these)
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \neq \emptyset$ iff $\family^* \subseteq \familytwo$ \dgg{seems to fail when $\family = \emptyset$ and $\familytwo = \mathcal{P}(S)$. Perhaps this is the only exception to the statement.}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Proof:
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item Edge cases
    \begin{itemize}
        % \item $\family \classcapdual \{S\}^* = \family$
        \item $\family \classcapdual \mathcal{P}(S) = \family$ \dgg{this does not match the definition above, but it is dual to our choice of $\family \classcap \emptyset = \family$.}
        \item $\family \classcapdual \family = \family$ if and only if $\family$ is a filter
        % \item (?) More generally, characterize when $\family \classcapdual \familytwo = \family \cap \familytwo$
    \end{itemize}
    \item On filters and partition regular families
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item If $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are partition regular, $\family \classcapdual \familytwo$ is the largest partition regular family contained in both $\family$ and $\familytwo$.
        
        \item $\classcapdual$ is not an interesting operation on filters.  If either $\family$ or $\familytwo$ is equal to $\mathcal{P}(S)$, then $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \in \{\family, \familytwo\}$.  Otherwise, if $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are filters not equal to $\mathcal{P}(S)$ and $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \neq \emptyset$, then $\family = \familytwo$ is an ultrafilter and $\family \classcapdual \familytwo = \family$.
        
        \item The previous implies that if $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are filters, then $\family \classcapdual \familytwo$ is a filter, but for a silly reason: it is equal to $\family$, $\familytwo$, $\emptyset$, or $\mathcal{P}(S)$.
        
        \item Proof of this fact: If either $\family$ or $\familytwo$ is equal to $\mathcal{P}(S)$, then $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \in \{\family, \familytwo\}$.  This follows by the work above.  Suppose $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are filters not equal to $\mathcal{P}(S)$ and that $\family \classcapdual \familytwo \neq \emptyset$. Then $\family^* \subseteq \familytwo$ and $\familytwo^* \subseteq \family$.  But because they are filters not equal to $\mathcal{P}(S)$, we see
        \[\family \subseteq \family^* \subseteq \familytwo \subseteq \familytwo^* \subseteq \family,\]
        which shows that $\family = \familytwo$ and that they are self-dual, hence ultrafilters.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}

Algebra involving both
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \dgg{This is a consequence of other results, so we should only state it helpful.} The following are equivalent: \dgg{careful with the edge cases here.  consider defining $\emptyset$ to be a filter but $\mathcal{P}(S)$ not to be?  Otherwise, need clarification for edge cases.}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\family$ is partition regular
        \item $\family^*$ is a filter
        \item $\family \classcap \family^* = \family$
        \item $\family \classcapdual \family^* = \family^*$
    \end{enumerate}
    If any one, all, of these hold, then $\family^* \subseteq \family$.
\end{enumerate}

Semigroup algebra operations
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\family + \familytwo$
    \item $A - \family$
\end{enumerate}
\vskip0.5cm


The following list of facts were useful for us in a previous paper.  It would be great to 1) extend the definitions and notation to arbitrary semigroups, 2) formally verify these facts.  I can't honestly say, however, that we would have a great application for any of these facts in this paper.

Let $n \in \N$, $A, B \subseteq \N$, and $\family, \familytwo, \family_1, \family_2, \familytwo_1, \familytwo_2$ be families.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item
    \label{item_family_prop_zero}
    If $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are proper, then $\family + \familytwo$ is proper.
    
    \item
    \label{item_family_prop_one_new}
    $(A - \family) \cap (B - \familytwo) \subseteq (A \cap B) - (\family \classcap \familytwo)$, with equality if $\family = \familytwo$ is a filter.

    \item \label{item_family_prop_two}
    If $\family$ and $\familytwo$ are filters, then $\family + \familytwo$ is a filter.

    \item \label{item_family_prop_three}
    $(A -n) - \family = (A - \family) - n$.

    \item \label{item_family_prop_four}
    $A - (\family + \familytwo) = (A - \familytwo) - \family$.

    \item \label{item_family_diff_containment}
    If $\family \subseteq \familytwo$, then $A - \family \subseteq A - \familytwo$.
    
    \item \label{item_filter_sums_containment}
    If $\family_1 \subseteq \familytwo_1$ and $\family_2 \subseteq \familytwo_2$, then $\family_1 + \family_2 \subseteq \familytwo_1 + \familytwo_2$.

    \item \label{item_sum_dual}
    $(\family + \familytwo)^* = \family^* + \familytwo^*$.

    \item \label{item_cap_sums}
    $(\family_1 + \family_2) \classcap (\familytwo_1 + \familytwo_2) \subseteq (\family_1 \classcap \familytwo_1) + (\family_2 \classcap \familytwo_2)$.
\end{enumerate}



\section{Topological dynamical systems}
\label{sec_topological_dynamical_systems}

Needs: \cref{sec_topology,sec_semigroups}

Contains:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Definitions and results concerning topological dynamical systems, the regionally proximal relation, equicontinuity, etc...
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Definitions}

\begin{leanrmk}
    The topological dynamical systems in Mathlib are called Flows.  See \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Dynamics/Flow.html}.  A flow is an action of a topological monoid on a topological space by continuous maps.  Since we care only about discrete semigroups (monoids), we are likely better off defining our own class of topological dynamical systems.  We may still find it useful to use the notion of invariance, here: \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Dynamics/Flow.html}
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{leanrmk}
    Question: Do we define topological dynamical systems as semigroup actions or monoid actions?  The only difference is that a monoid action requires that the identity element of the monoid acts by the identity map on the space.  This is a natural assumption, but may not be required for us.\\
    
    Answer: semigroup actions.  It seems we can do everything we need to do while avoiding monoids, and especially monoid actions.\\

    Question: Do we define topological dynamical systems with phase space ``compact Hausdorff'' or ``uniform''?  Uniform is more general, but perhaps is not necessary for our purposes.\\
    
    Answer: compact Hausdorff phase spaces\\

    Question: A ``system'' for us is usually a bundle of a phase space $X$ together with an action of a semigroup $S$.  But in our applications, it may be more efficient to un-bundle these by focusing on the action as the object.  So, instead, we would say: $T$ is an action of $S$ on $X$.  Then $T$ is minimal; a subset of $X$ is $T$-invariant; if $R$ is an action of $S$ on $Y$, then a surjection $\pi: X \to Y$ is a factor map if $S \circ \pi = \pi \circ T$; etc...\\
    
    Answer: since the data provided is essentially the same, I don't right now see a meaningful difference here.  Let's go with the more recognizable ``system'' as a bundling of space and action.
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{definition}[Semigroup action]
\lean{SemigroupAction}
\leanok{}
Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be a set.  An action of $S$ on $X$ is a map $f: S \to X \to X$ such that for all $s, t \in S$,
\[(f s) \circ (f t) = f (s*t).\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Topological dynamical system]
\lean{DynamicalSystem}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a non-empty semigroup and $X$ be a non-empty, compact Hausdorff space. An \emph{$S$-system} is an action of $S$ on $X$ such that for all $s \in S$, $f s$ is continuous.
\end{definition}

Notation: for $x \in X$, $U \subseteq X$, $g \in G$, and $H \subseteq G$, we write
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $gx$ to denote the image of $x$ under $g$;
    
    \item $Gx$ to denote the set $\{gx \mid g \in G\}$;

    \item $gU$ to denote the set $\{g x \mid x \in U\}$;

    \item $GU$ to denote the set $\{gx \mid g \in G, \ x \in U\}$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{definition}[Orbit]
\lean{orbit}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system and $x \in X$.  The $S$-orbit of $x$ is $Sx$.  The $S$-orbit closure of $x$ is $\overline{Sx}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Orbit closure]
\lean{orbitClosure}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system and $x \in X$.  The $S$-orbit closure of $x$ is $\overline{Sx}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Diagonal action]
\lean{diagSemigroupAction}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a $S$-system.  The \emph{diagonal action} of $S$ on $X^2$ is $s(x,x) = (sx,sx)$.  This makes $X^2$ into a $S$-system.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Diagonal system]
\lean{diagDynamicalSystem}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a $S$-system.  The \emph{diagonal action} of $S$ on $X^2$ is $s(x,x) = (sx,sx)$ makes $X^2$ into a $S$-system.
\end{definition}

%\dgg{Get this from mathlib already? see \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Dynamics/Flow.html}}
\begin{definition}[Invariant set]
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a $S$-system.  A set $Y \subseteq X$ is $S$-invariant if for all $g \in S$, $gY \subseteq Y$.
\end{definition}

\begin{leanrmk}
    Pay attention to how subsystems are defined in lean.  The primary need for subsystem is \cref{thm_every_system_has_minimal_subsystem}.  For precedent, see Submodule: \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/Algebra/Module/Submodule/Defs.html#Submodule}
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{definition}[Subsystem]
    \label{def_subsystem}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  Let $Z \subseteq X$ be a non-empty, closed, $S$-invariant set.  Then $Z$ is an $S$-system called an \emph{$S$-subsystem} of $X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Subsemigroup system]
\label{def_subsemi_system}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup.  Let $X$ be an $S$-system. Let $T \subseteq S$ be a subsemigroup. Then $X$ is a $T$-system by restricting the action of $S$ to $T$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[System via homomorphism]
\label{def_hom_system}
\lean{homDynamicalSystem}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup.  Let $X$ be an $S$-system.  Let $\varphi: T \to S$ be a semigroup homomorphism.  Then $X$ is a $T$-system via the homomorphism $\varphi$ as follows: $tx = (\varphi t)x$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Orbit closure transitivity]
\label{theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity}
\lean{orbitTransitivity}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system and $x,y,z \in X$. If $y \in \overline{Sx}$ and $z \in \overline{Sy}$, then $z \in \overline{Sx}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{leanrmk}
    Here we will use \verb|mem_closure_iff| repeatedly.
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Let $U \ni z$.  Since $z \in \overline{Sy}$, $U \cap Sy \neq \emptyset$, so there exists $s \in S$ such that $y \in s^{-1}U$.  Since $y \in \overline{Sx}$ and $s^{-1}U$ is open, $s^{-1} U \cap Sx \neq \emptyset$.  Thus, there exists $t \in S$ such that $tx \in s^{-1} U$.  Therefore, $stx \in U$, and so $U \cap Sx \neq \emptyset$.  Since $U$ was arbitrary, we get that $z \in \overline{Sx}$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_closure_of_inv_is_inv}
\lean{closureOfInvIsInv}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be an $S$-system, and $A \subseteq X$.  If $A$ is $S$-invariant, then $\overline{A}$ is $S$-invariant.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image}
\leanok{}
    Let $s \in S$.  We must show that $s \overline{A} \subseteq \overline{A}$.  Since $s A \subseteq A$, we have by \cref{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image} that $s \overline{A} = \overline{sA} \subseteq \overline{A}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_orb_closure_is_invariant}
    \lean{orbClosIsInv}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be an $S$-system, and $x \in X$.  The $S$-orbit closure of $x$, $\overline{Sx}$, is an $S$-invariant subset of $X$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_closure_of_inv_is_inv}
\leanok{}
    To see that $\overline{Sx}$ is $S$-invariant, it suffices by \cref{lemma_closure_of_inv_is_inv} to show that $Sx$ is $S$-invariant.  But this is immediate: $sSx = (sS)x \subseteq Sx$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_orb_closure_is_subsystem}
    \lean{orbitClosureIsSubDynamicalSystem}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be an $S$-system, and $x \in X$.  The $S$-orbit closure of $x$, $\overline{Sx}$, is an $S$-subsystem of $X$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_orb_closure_is_invariant}
    \leanok{}
    The set $\overline{Sx}$ is closed and hence compact by \verb|IsClosed.isCompact|.  Given the subspace topology, it is Hausdorff (note \verb|instT2SpaceSubtype|). By \cref{lemma_orb_closure_is_invariant}, the set $\overline{Sx}$ is $S$-invariant.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_intersection_of_invariant}
    \lean{InterOfInvIsInv}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be a $S$-system.  If $Y_i$, $i \in I$, is a collection of $S$-invariant subsets of $X$, then $\cap_i Y_i$ is $S$-invariant.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Let $x \in \cap_i Y_i$, $g \in S$, and $j \in I$.  Since $x \in Y_j$ and $Y_j$ is invariant, $gx \in Y_j$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Factor maps and ICERS}

\begin{definition}[Factor]
\lean{isFactor}
    \leanok{}
Let $X$ be a $G$-system.  A \emph{factor of $X$} is a $G$-system $Y$ together with a continuous surjection $X \to Y$ that intertwines the action of $G$.
\end{definition}

\begin{leanrmk}
    For precedent for ICERs in Lean, see \verb|AddCon| in \url{https://leanprover-community.github.io/mathlib4_docs/Mathlib/GroupTheory/Congruence/Defs.html}
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{definition}[ICER]
\lean{ICER}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a $G$-system.  A set $R \subseteq X^2$ is an \emph{ICER (on $X$)} if it is an equivalence relation, closed as a subset of $X^2$, and invariant under $G$ by the diagonal action.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_intersection_of_icers}
\lean{intersectionOfICERsIsICER}
\leanok{}
    Let $(X,T)$ be a system.  The intersection of any collection of ICERs is an ICER.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Invariance and closedness are immediate. The intersection of equivalence relations is an equivalence relation.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Quotient system]
\label{thm_quotient_system}
\lean{quotientDynamicalSystem}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a $S$-system, and let $R$ be an ICER on $X$.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The quotient space $X/R$ is a compact Hausdorff space;
        \item $(s,[x]_R) \mapsto [sx]_R$ is an action of $S$ by continuous maps;
        \item the space $X/R$ together with this $S$-action is a $S$-system.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    \dgg{This is straightforward and hopefully most of it is quotable from mathlib.  If this theorem exists for ``flows'' in Mathlib, we can just adapt the proof for our tds.}
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{theorem_quotient_map_is_factor_map}
\lean{quotientMapIsFactorMap}
\leanok{}
    The quotient map $\pi: X \to X/R$ is a factor map of $S$-systems.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \dgg{Clear by the definition of the system.}
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Quotient system]
\label{def_quotient_system}
    Let $X$ be a $G$-system and $R$ be an ICER on $X$.  The $G$-system $X/R$ (according to \cref{thm_quotient_system}) is the \emph{quotient system}.
\end{definition}

The following lemma describes the bijection between factors of a given system and ICERs. \dgg{I'm not sure we'll need to prove this formally yet, so wait to develop it or prove it.}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_img_of_inv_is_inv}
    \lean{imageOfSubsystemIsSubsystem}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, and let $\pi: X \to Y$ be an $S$-equivariant continuous map (eg. a factor map satisfies this).  If $A \subseteq X$ is an $S$-subsystem of $X$, then $\pi A$ is an $S$-subsystem of $Y$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Clearly, $\pi A$ is non-empty.  It is closed since $\pi$ is a closed map.  (By \verb|Continuous.isProperMap|, $f$ is proper. Therefore, by \verb|IsProperMap.isClosedMap|, $f$ is closed.)  To see $S$-invariance, let $s \in S$.  Then $s \pi A = \pi sA \subseteq \pi A$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Isomorphism of systems]
    \label{def_isomorphism}
    \lean{isIsomorphism}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ and $Y$ be $S$-systems.  We say that $X$ and $Y$ are \emph{isomorphic} if there exists a homeomorphism $\varphi: X \to Y$ that intertwines the actions of $S$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_system_is_isomorphic_to_injective_factor_image}
    \lean{systemIsomToInjectiveImage}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ and $Y$ be $S$-systems.  Suppose $\pi: X \to Y$ is a continuous injection that intertwines the actions of $S$.  The $S$-system $X$ is isomorphic to the $S$-system $\pi X$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_img_of_inv_is_inv}
    Note that $\pi X$ is an $S$-system by \cref{lemma_img_of_inv_is_inv}.  It is a general fact that if $f: A \to B$ is a continuous injection of compact Hausdorff spaces, then $f: A \to fA$ is a homeomorphism.  Therefore, we see that $\pi: X \to \pi X$ is a homeomorphism that intertwines the actions of $S$, hence an isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Unused from this subsection}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_factors_and_icers}
    Let $(X,T)$ be a system.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item For every factor $\pi: (X,T) \to (Y,T)$, the set $R_\pi \defeq \{(x,y) \in X^2 : \pi x = \pi y\}$ is an ICER.
        \item For every ICER $R \subseteq X^2$, the set $X / R$ is a compact metric space and the quotient map $\pi_R: (X,T) \to (Y,T)$ is a factor map of systems.
    \end{enumerate}
    Moreover, the map $\pi \mapsto R_\pi$ is an order-reversing bijection from the set of factors of $(X,T)$ (up to isomorphism, ordered by the ordering) and the set of ICERs of $X$ (ordered by inclusion) with inverse $R \mapsto \pi_R$.
\end{lemma}


\subsection{Visit time sets}

\begin{definition}[Point visit time sets]
\label{def_point_visit_time_set}
\lean{visitTimeSet}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be an $S$-system, $x \in X$, and $U, V \subseteq X$.  The \emph{visit time set of $x$ to $U$} is defined as
    \begin{align*}
        R(x,U) &\defeq \{s \in S \mid sx \in U \}.
    \end{align*}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Set visit time sets]
\label{def_set_visit_time_set}
\lean{setVisitTimeSet}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be an $S$-system, $x \in X$, and $U, V \subseteq X$.  The \emph{visit time set of $V$ to $U$} is defined as
    \begin{align*}
        R(V,U) &\defeq \{s \in S \mid sV \cap U \neq \emptyset\}.
    \end{align*}
\end{definition}

\begin{leanrmk}
    Note that $R(x,U)$ is the preimage of $U$ under the map $S \to X$ gotten by fixing $x$.  There is likely a slick way to write this in lean.  Then $R(V,U) = \cup_{x \in V} R(x,U)$.  Most if not all of the algebra involving return-time sets will come down to simple set algebra involving preimages.
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{lemma}[Visits to preimages]
\label{lemma_return_time_algebra_one}
\lean{visitsToPreimages}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be a $S$-system, $x \in X$, $U \subseteq X$, and $s \in S$. We have
    \[s^{-1}R(x,U) = R(x,s^{-1}U).\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Each line is equivalent to the one that follows it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $h \in R(x,g^{-1}U)$
        \item $hx \in g^{-1}U$
        \item $ghx \in U$
        \item $gh \in R(x,U)$
        \item $h \in g^{-1} R(x,U)$
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

% \begin{lemma}[Visit time algebra 2]
% \label{lemma_return_time_algebra_two}
%     Suppose $G$ is a semigroup.  Let $X$ be a $G$-system, $x \in X$, $U \subseteq X$, and $g \in G$.
%     \[R(gx,U) = R(x,U)g^{-1}.\]
% \end{lemma}

% \begin{proof}
%     Each line is equivalent to the one that follows it.
%     \begin{enumerate}
%         \item $h \in R(gx, U)$
%         \item $hgx \in U$
%         \item $hg\in R(x,U)$
%         \item $h \in R(x,U)g^{-1}$
%     \end{enumerate}
% \end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Set visits as quotient of point visits]
\label{lemma_return_time_algebra_three}
\lean{setVisitsAsQuotientSet}
\leanok{}
    Suppose $S$ is a semigroup, $X$ be a minimal $S$-system, $U, V \subseteq X$ open, and $x \in X$.
    \[R(x,V)R(x,U)^{-1} = R(U,V).\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Each line is equivalent to the one that follows it.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $g \in R(U,V)$
        \item $gU \cap V$ is non-empty
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item there exists $u \in U$ such that $gu \in V$
            \item there exists $u \in U$ such that $u \in g^{-1} V$
        \end{enumerate}
        \item $U \cap g^{-1} V$ is non-empty and open
        \item there exists $h \in G$ such that $h \in R(x, U \cap g^{-1}V)$
        \item there exists $h \in G$ such that $h \in R(x,U) \cap R(x,g^{-1}V)$
        \item there exists $h \in G$ such that $h \in R(x,U) \cap g^{-1} R(x,V)$
        \item there exists $h \in R(x,U)$ such that $h \in g^{-1} R(x,V)$
        \item there exists $h \in R(x,U)$ such that $gh \in R(x,V)$
        \item there exists $h \in R(x,U)$ such that $g \in R(x,V) h^{-1}$
        \item $g \in R(x,V) R(x,U)^{-1}$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_return_to_intersection}
    \lean{visitToInter}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  For all $x \in X$ and $U_1, \ldots, U_k \subseteq X$,
    \[R\left(x, \bigcap_{i=1}^k U_i \right) = \bigcap_{i=1}^k R(x,U_i).\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Standard: re-write as the set theoretic statement, ``preimage splits over intersections''
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_return_to_union}
    \lean{visitToUnion}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  For all $x \in X$ and $U_1, \ldots, U_k \subseteq X$,
    \[R\left(x, \bigcup_{i=1}^k U_i \right) = \bigcup_{i=1}^k R(x,U_i).\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Standard: re-write as the set theoretic statement, ``preimage splits over unions''
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Monotonicty of return-time sets]
\label{lemma_monotonicity_of_rts}
\lean{visitTimesMono}
\leanok{}
Let $X$ be an $S$-system, $x \in X$.  For all $U_1 \subseteq U_2 \subseteq X$,
\[R(x,U_1) \subseteq R(x,U_2).\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Immediate from the definitions.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Visit times for product actions]
\label{lemma_return_time_sets_in_prod_system}
\lean{visitsToProductsUnderDiagonal}
\leanok{}
Let $S$ be a semigroup, and let $X$ and $Y$ be $S$-systems.  For all $x \in X$, $y \in Y$, $U \subseteq X$, and $V \subseteq Y$, considering the product system,
\[R((x,y), U \times V) = R(x, U) \cap R(y,V).\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Immediate from the definitions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_visit_time_thru_factor}
\lean{visitTimesThruFactorMap}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, and let $\pi: X \to Y$ be a factor map of $S$-systems.  For all $x \in X$ and $V \subseteq Y$,
    \[R(x,\pi^{-1} U) = R(\pi x, V).\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    By definition, $s \in R(x,\pi^{-1} U)$ if and only if $sx \in \pi^{-1} U$, which holds iff $\pi s x \in U$.  But this is iff $s \pi x \in U$, that is iff $s \in R(\pi x, U)$.
\end{proof}

%     \item $R(sx, U)s \subseteq R(x,U)$.
%     \item $sR(x,U) \subseteq R(x,sU)$.
%     \item $sR(U,V) \subseteq R(U,sV)$.

\subsection{Minimality}

\begin{definition}[Minimal system]
    \label{def_minimal_system}
    \lean{isMinimal}
    \leanok{}
    An $S$-system $X$ is \emph{minimal} if the only non-empty, closed, $S$-invariant subset of $X$ is $X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Every system has a minimal subsystem]
\label{thm_every_system_has_minimal_subsystem}
\lean{existsMinimalSubset}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  There exists a non-empty, closed, $S$-invariant subset $Z \subseteq X$ (that is, an $S$-subsystem $Z$ of $X$) such that the $S$-system $Z$ is minimal.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_intersection_of_invariant}
\leanok{}
    The subsystems of $X$ are ordered by containment.  Given any collection $Z_i$, $i \in I$, of $S$-subsystems of $X$, $\cap_i Z_i$ is closed, non-empty (by compactness), and $S$-invariant (by \cref{lemma_intersection_of_invariant}). Thus, $\cap_i Z_i$ is an $S$-subsystem of $X$.  This shows that every totally ordered subset of the set of subsystems of $X$ has a lower bound in $X$.  By Zorn's lemma, the set of subsystems has a minimal element.  This minimal element is a subsystem of $X$ that is minimal.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Minimal iff dense orbits]
\label{thm_minimal_iff_dense_orbits}
\lean{minimalIffDenseOrbits}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  The system $X$ is minimal if and only if for all $x \in X$, $\overline{Sx} = X$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_orb_closure_is_invariant}
\leanok{}
    By \cref{lemma_orb_closure_is_invariant}, $\overline{Sx}$ is an $S$-system.  If $X$ is minimal, then $\overline{Sx} = X$, so $x$ has a dense orbit.

    On the other hand, if $Z$ is a closed, non-empty, $S$-invariant set, then choosing $x \in Z$, we see by the dense-orbit assumption that
    \[X = \overline{Sx} \subseteq \overline{SZ} \subseteq Z \subseteq X.\]
    It follows that $Z = X$, and hence that $X$ is a minimal $S$-system.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Images of minimal systems are minimal]
\label{lemma_img_of_min_is_min}
\lean{factorOfMinimalIsMinimal}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $\pi: X \to Y$ be a factor map of $S$-systems. If $X$ is minimal, then $Y$ is minimal.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Let $Z \subseteq Y$ be a non-empty, closed, $S$-invariant subset of $Y$.  Then $\pi^{-1} Z$ is a non-empty, closed, $S$-invariant subset of $X$.  By the minimality of $X$, $\pi^{-1} Z = X$.  Since $\pi$ is surjective, $Z = Y$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Minimal actions act surjectively]
\label{lemma_minimal_comm_action_implies_surjective}
\lean{minimalCommActionIsSurjective}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup.  Let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system.  For all $s \in S$, the map $s: X \to X$ is surjective.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Let $s \in S$.  Since $S$ is commutative, the set $sX$ is a closed, $S$-invariant subset of $X$.  By minimality, $sX = X$, showing that $s$ is surjective, as desired.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Uniform recurrence}

\begin{definition}[Uniformly recurrent point]
\label{def_uniformly_recurrent}
\lean{isUniformlyRecurrent}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be an $S$-system, and $x \in X$.  The point $x$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent if for all non-empty, open $U \ni x$, the set $R(x,U)$ is syndetic.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[All points in a minimal system are uniformly recurrent]
    \label{lemma_pts_in_minimal_are_ur}
    \lean{minimalImpliesUniformlyRecurrent}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be a minimal $S$-system. For all $x \in X$, $x$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_minimal_iff_dense_orbits,lemma_return_to_union,lemma_return_time_algebra_one}
\leanok{}
    Let $x \in U \subseteq X$. By \cref{thm_minimal_iff_dense_orbits}, all orbits are dense, so for $y \in X$, there exists $s_y \in S$ such that $sy \in U$.  Therefore, $X = \cup_{y \in X} s_y^{-1}U$.  By compactness, there exists a finite set $F \subseteq X$ such that $X = \cup_{y \in F} s_y^{-1} U$.  By \cref{lemma_return_to_union} and \cref{lemma_return_time_algebra_one},
    \[S = R(x,X) = R(x, \bigcup_{y \in F} s_y^{-1} U ) = \bigcup_{y \in F} R(x, s_y^{-1} U) = \bigcup_{y \in F} s_y^{-1} R(x, U).\]
    By definition, the set $R(x,U)$ is syndetic.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_orb_closure_of_ur_is_minimal}
    \lean{orbitClosureOfURPointIsMinimalSubset}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  If $x \in X$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent, then $\overline{Sx}$ is a minimal $S$-system containing $x$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_orb_closure_is_invariant}
\leanok{}
    Note that $\overline{Sx}$ is an $S$-system by \cref{lemma_orb_closure_is_invariant}. For all $U \ni x$, $R(x,U)$ is syndetic, hence non-empty.  Therefore, $x \in \overline{Sx}$.
    
    Let $y \in \overline{Sx}$ and $U \subseteq \overline{Sx}$ be open and non-empty.  We must show that there exists $s \in S$ such that $sy \in U$.

    Let $V \subseteq U$ be non-empty, open, and $\gamma \subseteq X^2$ be an entourage such that if $(a,b) \in \gamma$ and $a \in V$, then $b \in U$.
    
    Since $V \subseteq \overline{Sx}$ is open, non-empty, there exists $t \in S$ such that $tx \in V$, that is, $x \in t^{-1} V$.  By assumption $R(x,t^{-1} V)$ is syndetic: there exists a finite set $F \subseteq S$ such that for all $s \in S$, there exists $f \in F$ such that $fs \in R(x,t^{-1}V)$.

    By the uniform continuity of the maps $tf: X \to X$, $f \in F$, there exists an entourage $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ such that for all $f \in F$, $tf \alpha \subseteq \gamma$.  Since $y \in \overline{Sx}$, there exists $s \in S$ such that $(sx,y) \in \alpha$.  By syndeticity, there exists $f \in F$ such that $fs \in R(x,t^{-1}V)$, that is, $tfs x \in V$.

    Since $(sx,y) \in \alpha$, we have that $(tfsx,tfy) = tf(sx,y) \in tf \alpha \subseteq \gamma$.  Since $tfsx \in V$, by our choice of $V$ and $\gamma$, we have that $tfy \in U$.  Therefore, $tf \in R(y,U)$, and hence $R(y,U)$ is non-empty, as was to be shown.
    %\anh{I have trouble in Lean with giving $Y$ a uniform space structure given the subspace topology. Lean keeps complaining about the incompatibility of two topologies.}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_in_orb_clos_of_ur_implies_ur}
    \lean{inOrbitClosOfURPointImpliesUR}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be an $S$-system, and $x, y \in X$. If $x$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent and $y \in \overline{Sx}$, then $y$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent. 
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_orb_closure_of_ur_is_minimal,lemma_pts_in_minimal_are_ur}
\leanok{}
    By \cref{lemma_orb_closure_of_ur_is_minimal}, $\overline{Sx}$ is a minimal $S$-system.  By \cref{lemma_pts_in_minimal_are_ur}, all points of $\overline{Sx}$ are $S$-uniformly recurrent. \dgg{Something subtle here that may cause issues in Lean: $y$ being $S$-uniformly recurrent is independent of the system.  That is: if $Z$ is a subsystem of $X$ and $y$ is $S$-uniform recurrent in $Z$, then it is uniformly recurrent in $X$...}
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_ur_lift}
    \lean{liftUniformRecurrentPoint}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, and let $\pi: X \to Y$ be a factor map of $S$-systems.  For all $S$-uniformly recurrent points $y \in Y$, there exists an $S$-uniformly recurrent point $x \in X$ such that $\pi x = y$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \leanok{}
    Let $y \in Y$ be $S$-uniformly recurrent. Let $Z$ be the $S$-orbit closure of $y$.  It is easy to check that $\pi^{-1} Z$ is a subsystem of $X$.  By Fact \ref{item_every_system_has_minimal}. above, there exists a minimal subsystem $W \subseteq \pi^{-1} Z$ of $\pi^{-1} Z$ and, hence, of $X$.  Since images under factor maps of minimal systems are minimal systems, we have that $\pi W \subseteq Z$ is a minimal subsystem of $Y$.  But $Z$ is minimal, so $\pi W = Z$.  Since $y \in Z$, there exists $x\in W$ such that $\pi x = y$.  Since $W$ is minimal and $x \in W$, we see by Fact \ref{item_minimal_implies_ur}. that $x$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_dense_S_minimal_points_in_X2}
    \lean{inMinCommSystemURPairsDense}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, and let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system.  The set of points $(x,y) \in X^2$ that are $S$-uniformly recurrent is dense in $X^2$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_every_system_has_minimal_subsystem,lemma_img_of_inv_is_inv,lemma_img_of_min_is_min,lemma_pts_in_minimal_are_ur}
\leanok{}
    Let $U \times V \subseteq X^2$ be a non-empty, open set. We will show that $U \times V$ contains an $S$-uniformly recurrent point. 
    
    By \cref{thm_every_system_has_minimal_subsystem}, there exists a closed, non-empty, $S$-invariant (under the diagonal action) set $Z \subseteq X^2$.  Note that $\pi_1: X^2 \to X$ is an $S$-system factor map.  By \cref{lemma_img_of_inv_is_inv}, $\pi_1 Z$ is a closed, non-empty, $S$-invariant subset of $X$.  Since $X$ is minimal, $\pi_1 Z = X$.  Therefore, there exists $(x,y) \in Z$ such that $x \in U$. 
    
    Since $X$ is a minimal $S$-system, there exists $s \in S$ such that $sy \in V$.  Note that the point $(x,sy) \in U \times V$.  We have only left to show that $(x,sy)$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent.
    
    
    Since $S$ is commutative, the map $X^2 \to X^2$ given by $(w,z) \mapsto (w,sz)$ commutes with the diagonal action of $S$.  By \cref{lemma_img_of_inv_is_inv}, the image of $Z$ under this map is an $S$-system.  By \cref{lemma_img_of_min_is_min}, the image of $Z$ under this map is a minimal $S$ system.  By \cref{lemma_pts_in_minimal_are_ur}, the point $(x,sy)$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_forward_backward_set_orb_clos_coincide}
    \lean{forwardBackwardSetOrbClosCoincideInBronsSys}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$ system. Suppose that the $S$-uniformly recurrent points are dense in $X^2$.  For all thick $A, B \subseteq S$ and all non-empty, open $W \subseteq X^2$,
    \[\overline{AW} = \overline{B^{-1} W}.\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_thick_right_shift_is_thick}
\leanok{}
    Let $W \subseteq X^2$ open and non-empty.  Let $(x,y) \in \overline{AW}$, and let $V \subseteq X^2$ be an open neighborhood of $(x,y)$.  Since $V \cap AW \neq \emptyset$, there exists $s \in A$ such that $V \cap sW \neq \emptyset$.  Therefore, $W \cap s^{-1}V \neq \emptyset$. By assumption, there exists an $S$-uniformly recurrent point $(a,b) \in W \cap s^{-1}V$. Since the set $Bs$ is thick (by \cref{lemma_thick_right_shift_is_thick}) and $(a,b)$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent, $(a,b) \in \overline{Bs(a,b)}$.  Therefore, there exists $t \in B$ such that $ts(a,b) \in W$.  We see $s(a,b) \in V \cap t^{-1} W$, whereby $V \cap B^{-1} W \neq \emptyset$.  Since $V$ was an arbitrary open neighborhood of $(x,y)$, we have that $(x,y) \in \overline{B^{-1} W}$.

    Conversely, let $(x,y) \in \overline{B^{-1}W}$, and let $V \subseteq X^2$ be an open neighborhood of $(x,y)$.  Since $V \cap B^{-1}W \neq \emptyset$, there exists $s \in B$ such that $V \cap s^{-1}W \neq \emptyset$. By our assumption, there exists an $S$-uniformly recurrent point $(a,b) \in V \cap s^{-1}W$. Since the set $As$ is thick (by \cref{lemma_thick_right_shift_is_thick}) and $(a,b)$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent, $(a,b) \in \overline{As(a,b)}$.  Therefore, there exists $t \in A$ such that $ts(a,b) \in V \cap tW$, whereby $tW \cap V \neq \emptyset$.  Therefore, $V \cap A W \neq \emptyset$.  Since $V$ was an arbitrary open neighborhood of $(x,y)$, we have that $(x,y) \in \overline{A W}$.
\end{proof}



\subsection{Proximality}

\begin{leanrmk}
    It seems that the words ``proximal'' and ``distal'' do not appear in mathlib...  We don't need distal, but we will need proximal.
\end{leanrmk}

\begin{definition}[{Proximal, cf. \cite[Ch. 4, (2.11)]{devries_book_1993}}]
\label{def_proximal}
\lean{proximal}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system and $x, y \in X$.  The point $x$ is \emph{proximal to $y$} if for all $\alpha$ in the uniform structure on $X$, there exists $s \in S$ such that $(sx,sy) \in \alpha$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
\label{theorem_proximal_and_minimal_implies_orbit_closure_contains_diagonal}
\lean{minSystemOrbitClosProxPairContainsDiag}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be a minimal $S$-system, and $x, y \in X$.  If $x$ and $y$ are $S$-proximal, then $\Delta(X)$ is contained in the $S$-orbit-closure of $(x,y)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity}
\leanok{}
    By the definition of proximal, for all entourages $\alpha \subseteq X^2$, there exists $s \in S$ such that $(sx,sy) \in \alpha$.  The net $\alpha \mapsto (s_\alpha x, s_\alpha y)$ has a cluster point.  Since the cluster point is in every entourage, it belongs to $\Delta(X)$.  Therefore, there exists $z \in X$ such that $(z,z)$ is in the $S$-orbit closure of $(x,y)$.  By minimality, every point $x$ is in the $S$-orbit closure of $z$, so $\Delta(X)$ is in the $S$-orbit closure of $(z,z)$.  By orbit closure transitivity (\cref{theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity}), we have that $\Delta(X)$ is contained in the $S$-orbit closure of $(x,y)$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof \# 2]
    By a lemma, for all entourages $\alpha \subseteq X^2$, $R((x,y),\alpha)$ is a thick subset of $S$.  Let $w \in X$, and let $V \ni w$.  Let $\alpha \subseteq X$ be an entourage with the property that $(V \times X) \cap \alpha \subseteq V \times V$. \dgg{Why does such an entourage exist?}

    Because $R(x,V)$ is syndetic and $R((x,y),\alpha)$ is thick, there is $s \in R((x,y), \alpha) \cap R(x,V)$.  By the property of $\alpha$, we have that $(sx,sy) \in V \times V$.  Since $V$ was arbitrary, we have that $(w,w)$ is in $\overline{S(x,y)}$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{lemma_prox_pair_under_factor_map_is_prox}
    \lean{imageOfProxByFactorIsProx}
    \leanok{}
    Let $\pi: X \to Y$ be a factor map of $S$-systems.  If $x, y \in X$ are proximal, then $\pi x, \pi y \in Y$ are proximal.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \leanok{}
    This is easy to check from the definitions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_prox_pair_visits_diag_thickly}
\lean{proxPairVisitsDiagAlongThickSet}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  If $x, y \in X$ are proximal, then for all open $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ containing the diagonal, the set $R((x,y), \alpha)$ is thick.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Let $x, y \in X$ be proximal and $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ be open and contain the diagonal.  Let $F \subseteq S$ be finite. Define
    \[\beta = \bigcap_{f \in F} f^{-1} \alpha,\]
    and note that $\beta$ is an open set containing the diagonal.

    By the definition of proximal, there exists $s \in S$ such that $(sx,sy) \in \beta$.  Therefore, we see that $Fs (x,y) \subseteq \alpha$, implying that $Fs \subseteq R((x,y),\alpha)$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Regional proximality}

\subsubsection{Basics of RP}

\begin{definition}[{Regionally proximal relation, cf. \cite{dai_xiao_2017,auslander_dai_2019}}]
\label{def_rp_relation}
\lean{RP}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  The \emph{regionally proximal relation} is defined as
    \[\RP = \bigcap_{\alpha} \overline{S^{-1} \alpha},\]
    where the intersection is over all $\alpha$ in the uniformity on $X$, and $S$ acts diagonally.  We say that point $x \in X$ is \emph{regionally proximal to} point $y \in X$ if $(x,y) \in \RP$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_frp_basics_symmetric}
    \lean{RPisSymmetric}
    \leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system.  The relation $\RP$ is symmetric.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Denote by $\Phi: X^2 \to X^2$ the map $\Phi(x,y) = (y,x)$. Let $(x,y) \in \RP$ and $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ be an entourage. By the definition of a uniform space, $\Phi \alpha$ is an entourage (sometimes called $\alpha^{-1}$). Since $(x,y) \in \RP$, $(x,y) \in \overline{S^{-1} \Phi \alpha}$. But $\overline{S^{-1} \Phi \alpha} = \Phi \overline{S^{-1} \alpha}$. Therefore, $(y,x) \in \overline{S^{-1} \alpha}$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_frp_basics_invariant}
    \lean{RPInCommSemiIsInvariant}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, and let $X$ be an $S$-system.  The relation $\RP$ is $S$-invariant.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image,lemma_commuting_map_algebra}
\leanok{}
    Let $\alpha$ be an entourage and let $s \in S$.  Note that $\beta \defeq s^{-1} \alpha$ is an entourage.  Note that for $t \in S$, $st^{-1} \beta \subseteq t^{-1} s \beta$. Then by \cref{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image} and \cref{lemma_commuting_map_algebra},
    \[s \RP = s \bigcap_{\gamma} \overline{S^{-1}\gamma} \subseteq s \overline{S^{-1} \beta} = \overline{sS^{-1} \beta} \subseteq \overline{S^{-1} s \beta} \subseteq \overline{S^{-1} \alpha}.\]
    Since $\alpha$ was arbitrary, we see $s \RP \subseteq \RP$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_frp_basics_closed}
\lean{RPisClosed}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system.  The relation $\RP$ is closed.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    An arbitrary intersection of closed sets is closed. 
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_frp_reflexive}
\lean{RPisReflexive}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system.  The relation $\RP$ is reflexive.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    This is immediate from the definition, since $S^{-1} \alpha \supseteq \Delta.$
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_frp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal}
\lean{inRPiffForwardUOrbitClosHitsDiag}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  For all $(x,y) \in X^2$, $(x,y) \in \RP$ if and only if for all open sets $U \subseteq X^2$ containing $(x,y)$,
    \begin{align}
    \label{eqn_in_rp_equiv_set_v2}
         \Delta \cap \overline{S U} \neq \emptyset.
    \end{align}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
($\Rightarrow$) Suppose $(x, y) \in \RP = \bigcap_{\alpha} \overline{S^{-1} \alpha}$. For contradiction, assume there exists an open set $U$ containing $(x, y)$ such that $\Delta \cap \overline{S U} = \emptyset$. Then $\alpha = \overline{S U}^c$ is a neighborhood of $\Delta$ that satisfies $SU \cap \alpha = \emptyset$. Since $(x, y) \in \RP$, we have $(x, y) \in \overline{S^{-1} \alpha}$. It follows that $U \cap S^{-1} \alpha \neq \emptyset$ or equivalently, $SU \cap \alpha \neq \emptyset$, which is a contradiction.

($\Leftarrow$) Let $\alpha$ be a neighborhood of $\Delta$. We need to show $(x, y) \in \overline {S^{-1} \alpha}$. Let $U$ be an arbitrary open set containing $(x, y)$. It remains to show $U \cap S^{-1} \alpha \neq \emptyset$. The last condition is equivalent to $\alpha \cap SU \neq \emptyset$ which is true because $\Delta \cap \overline{S U} \neq \emptyset$.


    % \dgg{Update this.}
    % Let $(x,y) \in X^2$. By definition, $(x,y) \in \RP$ if and only if for all open neighborhoods $U \subseteq X^2$ of $(x,y)$ and all open neighborhoods $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ of $\Delta$,
    % \begin{align*}
    %     U \cap S^{-1}\alpha \neq \emptyset.
    % \end{align*}
    % By set algebra, the previous line is equivalent to $S U \cap \alpha \neq \emptyset.$

    
    % If the set on the right hand side of \eqref{eqn_in_rp_equiv_set} is non-empty, then for all open neighborhoods $U \subseteq X^2$ of $(x,y)$ and all open neighborhoods $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ of $\Delta$, $\alpha \cap SU \neq \emptyset$, implying that $(x,y) \in \RP$.
    % Conversely, if $(x,y) \in \RP$, then the collection $\{\overline{SU} \cap \Delta \mid U \ni (x,y)\}$ has the finite intersection property, and the set on the right hand in \eqref{eqn_in_rp_equiv_set} is non-empty.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[{cf. \cite[V. 2.12 and 2.14, pg. 416]{devries_book_1993}}]
    \label{lemma_frp_functorial_property}
    \lean{imageOfRPIsInRP}
    \leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a $S$-system and $\pi: X \to Y$ be a factor.  Then $(\pi \times \pi) \RP_X \subseteq \RP_Y$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image,lemma_commuting_map_algebra}
\leanok{}
    Let $\beta \subseteq Y^2$ be an entourage.  Since $\pi: X \to Y$ is continuous and $X$ is compact, it is uniformly continuous.  In particular, there exists $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ an entourage such that $(\pi \otimes \pi) \alpha \subseteq \beta$. (An alternative proof, which would be easier to implement in Lean is the following: Take $\alpha = (\pi \otimes \pi)^{-1} \beta$. We can show $\alpha$ is a neighborhood of the diagonal $\Delta_X$ easily.) We see then by \cref{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image} and \cref{lemma_commuting_map_algebra} that
    \[
        (\pi \times \pi) \RP_X \subseteq (\pi \otimes \pi) \overline{S^{-1} \alpha} \subseteq \overline{(\pi \otimes \pi) S^{-1} \alpha} = \overline{S^{-1}(\pi \otimes \pi) \alpha} \subseteq \overline{S^{-1} \beta},\]
    as was to be shown.
\end{proof}


\subsubsection{Basics of RP-}


\begin{definition}[{Backward regionally proximal relation, cf. \cite[Ch. 5, Section 1]{devries_book_1993}}]
\label{def_rp_relation}
\lean{RPM}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  The \emph{backward regionally proximal relation} is defined as
    \[\RP^- = \bigcap_{\alpha} \overline{S \alpha},\]
    where the intersection is over all $\alpha$ in the uniformity on $X$, and $S$ acts diagonally.  We say that point $x \in X$ is \emph{backward regionally proximal to} point $y \in X$ if $(x,y) \in \RP^-$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_rp_basics_symmetric}
    \lean{RPMIsSymmetric}
    \leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system.  The relation $\RP^-$ is symmetric.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Denote by $\Phi: X^2 \to X^2$ the map $\Phi(x,y) = (y,x)$. Let $(x,y) \in \RP^-$ and $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ be an entourage. By the definition of a uniform space, $\Phi \alpha$ is an entourage (sometimes called $\alpha^{-1}$). Since $(x,y) \in \RP^-$, $(x,y) \in \overline{G \Phi \alpha}$. But $\overline{G \Phi \alpha} = \Phi \overline{G \alpha}$. Therefore, $(y,x) \in \overline{G \alpha}$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_rp_basics_invariant}
    \lean{RPMIsInvariant}
    \leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system.  The relation $\RP^-$ is $S$-invariant.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_intersection_of_invariant}
\leanok{}
    The set $S\alpha$ is clearly $S$-invariant.  Since $S$ acts by continuous maps,
        \[g \overline{S\alpha} \subseteq \overline{gS \alpha} \subseteq \overline{S \alpha},\]
    whereby $\overline{S\alpha}$ is $S$-invariant.  Finally, by \cref{lemma_intersection_of_invariant}, an arbitrary intersection of $S$-invariant sets is $S$-invariant, so $\RP^-$ is $S$-invariant.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_rp_basics_closed}
\lean{RPMIsClosed}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system.  The relation $\RP^-$ is closed.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    An arbitrary intersection of closed sets is closed. 
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_rp_reflexive}
\lean{RPMIsReflexiveIfNondegen}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system.  If $\overline{SX} = X$, then $\RP^-$ is reflexive.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Let $x \in X$.  To show $(x,x) \in \RP^-$, by the definition of $\RP^-$, we must show: for all non-empty, open $U \subseteq X$ containing $x$ and all open $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ containing the diagonal, $(U \times U) \cap S\alpha \neq \emptyset$.

    Let $U \subseteq X$ be open and contain $x$ and $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ contain $\Delta$.  From the assumption that $\overline{SX} = X$, we get that there exists $s \in S$ and $y \in X$ such that $sy \in U$.  Therefore, $s(y,y) \in (U \times U) \cap S \alpha$, demonstrating that this set is non-empty. 
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_rp_implies_ultra}
\lean{xyInRPMIffUltraToSomewwxy}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, $X$ be an $S$-system, and $x, y \in X$. Define $\varphi: X^2 \times S \to X^4$ by
    \[\varphi (a, b, s) = (a, b, sa, sb).\]
    The point $(x,y) \in \RP^-$ if and only if there exist $w \in X$ and an ultrafilter $\family$ on $X^2 \times S$ such that $\lim \varphi \family = (w,w,x,y)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Suppose there exist $w \in X$ and an ultrafilter $\family$ on $X^2 \times S$ such that $\lim \varphi \family = (w,w,x,y)$.  Let $U, V \subseteq X$ and $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ be open neighborhoods of $x, y$ and the diagonal $\Delta$ respectively. Since $\lim \varphi \family = (w,w,x,y)$ and $\alpha \times U \times V$ is an open neighborhood of $(w,w,x,y)$, we have that $\varphi^{-1} (\alpha \times U \times V) \in \family$.  Since $\family$ is an ultrafilter, its members are non-empty.  In particular, there exists $(a,b,s) \in \varphi^{-1} (\alpha \times U \times V)$.  In other words, $(a,b,sa,sb) \in \alpha \times U \times V$, showing that $(U \times V) \cap s \alpha \neq \emptyset$.  By definition, it follows that $(x,y) \in \RP^-$.

    Conversely, suppose $(x,y) \in \RP^-$.  Denote by $N(\Delta)$ the set of open subsets of $X^2$ containing the diagonal, $N(x)$ the set of open subsets of $X$ containing $x$, and by $N(y)$ the set of open subsets of $X$ containing $y$.  By definition, for all $(\alpha, U, V) \in N(\Delta) \times N(x) \times N(y)$, there exists $(a, b, s) \in X^2 \times S$ such that $(a,b) \in \alpha$ and $s (a,b) \in U \times V$. This gives us a map $\psi$ from $N(\Delta) \times N(x) \times N(y)$ to $X^2 \times S$ defined by $\psi: (\alpha, U, V) \mapsto (a,b,s)$.

    
    The sets $N(\Delta)$, $N(x)$, and $N(y)$ are directed by inclusion, so $N(\Delta) \times N(x) \times N(y)$ is directed.  Let $\mathcal{G}$ be an ultrafilter extending a cofinal filter on $N(\Delta) \times N(x) \times N(y)$. We see that $\family \defeq \psi \mathcal{G}$ is an ultrafilter on $X^2 \times S$.  Because $\mathcal{G}$ extends a cofinal filter,
    \[
        \lim \varphi \family \in \Delta \times \{x\} \times \{y\},
    \]
    and hence there exists $w \in X$ such that $\lim \varphi \family = (w,w,x,y)$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal}
%OLD: \lean{commMinOrbInterDiagonalBackward}
\lean{inRPMiffBackwardUOrbitClosHitsDiag}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  For all $(x,y) \in X^2$, $(x,y) \in \RP^-$ if and only if for all open sets $U \subseteq X^2$ containing $(x,y)$,
    \begin{align}
    \label{eqn_in_rp_equiv_set_v2}
         \Delta \cap \overline{S^{-1} U} \neq \emptyset.
    \end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{remark}
    Note by the finite intersection property, it is easy to enhance the conclusion to
    \[\Delta \cap \bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} \overline{S^{-1} U} \neq \emptyset.\]
    We didn't find a use for this.
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}
In Lean, we only formalize the following statement:
if $(x, y) \in \RP^-$, then for all open neighborhood $U$ of $(x, y)$, 
\[
    \Delta \cap \overline{S^{-1} U} \neq \emptyset,
\]
which suffices for our purpose.
If we want to formalize the stronger statement, we need to invoke finite intersection property. \dgg{To satisfy the claim that we've verified ALL results, we will still need to do this!}    
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    \dgg{update this.}
    Let $(x,y) \in X^2$. By definition, $(x,y) \in \RP^-$ if and only if for all open neighborhoods $U \subseteq X^2$ of $(x,y)$ and all open neighborhoods $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ of $\Delta$,
    \begin{align*}
        U \cap S\alpha \neq \emptyset.
    \end{align*}
    By set algebra, the previous line is equivalent to $S^{-1} U \cap \alpha \neq \emptyset.$

    
    If the set on the right hand side of \eqref{eqn_in_rp_equiv_set_v2} is non-empty, then for all open neighborhoods $U \subseteq X^2$ of $(x,y)$ and all open neighborhoods $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ of $\Delta$, $\alpha \cap S^{-1}U \neq \emptyset$, implying that $(x,y) \in \RP^-$.
    Conversely, if $(x,y) \in \RP^-$, then the collection $\{\overline{S^{-1}U} \cap \Delta \mid U \ni (x,y)\}$ has the finite intersection property, and the set on the right hand in \eqref{eqn_in_rp_equiv_set_v2} is non-empty.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[{cf. \cite[V. 2.12 and 2.14, pg. 416]{devries_book_1993}}]
    \label{lemma_rp_functorial_property}
    \lean{imageOfRPMIsInRPM}
    %\lean{imageOfRPMIsInRPM}
    \leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a $S$-system and $\pi: X \to Y$ be a factor.  Then $(\pi \times \pi) \RP^-_X \subseteq \RP^-_Y$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image}
\leanok{}
    Let $\beta \subseteq Y^2$ be an entourage.  Since $\pi: X \to Y$ is continuous and $X$ is compact, it is uniformly continuous.  In particular, there exists $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ an entourage such that $(\pi \otimes \pi) \alpha \subseteq \beta$.  We see then by \cref{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image} that
    \[
        (\pi \times \pi) \RP^-_X \subseteq (\pi \otimes \pi) \overline{S \alpha} \subseteq \overline{(\pi \otimes \pi) S \alpha} = \overline{S(\pi \otimes \pi) \alpha} \subseteq \overline{S \beta},\]
    as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Image of RP in commutative minimal systems}

First, the under the assumptions that $S$ is commutative and $X$ is a minimal $S$-system, the regionally proximal relation is the same as the backward regionally proximal relation.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_forward_equals_backward_rp}
    \lean{forwardEqualsBackwardRPInMinCommSystem}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be a a minimal $S$-system. Then $\RP = \RP^-$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_dense_S_minimal_points_in_X2,lemma_forward_backward_set_orb_clos_coincide,lemma_frp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal,lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal}
    This is immediate by combining \cref{lemma_dense_S_minimal_points_in_X2,lemma_forward_backward_set_orb_clos_coincide} with \cref{lemma_frp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal,lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal}
\end{proof}

The following lemma upgrades \cref{lemma_frp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal} in the case that $S$ is commutative and $X$ is minimal.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_frp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_contains_diagonal}
\label{lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_contains_diagonal}
%OLD: \lean{commMinOrbContainDiagonalBackward}
\lean{inMinCommxyInRPIffNhdOrbitClosContainsDiag}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be a minimal $S$-system.  The point $(x,y) \in \RP$ if and only if for all open sets $U \subseteq X^2$ containing $(x,y)$,
    \[\Delta \subseteq \overline{S U}.\]
    The three other statements gotten by independently replacing $S$ by $S^{-1}$ and $\RP$ by $\RP^-$ also hold.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_frp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal,lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal,lemma_dense_S_minimal_points_in_X2,lemma_thick_right_shift_is_thick,lemma_forward_backward_set_orb_clos_coincide,lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image,thm_forward_equals_backward_rp}
    First, the proof of the $\RP^-$ variant with $S^{-1}$.  The ``if'' statement follows immediately from \cref{lemma_frp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal}.

    \dgg{This argument is slightly simplified now with $Z = \overline{S^{-1} U}$.  Can update proof.}
    
    Let $(x,y) \in \RP^-$, and put
    \[Z \defeq \bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} \overline{S^{-1} U}.\]
    We will show that $Z \cap \Delta$ is non-empty, closed, and $S$-invariant.  It will follow then from the minimality of $X$ that $Z \cap \Delta = \Delta$.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \item That $Z \cap \Delta$ is closed is immediate.

        \item That $Z \cap \Delta$ is non-empty follows from \cref{lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal}.

        \item To see that $Z \cap \Delta$ is $S$-invariant, let $s \in S$.  Since $\Delta$ is $S$-invariant, it suffices to show that $Z$ is $S$-invariant.  By our assumptions and \cref{lemma_dense_S_minimal_points_in_X2}, the $S$-system $X^2$ has a dense set of periodic points.  By \cref{lemma_thick_right_shift_is_thick}, the set $Ss$ is thick. Therefore, by \cref{lemma_forward_backward_set_orb_clos_coincide}, we have that
        \[Z = \bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} \overline{(Ss)^{-1} U}.\]
        Note that $s(Ss)^{-1} U \subseteq S^{-1} U$.
        Therefore, by \cref{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image},
        \[
            sZ = s\bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} \overline{(Ss)^{-1} U} \subseteq \bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} s \overline{(Ss)^{-1} U} \subseteq \bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} \overline{S^{-1} U} = Z.
        \]
    \end{enumerate}

    To see that the three other statements gotten by independently replacing $S$ by $S^{-1}$ and $\RP$ by $\RP^-$ also hold, note that by \cref{lemma_forward_backward_set_orb_clos_coincide}, for all non-empty, open $U \subseteq X^2$,
    \[\overline{S U} = \overline{S^{-1}U}.\]
    Also, by \cref{thm_forward_equals_backward_rp}, we have $\RP = \RP^-$.  Therefore, given the assumptions of the theorem, all four statements are equivalent.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Explicit proof of the RP variant]
\uses{lemma_frp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal,lemma_dense_S_minimal_points_in_X2,lemma_thick_right_shift_is_thick,lemma_forward_backward_set_orb_clos_coincide,lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image,thm_forward_equals_backward_rp}
    \dgg{Currently, I believe the RP minus with the backward U orbit is formalized.}
    Let $(x,y) \in \RP$, and put
    \[Z \defeq \bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} \overline{S U}.\]
    
    We will show that $Z \cap \Delta$ is a subsystem of $\Delta$, that is, non-empty, closed, and $S$-invariant. That $Z \cap \Delta$ is closed is immediate. That $Z \cap \Delta$ is non-empty follows from \cref{lemma_frp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal}.
    To see that $Z \cap \Delta$ is $S$-invariant, let $s \in S$.  Since $\Delta$ is $S$-invariant, it suffices to show that $Z$ is $S$-invariant. By our assumptions and \cref{lemma_dense_S_minimal_points_in_X2}, the $S$-system $X^2$ has a dense set of uniformly recurrent points.  By \cref{lemma_thick_right_shift_is_thick}, the set $Ss$ is thick, so by \cref{lemma_forward_backward_set_orb_clos_coincide}, $\overline{SsU} = \overline{SU}$. Therefore, by \cref{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image},
    \[
        sZ = s\bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} \overline{S U} \subseteq \bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} s \overline{S U} = \bigcap_{\substack{U \ni (x,y) \\ U \text{ open}}} \overline{sS U} = Z.
    \]

    Since $X$ is a minimal $S$-system, the diagonal $\Delta$ is a minimal $S$-system. Since $Z \cap \Delta$ is a subsystem of $\Delta$, we get that $Z \cap \Delta = \Delta$. Therefore, $\Delta \subseteq Z$, as was to be shown.

    To see that the three other statements gotten by independently replacing $S$ by $S^{-1}$ and $\RP$ by $\RP^-$ also hold, note that by \cref{lemma_forward_backward_set_orb_clos_coincide}, for all non-empty, open $U \subseteq X^2$,
    \[\overline{S U} = \overline{S^{-1}U}.\]
    Also, by \cref{thm_forward_equals_backward_rp}, we have $\RP = \RP^-$.  Therefore, given the assumptions of the theorem, all four statements are equivalent.
\end{proof}


The following lemma is shown in \cite[V. 2.12 and 2.14, pg. 416]{devries_book_1993} for group actions.  Our semigroup version is considerably more involved since our semigroup maps are not necessarily open.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{lemma_frp_functorial_property_minimal}
    \label{lemma_rp_functorial_property_minimal}
    \lean{commMinRPIsInImageOfRP}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system, and let $\pi: X \to Y$ be a factor map of $S$-systems.  Then $\RP_Y = (\pi \times \pi) \RP_X$ and $\RP^-_Y = (\pi \times \pi) \RP^-_X$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_forward_equals_backward_rp,lemma_rp_functorial_property,lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal,lemma_open_nbhd_of_fiber_contains_open_preimage,lemma_image_of_entourage_somewhere_dense_along_diagonal,lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_contains_diagonal}
    By \cref{thm_forward_equals_backward_rp}, we have $\RP = \RP^-$.  It suffices then to prove the result for $\RP^-$.

        By \cref{lemma_rp_functorial_property}, $(\pi \times \pi) \RP^-_X \subseteq \RP^-_Y$, so we only need to prove the reverse inclusion.

    To see that $\RP^-_Y \subseteq (\pi \times \pi) \RP^-_X$, we will show that $Y \setminus (\pi \times \pi) \RP^-_X \subseteq Y \setminus \RP^-_Y$.  Suppose $(x,y) \in Y \setminus (\pi \times \pi) \RP^-_X$.  For all $f \in (\pi \times \pi)^{-1} \{(x,y)\}$, we have that $f \not\in \RP^-_X$.  By \cref{lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_hits_diagonal}, there exists an open set $V_{f} \subseteq X^2$ with $f \in V_{f}$ such that $\overline{S^{-1}V_{f}} \cap \Delta = \emptyset$. Therefore, there exists an open $\alpha_f \subseteq X^2$ containing $\Delta_X$ such that
    \[S^{-1}V_{f} \cap \alpha_f = \emptyset.\]

    Now
    \[(\pi \times \pi)^{-1} \{(x,y)\} \subseteq \bigcup_{(w,z) \in (\pi \times \pi)^{-1} \{(x,y)\}} V_{f},\]
    so by compactness, there exists a finite set $F \subseteq (\pi \times \pi)^{-1} \{(x,y)\}$ such that
    \[(\pi \times \pi)^{-1} \{(x,y)\} \subseteq \bigcup_{f \in F} V_{f}.\]
    We have by \cref{lemma_open_nbhd_of_fiber_contains_open_preimage} (with $(x,y)$ as $y$ and $\bigcup_{f \in F} V_{f}$ as $W$) that there exists $U \ni (x,y)$ such that $(\pi \times \pi)^{-1} U \subseteq \bigcup_{f \in F} V_{f}$.

    Put $\beta = \bigcap_{f \in F} \alpha_f$; it is an open neighborhood of $\Delta_X$. We see that
    \begin{align*}
        \emptyset = \bigcup_{f \in F} S^{-1}V_f \cap \beta  &= \big( S^{-1} \bigcup_{f \in F} V_f \big) \cap \beta \\
        &\supseteq \big(S^{-1} (\pi \times \pi)^{-1} U \big) \cap \beta \\
        & = \big((\pi \times \pi)^{-1} S^{-1} U \big) \cap \beta.
    \end{align*}
    Since
    \[\big((\pi \times \pi)^{-1} S^{-1} U \big) \cap \beta = \emptyset,\]
    we have that
    \[\big(S^{-1} U \big) \cap (\pi \times \pi) \beta  = \emptyset.\]

    
    By \cref{lemma_image_of_entourage_somewhere_dense_along_diagonal}, there exists a non-empty, open set $Z \subseteq Y$ such that $Z \times Z \subseteq (\pi \times \pi) \beta$, so
    \[\big(S^{-1} U \big) \cap (Z \times Z)  = \emptyset.\]
    It follows that
    \[\Delta \not\supseteq \overline{S^{-1} U}.\]
    Since $U \ni (x,y)$, we see by \cref{lemma_rp_iff_nbhd_tran_int_contains_diagonal} that $(x,y) \not\in \RP^-_Y$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}
