\section{Furstenberg families}
\label{sec_furstenberg_families}

Needs: \cref{sec_ultrafilters,sec_family_algebra}

Contains:
\begin{itemize}
    \item A few abstract results, and specific families: syndetic, thick, dcsyndetic, Bohr sets, sets of Bohr recurrence, etc...
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Abstract results}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_pr_family_concentration}
\lean{visitTimeConcentrationForPRFamily}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be an $S$-system, $x \in X$, and $U \subseteq X$ be closed.  Let $\family$ be a partition regular family of subsets of $S$. If $R(x,U) \in \family$, then there exists $y \in U$ such that for all non-empty, open $V \ni y$, $R(x,V) \in \family$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    \dgg{This argument can be shortened considerably using filter limits, which are already part of mathlib.  The family $\family^*$ is a filter and $U$ is a closed set belonging to the pushforward of $\family^*$ under the action map through the point $x$.  By compactness, there is a cluster point $y$ of the pushforward of $\family^*$.  This says exactly that for all non-empty, open $V \ni y$, $R(x,V) \in \family$.}
    We prove the contrapositive.  Suppose for all $y \in U$, there exists non-empty, open $V_y \ni y$ such that $R(x,V_y) \not\in \family$.  By compactness of $U$, there exists a finite set $F \subseteq X$ such that $U \subseteq \cup_{y \in F} V_y$. Then
    \[R(x,U) \subseteq R(x,\cup_{y \in F} V_y) = \cup_{y \in F} R(x,V_y).\]
    Since no $R(x,V_y)$ is in $\family$, we have that $\cup_{y \in F} R(x,V_y) \not\in \family$, and hence that $R(x,U) \not\in \family$, as desired.
\end{proof}



\subsection{Syndetic and thick sets}

\begin{definition}
\label{def_syndetic}
\lean{isSyndetic}
\leanok{}
Let $(S,\cdot)$ be a semigroup and $A \subseteq S$.  The set $A$ is syndetic if there exists a finite set $F \subseteq S$ such that $S = \cup_{f \in F} f^{-1} A$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{def_thick}
\lean{isThick}
\leanok{}
Let $(S,\cdot)$ be a semigroup and $A \subseteq S$. The set $A$ is thick if for all finite $F \subseteq S$, there exists $s \in S$ such that $Fs \subseteq A$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_synd_thick_dual}
    \lean{dualSyndeticThick}
    \leanok{}
    $\syndetic = \thick^*$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Easy from definitions.
\end{proof}

% \begin{lemma}
%     \label{lemma_preimage_of_syndetic}
%     If $\varphi: G \to H$ is a surjective semigroup homomorphism and $A \subseteq H$ is syndetic in $H$, then $\varphi^{-1} A$ is syndetic in $G$.
% \end{lemma}

% \begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
%     Write $H = \cup_{f \in F} f^{-1} A$.  Then
%     \[G = \varphi^{-1} H = \cup_{f \in F} \varphi^{-1} f^{-1} A = \cup_{b \in B} b^{-1} \varphi^{-1} A,\]
%     where $B \subseteq G$ is finite and $\varphi B = F$.  Thus, $\varphi^{-1} A$ is syndetic.
% \end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_image_of_syndetic}
    \lean{surjImgOfSyndeticIsSyndetic}
    \leanok{}
    If $\varphi: G \to H$ is a surjective semigroup homomorphism and $A \subseteq G$ is syndetic in $G$, then $\varphi A$ is syndetic in $H$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Since $A \subseteq G$ is syndetic, there exists a finite set $F \subseteq G$ such that for all $g \in G$, there exists $f \in F$ such that $fg \in A$.

    We claim that $\varphi F$ has the property that for all $h \in H$, there exists $f \in F$ such that $\varphi(f)h \in \varphi A$.  Indeed, let $h \in H$.  Write $h = \varphi g$ for some $g \in G$.  There is $f \in F$ such that $fg \in A$.  Apply $\varphi$ to see that $\varphi(f) h \in \varphi A$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_thick_inverse_shift_intersections}
    \label{lemma_preimage_intersection_of_thick_is_thick}
    \lean{inverseDilateCapOfThickIsThick}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $H \subseteq S$ be thick.  For all finite $K \subseteq S$, the set $\cap_{k \in K} k^{-1} H$ is thick.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Let $K \subseteq S$ be finite.  To see that $\cap_{k \in K} k^{-1} H$ is thick, let $F \subseteq S$ be finite.  The set $KF$ is finite, so there is $s \in S$ such that $KFs \subseteq H$. We see $Fs \subseteq \cap_{k \in K} k^{-1} H$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_thick_right_shift_is_thick}
    \lean{rightTransOfThickIsThick}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $H \subseteq S$ be thick.  For all $t \in S$, the set $Ht$ is thick.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Let $F \subseteq S$ be finite and $t \in S$.  There exists $s \in S$ such that $Fs \subseteq H$.  Then $Fst \subseteq Ht$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_thick_shift_intersections}
    \lean{commDilateCapOfThickIsThick}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $H \subseteq S$ be thick. For all finite $K \subseteq S$, the set $\cap_{k \in K} kH$ is thick.
\end{lemma}

\dgg{We may not use this?}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_thick_inverse_shift_intersections}
    Let $K \subseteq S$ be finite.  For $k \in K$, let $p_k$ be the product of all the elements of $K$ except $k$, and let $p$ be the product of all elements of $K$.  By \cref{lemma_thick_inverse_shift_intersections}, the set $\cap_{k \in K} p_k^{-1} H$ is thick.
    
    To see that $\cap_{k \in F} kH$ is thick, let $F \subseteq S$ finite.  Since $\cap_{k \in K} p_k^{-1} H$ is thick, there exists $s \in S$ such that $Fs \subseteq \cap_{k \in K} p_k^{-1} H$.  Therefore,
    \[Fps = p F s \subseteq p \cap_{k \in K} p_k^{-1} H \subseteq \cap_{k \in K} p p_k^{-1} H \subseteq \cap_{k \in K} k H,\]
    as was to be shown. \anh{This proof requires $S$ to be a monoid. Otherwise, $p_k$ may not be defined if $|K| \leq 1$.}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_thick_closure_contains_ideal}
    \lean{thickClosureContainsIdeal}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $H \subseteq S$ be thick.  The set $\overline{H}$ contains a minimal left ideal.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_thick_inverse_shift_intersections,thm_ultrafilters_are_compact_right_top,lemma_pull_out_inverse_mult_in_basis_set,thm_ultra_right_mult_continuous,lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image,thm_every_left_contains_minimal_left}
    By \cref{lemma_thick_inverse_shift_intersections}, the set
    \[\big\{ \bigcap_{f \in F} \overline{f^{-1} H} \mid F \subseteq G \text{ finite, non-empty}\big\}\]
    has the finite intersection property.  Since $\beta S$ is compact (\cref{thm_ultrafilters_are_compact_right_top}), the set
    \[L \defeq \bigcap_{g \in S} \overline{g^{-1}H}\]
    is non-empty.
    
    We will show that $L$ is a left ideal.  Let $p \in L$.  We will show first that for all $s \in S$, $\delta_s p \in L$.  Let $s \in S$, $g \in S$. We must show that $\delta_s p \in \overline{g^{-1} H}$.  By the definition of $L$ and \cref{lemma_pull_out_inverse_mult_in_basis_set}, we see
    \[\delta_sp \in \delta_s \overline{(gs)^{-1} H} = \delta_s \overline{s^{-1}g^{-1} H} = \delta_s \delta_s^{-1} \overline{g^{-1} H} \subseteq \overline{g^{-1} H},\]
    as desired.  Denoting right multiplication by $p$ by $\rho_p: \beta S \to \beta S$, we have shown that $\rho_p(\{\delta_s : s \in S\}) \subseteq L$.

    By \cref{thm_ultra_right_mult_continuous}, $\rho_p$ is continuous.  Since $L$ is closed, by \cref{lemma_image_closure_equals_closure_image}, we see
    \[\rho_p(\beta S) = \rho_p(\overline{S}) = \overline{\rho_p(\{\delta_s : s \in S\})} \subseteq \overline{L} = L.\]
    In other words: for all $q \in \beta S$, $qL \subseteq L$, as was to be shown.
    
    By \cref{thm_ultrafilters_are_compact_right_top} and \cref{thm_every_left_contains_minimal_left}, the left ideal $L$ contains a minimal left ideal, hence so does $H$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_thick_closure_contains_strong_ultrafilter}
    \lean{minIdempotentWitnessesShiftIntersectionLargeness}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $H \subseteq S$ be thick. There exists a minimal idempotent $p \in \overline{H}$ such that for all finite $F \subseteq S$, the set $\cap_{f \in F} f^{-1} H \in p$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_thick_closure_contains_ideal,lemma_exists_idempotent_in_left_ideals}
    From the proof of \cref{lemma_thick_closure_contains_ideal}, there exists a minimal left ideal
    \[L' \subseteq \bigcap_{g \in S} \overline{g^{-1}H}.\]

    By \cref{lemma_exists_idempotent_in_left_ideals}, $L'$ contains an idempotent $p$.  Then $p$ is a minimal, idempotent ultrafilter.  To see that it has the stated property, let $F \subseteq S$ be finite.  Then
    \[p \in L' \subseteq \bigcap_{f \in F} \overline{f^{-1} H},\]
    which means that $\cap_{f \in F} f^{-1} H \in p$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Delta sets}

\begin{definition}
\lean{isDeltaZero}
\leanok{}
Let $(S,\cdot)$ be a semigroup and $A \subseteq S$.
The set $A$ is a $\Delta_0$ set if for all $N \in \N$, there exists a sequence $(s_n)_{n=1}^N \subseteq S$ such that for all $1 \leq m < n \leq N$, $s_n \in s_m A$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\lean{isDelta}
\leanok{}
Let $(S,\cdot)$ be a semigroup and $A \subseteq S$. The set $A$ is a $\Delta$ set (Hindman-Strauss call this more generally a $Q$-set, where $Q$ is for quotient) if there exists a sequence $(s_n)_n \subseteq S$ such that for all $1 \leq m < n$, $s_n \in s_m A$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_delta_in_deltazero}
    \lean{deltaFamilyContainedInDeltaZeroFamily}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup.  $\Delta \subseteq \Delta_0$
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    This is immediate from the definitions.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Bohr sets}

\dgg{In the definition below, we require the system to be minimal.  We can drop that assumption if all points are uniformly recurrent for an equicontinuous semigroup action.  This is true for group actions, but may not be true any longer for semigroup actions. The papers \cite{dai_xiao_2017,auslander_dai_2019} may have something to say about that.}

\dgg{Note that for our definition of semigroup action, $S$ could act on $X$ by every element sending all points $x$ to a single point $x_0$.  Then this action is equicontinuous, but there are lots of non-uniformly recurrent points!}

\begin{definition}
\label{def_dcsyndeticbohr}
\lean{bohrZeroFamily}
\leanok{}
Let $(S,\cdot)$ be a semigroup and $A \subseteq S$. The set $A$ is a $\dcsyndeticbohr$ set if there exists a minimal equicontinuous $S$-system $X$, a point $x \in X$, and a non-empty, open set $U \subseteq X$ such that $R(x,U) \subseteq A$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{def_dcthickbohr}
\lean{setOfBohrRecurrenceFamily}
\leanok{}
Let $(S,\cdot)$ be a semigroup and $A \subseteq S$. The set $A$ is a $\dcthickbohr$ set (that is, a set of Bohr recurrence for $S$ actions) if for all minimal equicontinuous $S$-systems $X$, all $x \in X$, and all $U \subseteq X$ containing $x$, $A \cap R(x,U) \neq \emptyset$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_dcthickbohr_dual}
    \lean{dualBohrZeroSetsOfBohrRecurrence}
    \leanok{}
    $\dcsyndeticbohr = \dcthickbohr^*$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Immediate from definitions.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_is_filter}
    \lean{commBohrZeroFamilyIsFilter}
    \leanok{}
    Suppose $S$ is commutative. $\dcsyndeticbohr$ is a filter.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_arbitrary_product_of_equi_is_equi,lemma_subsys_of_equi_is_equi,thm_every_system_has_minimal_subsystem,lemma_minimal_equi_implies_homogeneous,lemma_return_time_sets_in_prod_system}
    Let $A, B \in \dcsyndeticbohr$.  Then $A = R(x,U)$ and $B = R(y,V)$ in minimal equicontinuous $S$-systems $X$ and $Y$.  By \cref{lemma_arbitrary_product_of_equi_is_equi}, the $S$-system $X \times Y$ is equicontinuous.  By \cref{lemma_subsys_of_equi_is_equi}, subsystems of equicontinuous systems are equicontinuous.  So, it suffices to show that the point $(x,y)$ belongs to a minimal subsystem of $X \times Y$.

    By \cref{thm_every_system_has_minimal_subsystem}, there exists an $S$-minimal subsystem $Z \subseteq X \times Y$.  Since $\pi_2 Z = Y$, there exists $(w,y) \in Z$.  By \cref{lemma_minimal_equi_implies_homogeneous}, there exists a factor map $\varphi: X \to X$ such that $\varphi(x) = w$.  Apply $\varphi \otimes \text{Id}$ to $Z$ to get a minimal subsystem of $X \times Y$ containing $(x,y)$. By product system visit time algebra (\cref{lemma_return_time_sets_in_prod_system}), $A \cap B = R((x,y), U \times V)$, whereby $A \cap B \in \dcsyndeticbohr$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_dcthickbohr_is_pr}
    \lean{commSetOfBohrRecurrenceFamilyIsPR}
    \leanok{}
    $\dcthickbohr$ is partition regular.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_dcthickbohr_dual,lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_is_filter,thm_family_pr_iff_dual_is_filter}
    That $\dcthickbohr$ is partition regular follows immediately from \cref{lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_dcthickbohr_dual}, \cref{lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_is_filter}, and \cref{thm_family_pr_iff_dual_is_filter}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm_delta_zero_set_of_bohr_rec}
    \label{commDeltaZeroImpliesSetOfBohrRecurrence}
    \leanok{}
    For $S$ a commutative semigroup, $\Delta_0 \subseteq \dcthickbohr$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_minimal_comm_action_implies_surjective}
    Suppose $A \in \Delta_0$ and we want to show that $A \cap R(x,U) \neq \emptyset$, where $X$ is a minimal, equicontinuous $S$-system, $x \in U \subseteq X$.  Since $X$ is compact, Hausdorff, it has a unique uniform structure.  Thus, there exists an entourage $\beta \subseteq X^2$ such that $\beta[x] \subseteq U$.  By equicontinuity, there exists an entourage $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ such that for all $s \in S$, $(s \otimes s) \alpha \subseteq \beta$.

    Let $\gamma$ be an entourage such that $\gamma \circ \gamma \subseteq \alpha$. But replacing $\gamma$ with $\gamma \cap \gamma^{-1}$, we can assume $\gamma$ is symmetric (i.e. $(x, y) \in \gamma$ if and only if $(y, x) \in \gamma)$.
    
    Note that $\Delta(X) \subseteq \gamma$, whereby
    \[
        X = \bigcup_{x \in X} \gamma[x].
    \]
    By compactness, there exists a finite set $Z \subseteq X$ such that
    \[
        X = \bigcup_{z \in Z} \gamma[z].
    \]
    Put $N = |Z|+1$.  By the assumption that $A$ is a $\Delta_0$ set, there exists $s_1, \ldots, s_N \in S$ such that for all $1 \leq m < n \leq N$, $s_n \in s_m A$.
    
    By \cref{lemma_minimal_comm_action_implies_surjective}, the elements $s_1, \ldots, s_N$ act surjectively on $X$.  Therefore, there exists $y_1, \ldots, y_N \in X$ such that $s_i y_i = x$.
    
    Consider the points $y_1, \ldots, y_N$.  Since $N > |Z|$, by pigeonhole principle, there are $1 \leq m < n \leq N$ and $z \in Z$ such that $(y_n, y_m) \in \gamma[z] \times \gamma[z]$. It follows that $(z, y_n), (z, y_m) \in \gamma$. Since $\gamma$ is symmetric, $(y_n, z) \in \gamma$. Since $\gamma \circ \gamma \subseteq \alpha$, we have $(y_n, y_m) \in \alpha$.
    
    Since $s_n \in s_m A$, there exist $a \in A$ such that $s_n = s_m a$.
    Act on the product by $s_n$ to see that
    \[
        (x,a x) = (x, a s_m y_m) = (x, s_n y_m) = (s_n \otimes s_n) (y_n, y_m) \in (s_n \otimes s_n) \alpha \subseteq \beta,
    \]
    whereby $a x \in \beta[x]$. Since $\beta[x] \subseteq U$, we have $a \in R(x,U)$.  Note that $a \in A$ and so $A \cap R(x,U) \neq \emptyset$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The proof would be a straight-forward application of the pigeonhole principle if $S$ were a group and the phase spaces of the systems we consider were metrizable. In the general setting we are in, without access to inverses in $S$ and even not assuming cancellativity of $S$, more work is required before the pigeonhole principle is applied.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Dynamically central syndetic sets}

The following definition of central sets is ``Lean friendly'' since it does not introduce another universe-level metavariable.

\begin{definition}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup.  A set $A \subseteq S$ is dynamically central syndetic if there exists a minimal ultrafilter $p \in \beta S$ and an open set $U \subseteq \beta S$ containing $p$ such that $R(p,U) \subseteq A$.
\end{definition}

\dgg{To bring back ``metrizability'', if we would care to, we could make the definition for symbolic space.  Though that would only work for countable $S$, so maybe this is not really interesting.}

\begin{theorem}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $A \subseteq S$.  The following are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item the set $A$ is dynamically central syndetic;

        \item there exists a minimal $S$-system $X$, a point $x \in X$, and an open set $U \subseteq X$ containing $x$ such that $R(x,U) \subseteq A$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_ur_lift}
    1. implies 2. We assume $A \supseteq R(p,U)$ where $p$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent.  Put $X = \overline{Sp}$, $x = p$, and $V = X \cap U$.  Then $R(x,V) = R(p,U) \subseteq A$, as was to be shown.

    2. implies 1.  We assume $A \supseteq R(x,U)$.  Let $\pi: \beta S \to X$ be $p \mapsto px$.  By \cref{thm_ur_lift}, there exists an $S$-uniformly recurrent point $p \in \beta S$ so that $\pi p = x$.  Then we see that
    \[R(p,\pi^{-1}U) = R(x,U) \subseteq A,\]
    as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Central sets}

The following definition of central sets is ``Lean friendly'' since it does not introduce another universe-level metavariable.

\begin{definition}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup.  A set $A \subseteq S$ is \emph{central} if it belongs to a minimal idempotent ultrafilter on $S$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $A \subseteq S$.  The following are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item
        \label{item_minidemp_central}
        the set $A$ is central;

        \item 
        \label{item_f_central}
        there exists a minimal $S$-system $X$, a point $x \in X$, and an open set $U \subseteq X$ containing a point proximal to $x$ that is $S$-uniformly recurrent such that $A = R(x,U)$; and \dgg{This is almost Furstenberg's definition, but note that we have not assumed that $X$ is metric.  Maybe we cannot anyways if $S$ is not assumed to be countable?}

        \item
        \label{item_dcscapthick_central}
        there exists a thick set $H \subseteq S$ and a dynamically central syndetic set $D \subseteq S$ such that $A \supseteq D \cap H$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_uniform_near_diag,thm_prox_pair_visits_diag_thickly}
    1. implies 2. \ Let $A \in p$, a minimal idempotent ultrafilter.  Let $M_S$ be the monoid extension of $S$.  Note that $\beta (M_S)$ is an $M_S$-system, so via $i: S \to M_S$, it is an $S$-system.  Since $p$ is a minimal idempotent ultrafilter, we have that $\delta_e$ is proximal to the $S$-uniformly recurrent point $p \delta_e$.
    
    We claim that $R(\delta_e, \overline{iA}) = A$ and that $p \delta_e \in \overline{iA}$.  This finishes the proof.
    
    To see that $R(\delta_e, \overline{iA}) = A$, note that $s \in R(\delta_e, \overline{iA})$ iff $s \delta_e \in \overline{iA}$ iff $(is) \delta_e \in \overline{iA}$ iff $\delta_{is} \in \overline{iA}$ iff $is \in iA$ iff $s \in A$ (since $i$ is injective).

    To see that $p \delta_e \in \overline{iA}$, note that since $\overline{iA}$ is closed, it suffices to have that $R(\delta_e,\overline{iA}) \in p$.  But $R(\delta_e,\overline{iA}) = A$ by the previous point, and $A \in p$ by assumption.

    2. implies 3. Suppose $x$ is proximal to $y \in U$.  Using \cref{lemma_uniform_near_diag}, there exists $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ and $V$ containing $y$ such that
    \[R((x,y),\alpha) \cap R(y,V) \subseteq R(x,U).\]
    We see that $R(y,V) \in \dcsyndetic$ and $R((x,y), \alpha)$ is thick (by \cref{thm_prox_pair_visits_diag_thickly}), as desired.

    3. implies 1. \ Suppose $A \in \dcsyndetic \classcap \thick$.  We will prove that there exists a minimal idempotent ultrafilter $p \in \beta S$ such that $A \in p$.  It will follow from \cite[Thm. 19.27]{hindman_strauss_book_2012} that the set $A$ is central according to our definition above.

    There exists a minimal $S$-system $X$, a point $x \in X$, an open set $U \subseteq X$ containing $x$, and a thick set $H \subseteq S$ such that $A \supseteq R(x,U) \cap H$.  Define $\mathscr{F}$ to be the upward closure of all sets of the form $R(x,V)$, where $V \subseteq X$ is open and contains $x$.
    It is easy to see that $\mathscr{F}$ is a filter every member of which is syndetic.
    
    Define
    \[\overline{\mathscr{F}} = \bigcap_{C \in \mathscr{F}} \overline{C}.\]
    We claim that $\overline{\mathscr{F}}$ is a compact subsemigroup of $\beta S$. Indeed, it is compact since it is closed and $\beta S$ is Hausdorff.  To see that it is a semigroup, suppose $p, q \in \overline{\mathscr{F}}$. To see that $pq \in \overline{\mathscr{F}}$, we must show that for all open $V \subseteq X$ containing $x$, we have that $R(x, V) \in pq$.  Let $V \subseteq X$ be open and contain $x$.
    Note that since $q \in \overline{\mathscr{F}}$, we have that if $s \in R(x,V)$, then $R(x,s^{-1}V) \in q$, whereby
    \begin{align*}
        R(x,V) &\subseteq \big\{ s \in S \mid R(x,s^{-1}V) \in q \} \\
        &= \big\{ s \in S \mid s^{-1}R(x,V) \in q \}\\
        &= \big\{ s \in S \mid \{ t \in T \mid st \in R(x,V) \} \in q \}.
    \end{align*}
    Because $p \in \overline{\mathscr{F}}$, we have that if $R(x,V) \in p$, so
    \[\big\{ s \in S \mid \{ t \in T \mid st \in R(x,V) \} \in q \} \in p,\]
    which is to say that $R(x, V) \in pq$, as desired.

    Since $H$ is thick, by \cite[Thm. 4.48]{hindman_strauss_book_2012}, there exists a minimal left ideal $L \subseteq \overline{H}$.
    We claim that the set $L \cap \overline{\mathscr{F}}$ contains an idempotent.
    We will use the fact that for all syndetic sets $A \subseteq S$ and all minimal left ideals $L \subseteq \beta \N$, we have $\overline{A} \cap L \neq \emptyset$ (\cite[Thm. 4.48]{hindman_strauss_book_2012}).  Since $L$ is a minimal left ideal, it is compact by \cite[Thm. 2.6]{hindman_strauss_book_2012}.  Since $\mathscr{F}$ is a filter and every member is syndetic, the family
    \[\big\{\overline{A} \cap L  \mid  A \in \mathscr{F} \big\}\]
    has the finite intersection property.  Since $L$ is compact, we have that the set $\overline{\mathscr{F}} \cap L$ is a compact, non-empty subsemigroup. By \cite[Thm. 2.5]{hindman_strauss_book_2012}, there exists an idempotent $q \in L \cap \overline{\mathscr{F}}$, as claimed.

    Finally, note that
    \[q \in L \cap \overline{\mathscr{F}} \subseteq \overline{H} \cap \overline{R(x,U)},\]
    whereby the set $R(x,U) \cap H$ is contained in the minimal idempotent $q$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}


\section{RP is an EQ relation}
\label{sec_RP}

Needs: \cref{sec_ultrafilters}

Contains:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Proof that RP is an equivalence relation
    \item Application: $\RP = \seq$
\end{itemize}

In this section, $S$ is a commutative (discrete) semigroup.  We prove that $\RP$ is an equivalence relation.  This has not been shown before in this generality, to our knowledge.

\subsection{Corner space}
\label{sec_corner_space}

% \dgg{At points below, we need an identity.  Two routes: follow the above, where we get a monoid action from a semigroup action, or, instead of having $S^2$ act, we have have the semigroup of $C(X^3,C^3)$ generated by $T_{L,s}$ and $T_{R,t}$ act, where we note in that case that we do have an identity which acts as the identity.  (But then careful: $S^2$ does not surject onto this semigroup if $S$ does not contain an identity element...)  I vote for the second option if there are no other places in this project where we need a monoid action!}

Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  For $s, t \in S$, define maps $T_{L,s}, T_{R,t}: X^3 \to X^3$ by
\begin{align*}
    T_{L,s}(x,y,z) &= (sx, sy, z)\\
    T_{R,t}(x,y,z) &= (x, ty, tz).
\end{align*}

\begin{lemma}[Action of $S^2$ on $X^3$]
\label{lemma_S2_action_on_X3}
\lean{cornerSystem3}
\leanok{}
    The map $S^2 \times X^3 \to X^3$ given by
    \[(s,t)(x,y,z) = T_{L,s}T_{R,t}(x,y,z) = (sx,sty,tz)\]
    makes $X^3$ an $S^2$-system.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    This follows easily by the commutativity of $S$.
\end{proof}


\subsubsection{Action of $S^2$ on $X^2$}

Overloading notation, for $s, t \in S$, we define maps $T_{L,s}, T_{R,s}: X^2 \to X^2$ by
\begin{align*}
    T_{L,s}(x,y) &= (sx, sy)\\
    T_{R,t}(x,y) &= (x, ty).
\end{align*}

\begin{lemma}[Action of $S^2$ on $X^2$]
\label{lemma_S2_action_on_X2}
\lean{cornerSystem2}
\leanok{}
    The map $S^2 \times X^2 \to X^2$ given by
    \[(s,t)(x,y) = T_{L,s}T_{R,t}(x,y) = (sx,sty)\]
    makes $X^2$ an $S^2$ system.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    This follows easily by the commutativity of $S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_x3_to_x2_is_S2_factor_map}
    \lean{pi12FromCorner3ToCorner2IsFactor}
    \leanok{}
    The map $\pi_{12}: X^3 \to X^2$ is a factor map of $S^2$-systems.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_S2_action_on_X2}
\leanok{}
    By \cref{lemma_S2_action_on_X2}, both $X^3$ and $X^2$ are $S^2$-systems.  We need only show that for all $(s,t) \in S^2$, $(s,t) \pi_{12} = \pi_{12} (s,t)$.  Let $(s,t) \in S^2$ and $(x,y,z) \in X^3$.  Then
    \[(s,t) \pi_{12} (x,y,z) = (s,t)(x,y) = (sx,sty) = \pi_{12} (s,t) (x,y,z).\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem_minimal_h_from_g}
    \lean{minimalSystemImpliesMinimalcornerSystem2}
    \leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system. The $S^2$-system $X^2$ is minimal.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    We will show that every point in $X^2$ has a dense $S^2$-orbit.  Let $(x,y) \in X^2$, $U \subseteq X$, and $V \subseteq X$.  Since $X$ is a minimal $S$-system, there exists $s \in R_S(x,U)$ so that $(sx,sy) \in U \times X$.  Since $X$ is a minimal $S$-system, there exists $t \in R_S(sy,V)$ so that
    \[(s,t) (x,y) = (sx,tsy) \in U \times V.\]
\end{proof}

% \begin{lemma}
%     \label{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure}
%     Let $X$ be a $S$-system and $(x,y) \in X^2$.  The $S$-orbit closure of $(x,y)$ (under the diagonal action) is contained in the $S^2$-orbit closure of $(x,y)$.
% \end{lemma}

% \begin{proof}
%     \dgg{Sorry.}
% \end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v2}
    \lean{liftTo3Right}
    \leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a $S$-system and $x, y, z \in X$.  If $(x,y,z)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, then
    \[\overline{S (x,y)} \times \{z\} \subseteq \overline{S^2(x,y,z)}.\]
\end{lemma}

\dgg{We can strengthen this by replacing ``uniformly recurrent'' with ``recurrent,'' but we don't have an application for that, and we've not defined recurrent.}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    We will show that for all $s \in S$, $(sx,sy,z) \in \overline{S^2(x,y,z)}$.  Let $s \in S$, and let $W \subseteq X^3$ be open and contain $(sx,sy,z)$.  The map $T_{L,s}: X^3 \to X^3$ is continuous, so $T_{L,s}^{-1}W$ is an open neighborhood of $(x,y,z)$. Since $(x,y,z)$ is uniformly recurrent, there is $(a,b) \in S^2$ such that $(a,b)(x,y,z) \in T_{L,s}^{-1}W$.  Therefore, $(as,b)(x,y,z) \in W$.  Therefore, $W \cap \overline{S^2(x,y,z)} \neq \emptyset$.  Since $W$ was arbitrary, we see that $(sx,sy,z) \in \overline{S^2(x,y,z)}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3}
    \lean{liftTo3Left}
    \leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a $S$-system and $x, y, z \in X$.  If $(x,y,z)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, then
    \[\{x\} \times \overline{S (y,z)} \subseteq \overline{S^2(x,y,z)}.\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    We will show that for all $s \in S$, $(x,sy,sz) \in \overline{S^2(x,y,z)}$.  Let $s \in S$, and let $W \subseteq X^3$ be open and contain $(x,sy,sz)$.  The map $T_{R,s}: X^3 \to X^3$ is continuous, so $T_{R,s}^{-1}W$ is an open neighborhood of $(x,y,z)$. Since $(x,y,z)$ is uniformly recurrent, there is $(a,b) \in S^2$ such that $(a,b)(x,y,z) \in T_{R,s}^{-1}W$.  Therefore, $(a,bs)(x,y,z) \in W$.  Therefore, $W \cap \overline{S^2(x,y,z)} \neq \emptyset$.  Since $W$ was arbitrary, we see that $(x,sy,sz) \in \overline{S^2(x,y,z)}$.
\end{proof}

% \begin{lemma}
% \label{lemma_min_lift_right}
%     Let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system and $x, y, z \in X$.  Then
%     \[(x,y,y) \in \overline{S^2(x,z,z)}.\]
% \end{lemma}

% \begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3}
%     Since $X$ is $S$-minimal, $(y,y) \in \overline{S(z,z)}$. Apply \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3}.
% \end{proof}

\subsubsection{Action of $S^2$ on $X$}

Overloading notation again, for $s, t \in S$, we define maps $T_{L,s}, T_{R,s}: X \to X$ by
\begin{align*}
    T_{L,s}z &= z\\
    T_{R,t}z &= tz.
\end{align*}
We define an action of $S^2$ on $X$ via
\[(s,t)z = T_{L,s}T_{R,t}z = tz.\]

\begin{lemma}[Action of $S^2$ on $X$]
    \label{lemma_S2_action_on_X}
    \lean{cornerSystem1}
    \leanok{}
    The map $S^2 \times X \to X$ given by
    \[(s,t)z = T_{L,s}T_{R,t}z = tz\]
    makes $X$ an $S^2$ system.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    This follows easily by the commutativity of $S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_x3_to_x_is_S2_factor_map}
    \lean{pi3FromCorner3ToCorner1IsFactor}
    \leanok{}
    The map $\pi_3: X^3 \to X$ is a factor map of $S^2$-systems.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_S2_action_on_X}
\leanok{}
    By \cref{lemma_S2_action_on_X}, both $X^3$ and $X$ are $S^2$-systems.  We need only show that for all $(s,t) \in S^2$, $(s,t) \pi_{3} = \pi_{3} (s,t)$. Let $(s,t) \in S^2$ and $(x,y,z) \in X^3$.  Then
    \[(s,t) \pi_{3} (x,y,z) = (s,t)z = tz) = \pi_{3} (s,t) (x,y,z).\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_prox_in_h_implies_in_g}
    \lean{cornerProxImpliesProx}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup and $X$ be a minimal $S$-system. If two points $x, y \in X$ are $S^2$-proximal, then they are $S$-proximal.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok{}
    Suppose $x, y$ are $S^2$-proximal.  Let $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ be an entourage.  There exists $(s,t) \in S^2$ such that $((s,t)x,(s,t)y) \in \alpha$.  But $((s,t)x,(s,t)y) = (tx,ty) = t(x,y)$, meaning the $S$-orbit of $(x,y)$ under the diagonal action intersects $\alpha$. Since $\alpha$ was arbitrary, this shows that $x$ and $y$ are $S$-proximal.
\end{proof}


\subsubsection{Ultrafilters and the corner space}

We showed above that $X^3$, $X^2$, and $X$ are all $S^2$-systems.  By \cref{thm_ultra_semigroup_action}, we can consider the semigroup action of $\beta S^2$ on $X^3$, $X^2$, and $X$.  For $p \in \beta S^2$, we write $p(x,y,z)$, $p(x,y)$, and $px$ to denote this action.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma_ultrafilter_action_on_X3}
\lean{ultraActionOnX3}
\leanok{}
    For all $p \in \beta S^2$ and all $(x,y,z) \in X^3$,
    \begin{align}
    \label{eqn_ultrafilter_action_on_X3}
        p (x,y,z) = (p (x,y), p z).
    \end{align}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_x3_to_x2_is_S2_factor_map,lemma_x3_to_x_is_S2_factor_map,thm_ultra_semigroup_action_thru_factor}
\leanok{}
    Since $\pi_{12}: X^3 \to X^2$ and $\pi_3: X^3 \to X$ are factor maps of $S^2$-systems (\cref{lemma_x3_to_x2_is_S2_factor_map} and \cref{lemma_x3_to_x_is_S2_factor_map}), by \cref{thm_ultra_semigroup_action_thru_factor}, we get that $\pi_{12} p (x,y,z) = p \pi_{12} (x,y,z) = p(x,y)$ and $\pi_3 p (x,y,z) = p \pi_3 (x,y,z) = pz$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The $\RP$ relation and corner space dynamics}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}
\lean{xxxUniformlyRecurrent}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system and $x \in X$.  The point $(x,x,x)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lem_minimal_h_from_g,lemma_exists_minimal_left_ideal,lemma_exists_idempotent_px_is_x,lemma_ultrafilter_action_on_X3,lemma_minimal_implies_uniformly_recurrent,lemma_idempotent_implies_proximal,lemma_x3_to_x_is_S2_factor_map,lemma_prox_pair_under_factor_map_is_prox,lemma_prox_in_h_implies_in_g,theorem_proximal_and_minimal_implies_orbit_closure_contains_diagonal,lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3,lemma_in_orb_clos_of_ur_implies_ur}
    \leanok{}
Since $X$ is a minimal $S$-system, by \cref{lem_minimal_h_from_g}, $X^2$ is a minimal $S^2$-system.  By \cref{lemma_exists_minimal_left_ideal}, $S$ contains a minimal left ideal $L$. By \cref{lemma_exists_idempotent_px_is_x}, there exists a minimal, idempotent $p \in L$ such that $p(x,x) = (x,x)$.  We see by \cref{lemma_ultrafilter_action_on_X3} that $p(x,x,x) = (x,x,p x)$.

Since $p$ is minimal, by \cref{lemma_minimal_implies_uniformly_recurrent}, the point $p(x,x,x) = (x,x,px)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent.  Since $p$ is idempotent, the points $(x,x,x)$ and $(x,x,px)$ are $S^2$-proximal by \cref{lemma_idempotent_implies_proximal}.  By \cref{lemma_x3_to_x_is_S2_factor_map}, the map $\pi_3$ is a factor map of $S^2$-systems.  By \cref{lemma_prox_pair_under_factor_map_is_prox}, the points $x$ and $px$ are $S^2$-proximal.

By \cref{lemma_prox_in_h_implies_in_g}, the points $x$ and $px$ are $S$-proximal.  It follows by \cref{theorem_proximal_and_minimal_implies_orbit_closure_contains_diagonal} that $(x,x) \in \overline{S(x,px)}$. By \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3}, $(x,x,x) \in \overline{S^2(x,x,px)}$.  By \cref{lemma_in_orb_clos_of_ur_implies_ur}, all points in $\overline{S^2(x,x,px)}$ are $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, whereby $(x,x,x)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_xyy_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}
\lean{xyyUniformlyRecurrent}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system and $x, y \in X$.  The point $(x,y,y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3,lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3,lemma_in_orb_clos_of_ur_implies_ur}
\leanok{}
Since $X$ is $S$-minimal, $(y,y) \in \overline{S(x,x)}$. By \cref{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}, $(x,x,x)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, it follows by \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3} that $(x,y,y)$ belongs to $\overline{S^2(x,x,x)}$. By \cref{lemma_in_orb_clos_of_ur_implies_ur}, all points in $\overline{S^2(x,x,x)}$ are $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, whereby $(x,y,y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, as desired.
\end{proof}
%By \cref{lemma_orb_closure_of_ur_is_minimal}, $\overline{S^2(x,x,px)}$ is a minimal $S$-system, and hence, by \cref{lemma_pts_in_minimal_are_ur}, that all points of $\overline{S^2(x,x,px)}$ are $S$-uniformly recurrent.

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_xxy_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}
\lean{xxyUniformlyRecurrent}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system and $x, y \in X$.  The point $(x,x,y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3,lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v2,lemma_in_orb_clos_of_ur_implies_ur}
\leanok{}
Since $X$ is $S$-minimal, $(x,x) \in \overline{S(y,y)}$. By \cref{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}, $(y,y,y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, it follows by \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v2} that $(x,x,y)$ belongs to $\overline{S^2(y,y,y)}$. By \cref{lemma_in_orb_clos_of_ur_implies_ur}, all points in $\overline{S^2(y,y,y)}$ are $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, whereby $(x,x,y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, as desired.
\end{proof}

The proof of \cref{thm_rp_implies_same_minimal_component} below uses ultrafilters (in two ways!) to avoid nets.  The old net proof is commented out.

\dgg{I tried briefly to replace the ``backward $\RP$'' that we use in this proof with the ``forward $\RP$'' we could/should use as the main definition.  It was not clear how to do that.  So perhaps this proof is a way to sell ``backward $\RP$''}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_rp_implies_same_minimal_component}
\label{theorem_cube_space_orbit_implies_rp}
\lean{xyInRPIffxyyInyyyOrbClosure}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system and $x, y \in X$. Consider $X^3$ as an $S^2$-system as described above. The point $(x,y) \in \RP$ if and only if $(x,y,y) \in \overline{S^2(y,y,y)}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_forward_equals_backward_rp,lemma_rp_implies_ultra,lemma_exists_minimal_left_ideal,lemma_ultrafilter_surjective_action,lemma_ultrafilter_action_on_X3,thm_ultrafilter_action_with_fixed_x_is_continuous,lemma_ultrafilter_idempotent_lift,thm_ultra_point_in_orb_closure,thm_xxy_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3,lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v2,theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity,thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3,lemma_in_orb_clos_of_ur_implies_ur,lemma_idempotent_implies_proximal,lemma_prox_in_h_implies_in_g,theorem_proximal_and_minimal_implies_orbit_closure_contains_diagonal,lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3}
    By \cref{thm_forward_equals_backward_rp}, $\RP = \RP^-$, so we will prove the theorem for $\RP^-$.
    
    Suppose $(x,y) \in \RP^-$. By \cref{lemma_rp_implies_ultra}, there exists $w \in X$ and an ultrafilter $\family$ on $X \times X \times S$ such that the image of $\family$ under the map $\varphi_1: (a,b,s) \mapsto (a,b,sa,sb)$ \dgg{$\tilde \varphi_1: (a,b,s) \mapsto (sa,sb,a,b)$} converges to $(w,w,x,y)$:
    \[\varphi_1 \family \to (w,w,x,y).\]
    \dgg{$\tilde\varphi_1 \family \to (w,w,a,b)$}

    By \cref{lemma_exists_minimal_left_ideal}, $\beta S^2$ contains a minimal left ideal $L$.  Since $X^2$ is a minimal $S^2$-system, by \cref{lemma_ultrafilter_surjective_action}, for $(a,b,s) \in X \times X \times S$, there exists $p_{(a,b)} \in L$ such that $p_{(a,b)}(w,w) = (a,b)$ \dgg{$p_{(a,b)}(a,b) = (w,w)$}.  Note that by \cref{lemma_ultrafilter_action_on_X3}, $p_{(a,b)} (w,w,y) = (a, b, p_{(a,b)} y)$ \dgg{$p_{(a,b)} (a,b,y) = (w, w, p_{(a,b)} y)$}. Thus we get a map $\varphi_2: X \times X \times S \to \beta S^2$ with image in $L$. Now $\varphi_2 \family$ is an ultrafilter on $\beta S^2$ and hence converges to some $p$:
    \[\varphi_2 \family \to p.\]
    Since $L$ is closed and $\varphi_2$ has image in $L$, $p \in L$.
    
    By \cref{thm_ultrafilter_action_with_fixed_x_is_continuous}, the map $\varphi_3: \beta S^2 \to X^2$ given by $q \mapsto q(w,w)$ \dgg{$q \mapsto q(a,b)$} is continuous. Consider the composition $\varphi_3 \circ \varphi_2: X^2 \times S \to X^2$.  On the one hand, by the definitions, $\varphi_3 \circ \varphi_2 = \pi_{12} = \pi_{12} \circ \varphi_1$. We see from the fact that
    \[\varphi_1 \family \to (w,w,x,y)\]
    and the fact that $\pi_{12}$ is continuous that
    \[(\varphi_3 \circ \varphi_2) \family = (\pi_{12} \circ \varphi_1) \family \to \pi_{12} (w,w,x,y) = (w,w).\]
    \dgg{$\varphi_3 \circ \varphi_2$ is constant $(w,w)$}
    On the other hand, $\varphi_3$ is continuous, so
    \[(\varphi_3 \circ \varphi_2) \family \to \varphi_3 (\text{limit of } \varphi_2 \family).\]
    Since $\varphi_2 \family \to p$, we see that
    \[(\varphi_3 \circ \varphi_2) \family \to \varphi_3 p = p(w,w).\]
    Therefore $p(w,w) = (w,w)$.

    By \cref{lemma_ultrafilter_idempotent_lift}, there exists $q \in L$ such that $q (w,w) = (w,w)$ and $pq$ is idempotent. Consider the map $\varphi_4: X^2 \times S \to X^3$ given by
    \[(a,b,s) \mapsto (sa,sb,p_{(a,b)}qy).\]
    We make two claims.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Claim 1: $\varphi_4 \family$ converges to $(x,y,pqy)$.  This is easy since the first two coordinates converge to $(x,y)$ by $\varphi_1 \family \to (w,w,x,y)$ and the third coordinate converges to $pqy$ by the continuity of $r \mapsto rqy$ and the fact that $\varphi_2 \family \to p$.
        
        \item Claim 2: the image of $\varphi_4$ is contained in $\overline{S^2 (w,w,y)}$.  Indeed, let $(a,b,s) \in X^2 \times S$, and note that
        \[\varphi_4 (a,b,s) = (sa,sb,p_{(a,b)} q y) = T_{L,s} p_{(a,b)} q (w,w,y).\]
        This follows since $q(w,w) = (w,w)$ and $p_{(a,b)}(w,w) = (a,b)$.
        
        Since $p_{(a,b)}q \in L \subseteq \beta S^2$, we have by \cref{thm_ultra_point_in_orb_closure} that
    \[p_{(a,b)} q (w,w,y) \in \overline{S^2 (w,w,y)}.\]
    Applying $T_{L,s}$, we see
    \[\varphi_4 (a,b,s) = T_{L,s} p_{(a,b)} q (w,w,y) \in T_{L,s} \overline{S^2 (w,w,y)} \subseteq \overline{S^2 (w,w,y)},\]
    as was to be shown.
    \end{itemize}
% \[(sa,sb,p_{(a,b)}qy) = T_{L,s} p_{(a,b)} q (w,w,y).\]
%      Thus, it suffices to show that $T_{L,s} p_{(a,b)} q (w,w,y) \in \overline{S^2 (w,w,y)}$. 
%     Since $(w,w,y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent (by \cref{thm_xxy_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}) and $(sa, sb) \in \overline{S(a,b)}$, we have by \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v2} that $(sa, sb, p_{(a,b)} q y) \in \overline{S^2 (a,b,p_{(a,b)}qy)}$.  By transitivity (\cref{theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity}), we get that
%     \[(sa, sb, p_{(a,b)} q y) \in \overline{S^2 (w,w,y)}.\]
    Combining the claims with the fact that $\overline{S^2 (w,w,y)}$ is closed, we get that $(x,y,pqy) \in \overline{S^2 (w,w,y)}$.
    
    Since $X$ is a minimal $S$-system, by the definition of minimal and the diagonal action, $(w,w) \in \overline{S(y,y)}$.  Since $(y,y,y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent (\cref{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}), by \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v2}, we have that $(w,w,y) \in \overline{S^2(y,y,y)}$. By transitivity (\cref{theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity}), we get that
    \[(x, y, pq y) \in \overline{S^2(y,y,y)}.\]
    Note that since $(y,y,y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent (\cref{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}), by \cref{lemma_in_orb_clos_of_ur_implies_ur}, we have that $(x,y, pq y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent.
    
    Since $pq \in L \subseteq \beta S^2$ is idempotent, by \cref{lemma_idempotent_implies_proximal}, the point $pq y$ is $S^2$-proximal to $y$. By \cref{lemma_prox_in_h_implies_in_g}, the points $pq y$ and $y$ are $S$-proximal. It follows by \cref{theorem_proximal_and_minimal_implies_orbit_closure_contains_diagonal} that $(y,y) \in \overline{S(y,pqy)}$. Since $(x,y, pq y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent, by \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3}, $(x, y, y) \in \overline{S^2 (x, y, pq y)}$. By transitivity (\cref{theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity}), we have that
    \[(x, y, y) \in \overline{S^2 (y,y,y)},\]
    as was to be shown.\\

    Conversely, suppose $(x,y,y) \in \overline{S^2 (y,y,y)}$. To show that $(x,y) \in \RP$, we will show that for all entourages $\alpha \subseteq X^2$, all open $W \subseteq X^2$ containing $(x,y)$, $W \cap S \alpha \neq \emptyset$.
    
    Let $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ be an entourage and let $W \ni (x,y)$. Consider the neighborhood $W \times \alpha[y]$ of the point $(x,y,y)$.  \dgg{Check definition of $\alpha[y]$} By assumption, $(x,y,y) \in \overline{S^2 (y,y,y)}$, so there exists $(s,t) \in S^2$ so that $(s,t)(y,y,y) = (sy,sty,ty) \in W \times \alpha[y]$. That is: $(y,ty) \in \alpha$ and $s(y,ty) = (sy,sty) \in W$.  Therefore, $W \cap S \alpha \neq \emptyset$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\subsection{RP is an equivalence relation}

\begin{theorem}
\label{theorem_rp_is_transitive}
\lean{minimalImpliesRPisTrans}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, and let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system.  The $\RP$ relation is transitive.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_rp_implies_same_minimal_component,thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3,lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3,theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity,theorem_cube_space_orbit_implies_rp}
\leanok{}
    Let $(x,y), (y,z) \in \RP$.  Since $(x,y) \in \RP$, by \cref{thm_rp_implies_same_minimal_component}, we have that $(x,y,y) \in \overline{S^2(y,y,y)}$.  By minimality, $(z,z) \in \overline{S(y,y)}$.  Since $(x,y,y)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent (\cref{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}), by \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3}, $(x,z,z) \in \overline{S^2(x,y,y)}$.  Since $(y,z) \in \RP$, by \cref{thm_rp_implies_same_minimal_component}, we have that $(y,z,z) \in \overline{S^2(z,z,z)}$.  By minimality, $(y,y) \in \overline{S(z,z)}$.  Since $(y,z,z)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent (\cref{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}), by \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3}, $(y,y,y) \in \overline{S^2(y,z,z)}$.
    
    Recapping the conclusions from the previous paragraph:
    \begin{align*}
        (y,z,z) \in \overline{S^2(z,z,z)}\\
        (y,y,y) \in \overline{S^2(y,z,z)}\\
        (x,y,y) \in \overline{S^2(y,y,y)}\\
        (x,z,z) \in \overline{S^2(x,y,y)}\\
    \end{align*}
    By transitivity (\cref{theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity}), we see that
    \[(x,z,z) \in \overline{S^2(z,z,z)}.\]
    It follows by \cref{theorem_cube_space_orbit_implies_rp} that $(x,z) \in \RP$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{theorem_rp_is_ICER}
\lean{RPisICER}
\leanok{}
    Let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system.  The $\RP$ relation is an ICER.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_rp_basics_symmetric,lemma_rp_basics_invariant,lemma_rp_basics_closed,lemma_rp_reflexive,theorem_rp_is_transitive}
\leanok{}
    Combine \cref{lemma_rp_basics_symmetric}, \cref{lemma_rp_basics_invariant}, \cref{lemma_rp_basics_closed}, \cref{lemma_rp_reflexive}, and \cref{theorem_rp_is_transitive}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_rp_equals_s}
\lean{RPisEquiStructureRelation}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be a minimal $S$-system.  The equicontinuous structure relation $\seq$ is equal to $\RP$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{theorem_rp_is_ICER,lemma_rp_characterization_of_equicont_icer,thm_equi_structure_relation_is_equi_ICER}
\leanok{}
    By \cref{theorem_rp_is_ICER}, $\RP$ is an ICER.  It follows from \cref{lemma_rp_characterization_of_equicont_icer} that $\RP$ is an equicontinuous ICER so that by the definition of $\seq$, $\seq \subseteq \RP$.  Since $\seq$ is an equicontinuous ICER (\cref{thm_equi_structure_relation_is_equi_ICER}), it follows from \cref{lemma_rp_characterization_of_equicont_icer} that $\RP \subseteq \seq$.  Therefore, $\RP = \seq$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A set recurrence corollary}

Here is another application of the material in this section. The theorem below is used later to build $\Delta$ sets.  A version of this theorem for abelian group actions can be found already in Veech \cite{veech_1968}.

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_delta_set_builder_prestep}
\lean{xyInRPImpliesSyndeticVisitTimeIntersection}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system, and let $x, y \in X$.  If $(x,y) \in \RP$, then for all non-empty, open $U, V \subseteq X$ with $y \in V$, we have that
    \[R(x,U) \cap R(V,U)\]
    is syndetic. The same statement holds with $\RP$ replaced by $\RP^-$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_rp_basics_symmetric,thm_rp_implies_same_minimal_component,thm_xyy_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3,lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3,theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity,thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3,lemma_return_time_algebra_three,lemma_image_of_syndetic}
\leanok{}
    Let $U, V \subseteq X$ be non-empty, open with $y \in V$.  Let $z \in U$.  By \cref{lemma_rp_basics_symmetric}, $\RP$ is symmetric, so $(y,x) \in \RP$.  By \cref{thm_rp_implies_same_minimal_component}, $(y,x,x) \in \overline{S^2 (x,x,x)}$. By minimality, $(z,z) \in \overline{S(x,x)}$.  Since $(y,x,x)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent (\cref{thm_xyy_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}), by \cref{lemma_S_orb_closure_contained_in_S2_orbit_closure_v3}, $(y,z,z) \in \overline{S^2(y,x,x)}$.  By transitivity (\cref{theorem_orbit_closure_transitivity}), $(y,z,z) \in \overline{S^2 (x,x,x)}$.
    
    By \cref{thm_xxx_is_trtl_unif_rec_v3}, the point $(x,x,x)$ is $S^2$-uniformly recurrent.  Since $V \times U \times U$ is an open neighborhood of $(y,z,z)$ and $(y,z,z) \in \overline{S^2 (x,x,x)}$, we get that $R_{S^2}((x,x,x), V \times U \times U)$ is syndetic. This is the set of pairs $(g,r) \in S^2$ such that $T_{L,g} T_{R,r} (x,x,x) \in V \times U \times U$ -- that is, such that $r \in R(x,U)$, $g \in R(x,V)$, $rg \in R(x,U)$.  For each such pair $(g,r) \in S^2$, we see by \cref{lemma_return_time_algebra_three} that
    \[r \in R(x,U) \cap (R(x,U) R(x,V)^{-1}) = R(x,U) \cap R(V,U).\]
    Applying $\pi_2: S^2 \to S$, a surjective semigroup homomorphism, to this set of pairs and using \cref{lemma_image_of_syndetic}, we see that the set $R(x,U) \cap R(V,U)$ is syndetic, as desired.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A strong invariance corollary}

The assertion that $\RP \subseteq S^{-1} \RP$
is equivalent to the assertion that $\RP$ is $S$-invariant, which was shown in \cref{lemma_frp_basics_invariant} to hold for any $S$-system under the assumption that $S$ is commutative.  In \cref{thm_rp_strong_s_inv} below, we show that $\RP = S^{-1} \RP$ under the additional assumption of minimality.  This turns out to be an important ingredient in our proof of \cref{mainthm_kronecker_is_same_as_extension_kronecker}.  We derive \cref{thm_rp_strong_s_inv} as a corollary of \cref{thm_rp_equals_s} and the following analogue for the equicontinuous structure relation.  

\begin{lemma}
\label{thm_seq_strong_s_inv}
\lean{equiStructureRelationIsStrongSInvariant}
\leanok{}
    If $S$ is a commutative semigroup and $X$ is a minimal $S$-system, then
    \[\seq = S^{-1} \seq.\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_equi_implies_distal,lemma_distal_implies_homeo}
    The relation $\seq$ is $S$-invariant by its definition since an arbitrary intersection of $S$-invariant sets is $S$-invariant.  Therefore,
    \[\seq \subseteq S^{-1}\seq.\]
    
    To see the reverse inclusion, suppose $(x,y) \not\in \seq$ so that there exists an equicontinuous factor $\pi: X \to Y$ for which $\pi x \neq \pi y$.  We must show that for all $s \in S$, $s(x,y) \not\in \seq$.
    
    Let $s \in S$. Combining \cref{lemma_equi_implies_distal} and \cref{lemma_distal_implies_homeo}, the map $s: Y \to Y$ is a homeomorphism, so $s \pi x \neq s \pi y$.  Therefore, $\pi s x \neq \pi s y$.  Since $\pi: X \to Y$ is an equicontinuous factor of $X$, we have that $(sx, sy) \neq \seq$, as desired. 
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_rp_strong_s_inv}
\lean{RPIsStrongSInvariant}
\leanok{}
    If $S$ is a commutative semigroup and $X$ is a minimal $S$-system, then
    \[\RP = S^{-1} \RP.\]
    The same statement holds with $\RP$ replaced by $\RP^-$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_rp_equals_s,thm_seq_strong_s_inv}
    By \cref{thm_rp_equals_s}, we have $\RP = \seq$. By \cref{thm_seq_strong_s_inv}, we have $\seq = S^{-1} \seq$. The conclusion follows immediately.
\end{proof}

\section{Delta star sets are locally Bohr}
\label{sec_delta_star_locally_bohr}

Needs: \cref{sec_RP,sec_furstenberg_families}

Contains:
\begin{itemize}
    \item This is the main application: $\syndetic \classcapdual \dcthickbohr = \syndetic \classcapdual \Delta$
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Reduction to dynamically syndetic sets}

% \dgg{I've opted to replace ``uniformly recurrent set'' with the clunkier ``CRT'' (see definition below) since that will shorten the verification by cutting out a step (namely, we won't have to prove that $B$ is CRT implies $1_B$ uniformly recurrent).}

% \begin{definition}
% \lean{isCRTSet}
% \leanok{}
%     Let $S$ be a semigroup. A set $A \subseteq S$ is called a \emph{clopen return-time set of a uniformly recurrent point (CRT)} if there exists an $S$-system $X$, an $S$-uniformly recurrent point $x \in X$, and a clopen set $U \subseteq X$ such that $A = R(x,U)$.
% \end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \label{def_ur_set}
    \lean{isURSet}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup.  A set $A \subseteq S$ is a UR set if $1_A \in \{0,1\}^S$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Symbolic system]
\label{def_symbolic_system}
\lean{selfSymbolicSystem}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup.  The symbolic system on $S$ is set $X = \{0,1\}^S$ with the product topology (so that it is compact and Hausdorff) together with the action of $S$ on $X$ given for $s \in S$ and $\omega \in X$ by
    \[\forall \ t \in S, \ (s \omega)(t) = \omega(ts).\]
\end{definition}

\dgg{I had to switch the definition of the shift in the symbolic system.  The original definition yielded an anti-action, oops!  But this allowed me to remove the ``commutative'' assumption in a couple of lemmas below.  Proceed with caution.}

Action check: $s_1(s_2w) = (s_1s_2)w$?
\[(s_1(s_2w))(t) = (s_2w)(ts_1) = w(ts_1s_2) = (s_1s_2 w)(t).\]

\begin{definition}[Cylinder set]
\label{def_cylinder_set}
\lean{cylinderSet}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a set, $s \in S$, and $a \in \{0,1\}$. The cylinder set $[a]_s$ is defined as
    \[\{\omega \in \{0,1\}^{S} : \omega(s) = a\}.\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Monoid extension]
    For any semigroup $S$, there exists a \emph{monoid extension} $M_S$.  In lean: \verb|WithOne.instMonoid|.  Define the $S$-action on $M_S$ by for $s \in S$ and $m \in M_S$ by $s(m) = m * s$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_unif_rec_implies_UR}
    \lean{urSetIsRxU}
    \leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup, and let $A \subseteq S$.  If $A$ is an UR set, then there exists $x \in \{0, 1\}^{M_S}$ which is $S$-uniformly recurrent and a clopen set $U \subseteq \{0,1\}^{M_S}$ such that $A = R(x,U)$.
\end{lemma}
