\begin{lemma}
\label{lem:auslander-ellis}
\lean{syndSetIsUROnThickSet}
\leanok{}
Let $S$ be a semigroup.  Let $A \subseteq S$ be a syndetic set and $H \subseteq S$ be a thick set. There exists an UR set $B \subseteq S$ and a thick set $H' \subseteq H$ such that $A \cap H' = B \cap H'$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_thick_closure_contains_strong_ultrafilter,lemma_minimal_implies_uniformly_recurrent,lemma_unif_rec_implies_CRT,lemma_plim_in_open_set_return_time}
    Since $H$ is thick, by \cref{lemma_thick_closure_contains_strong_ultrafilter}, there exists a minimal, idempotent ultrafilter $p \in \overline{H} \subseteq \beta S$ such that for all finite $F \subseteq S$, $\cap_{f \in F} f^{-1} H \in p$. Let $1_B = p 1_{A \cap H}$.  It follows from \cref{lemma_minimal_implies_uniformly_recurrent} that $1_B$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent.
    %By \cref{lemma_unif_rec_implies_CRT}, $B$ is a CRT set.
    Define $T = \{g \in S \mid 1_B(g) = 1_{A \cap H}(g) \}$.
    
    We claim that the set $H \cap T$ is thick.  Indeed, let $F \subseteq S$ be finite. The set
    \[U \defeq \{(\alpha, \beta) \in (\{0,1\}^S)^2 \mid \forall f \in F, \ \alpha(f) = \beta (f)\} \subseteq (\{0,1\}^S)^2\]
    is, by the definition of the product topology on $\{0,1\}^S$, an open set containing the diagonal.  Since $p = pp$, we see
    \[p 1_B = p p 1_{A \cap H} = p 1_{A \cap H},\]
    we see by \cref{lemma_plim_in_open_set_return_time} that $R_S((1_B,1_{A \cap H}), U) \in p$ in the product space $(\{0,1\}^S)^2$ under the diagonal action.  By the property of $p$, we see that $\bigcap_{f \in F} f^{-1} H \in p$.  Since $p$ is a filter,
    \[\bigcap_{f \in F} f^{-1} H \cap R_S((1_B,1_{A \cap H}), U) \in p.\]
    Choose $g$ in this set.  We see for all $f \in F$, $fg \in H$ and
    \[1_B(fg) = (g1_B)(f) = (g1_{A \cap H})(f) = 1_{A \cap H}(fg).\]
    Therefore, $Fg \subseteq H \cap T$, as desired.
    
    Set $H' = H \cap T$.  Then $H' \subseteq H$ is thick and $1_B$ and $1_{A \cap H}$ agree on $H'$, that is, $A \cap H' = B \cap H'$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_lspwf_upgrade}
\lean{urContainmentSufficesForFamilyContainment}
    Let $S$ be a semigroup. Let $\family$ and $\familytwo$ be families of subsets of $S$. Suppose that for all UR set $B \subseteq S$ and all thick $H \subseteq S$, if $B \cap H \in \family$, then $B \cap H \in \familytwo$.  Then we have
    \[\syndetic \classcapdual \family \subseteq \syndetic \classcapdual \familytwo.\]
\end{theorem}

% \dgg{Note: I removed the assumption that $\family \neq \mathcal{P}(S)$, since by definition, if $\family = \mathcal{P}(S)$, then $S \classcapdual \family = S$.}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_classcapdual_contained_in_intersection,lem:auslander-ellis}
\leanok{}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Let $A \in \syndetic \classcapdual \family$ and $H \subseteq S$ be a thick set. Our goal is to show that $A \cap H \in \familytwo$.

    \item By \cref{thm_classcapdual_contained_in_intersection}, $\syndetic \classcapdual \family \subseteq \syndetic$, so $A \in \syndetic$.

    \item By \cref{lem:auslander-ellis}, there exists a thick set $H' \subseteq H$ and UR set $B$ such that $A \cap H' = B \cap H'$.

    \item Since $A \in \syndetic \classcapdual \family$ and $H' \in \thick$, we have $A \cap H' \in \family$ (this holds by the definition of $\classcapdual$, even if $\family = \mathcal{P}(S)$), and so $B \cap H' \in \family$. 

    \item Since $B$ is UR set, by our assumption, $B \cap H' \in \familytwo$.

    \item Thus $A \cap H' \in \familytwo$.

    \item Since $H' \subseteq H$, we deduce that $A \cap H \in \familytwo$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\subsection{A delta set builder}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_delta_from_kronecker}
\lean{commVisitTimeSetForRPPairIsDelta}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system, $(x, y) \in \RP$, and $V \subseteq X$ an open neighborhood of $y$.  For all thick $H \subseteq S$, the set $R(x,V) \cap H \in \Delta$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_delta_set_builder_prestep,lemma_synd_thick_dual,lemma_minimal_comm_action_implies_surjective,lemma_return_time_algebra_one,lemma_return_to_intersection,lemma_preimage_intersection_of_thick_is_thick}
    \dgg{I swapped $H$'s for $B$'s in this proof for some reason.}
    Let $B \subseteq S$ be thick. Put $U_1 = V$ and $B_1 = B$.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \item[Step 1]
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item By \cref{thm_delta_set_builder_prestep}, the set $R(x,U_1) \cap R(V,U_1)$ is syndetic.  By assumption, the set $B_1$ is thick. By \cref{lemma_synd_thick_dual}, the intersection of a thick set and a syndetic set is non-empty.  Therefore, there exists
            \[t_1 \in R(x,U_1) \cap R(V,U_1) \cap B_1.\]
            
            \item Choose $s_1 \in S$ arbitrarily. Since $t_1 \in R(x,V) \cap B$, we see that
            \begin{align}
                \label{eqn_delta_it_one}
                s_2 \defeq s_1t_1 \in s_1 (R(x,V) \cap B).
            \end{align}

            \item Since $t_1 \in R(V,V)$, $V \cap t_1^{-1} V \neq \emptyset$.  Since $s_1$ acts surjectively (\cref{lemma_minimal_comm_action_implies_surjective}),
            \[U_2 \defeq s_1^{-1} V \cap s_2^{-1} V = s_1^{-1} (V \cap t_1^{-1} V) \neq \emptyset.\]
            Note that by \cref{lemma_return_time_algebra_one} and \cref{lemma_return_to_intersection},
            \[R(x,U_2) = s_1^{-1} R(x,V) \cap s_2^{-1} R(x,V).\]
            Put
            \[B_2 \defeq s_1^{-1} B \cap s_2^{-1} B.\]
        \end{enumerate}

        \item[Step 2]
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item By \cref{thm_delta_set_builder_prestep}, the set $R(x,U_2) \cap R(V,U_2)$ is syndetic.  By \cref{lemma_preimage_intersection_of_thick_is_thick}, the set $B_2$ is thick. By \cref{lemma_synd_thick_dual}, the intersection of a thick set and a syndetic set is non-empty.  Therefore, there exists
            \[t_2 \in R(x,U_2) \cap R(V,U_2) \cap B_2.\]

            \item By \cref{lemma_return_time_algebra_one} and \cref{lemma_return_to_intersection} and the definition of $B_2$,
            \[t_2 \in R(x,U_2) \cap B_2 = s_1^{-1} R(x,V) \cap s_1^{-1} B \cap s_2^{-1} R(x,V) \cap s_2^{-1} B.\]
            Therefore, $s_1 t_2, s_2 t_2 \in R(x,V) \cap B$.  Therefore
            \begin{align}
                \label{eqn_delta_it_two}
                s_3 \defeq s_1s_2t_2 \in s_1 (R(x,V) \cap B) \cap s_2 (R(x,V) \cap B).
            \end{align}

            \item Since $t_2 \in R(V,U_2)$, $V \cap t_2^{-1} U_2 \neq \emptyset$.  Since $s_1s_2$ acts surjectively (\cref{lemma_minimal_comm_action_implies_surjective}),
            \begin{align*}
                U_3 &\defeq (s_1s_2)^{-1} V \cap (s_1 s_3)^{-1} V \cap (s_2 s_3)^{-1} V \\
                &= (s_1s_2)^{-1} \big( V \cap (s_1 t_2)^{-1} V \cap (s_2 t_2)^{-1} V \big)\\
                &= (s_1s_2)^{-1} \big( V \cap t_2^{-1} U_2\big) \neq \emptyset.
            \end{align*}
            Note that by \cref{lemma_return_time_algebra_one} and \cref{lemma_return_to_intersection},
            \[R(x,U_3) = (s_1s_2)^{-1} R(x,V) \cap (s_1s_3)^{-1} R(x,V) \cap (s_2s_3)^{-1}R(x,V).\]
            Put
            \[B_3 \defeq (s_1s_2)^{-1} B \cap (s_1s_3)^{-1} B \cap (s_2s_3)^{-1} B.\]
        \end{enumerate}

        \item[Step 3]
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item By \cref{thm_delta_set_builder_prestep}, the set $R(x,U_3) \cap R(V,U_3)$ is syndetic.  By \cref{lemma_preimage_intersection_of_thick_is_thick}, the set $B_3$ is thick. By \cref{lemma_synd_thick_dual}, the intersection of a thick set and a syndetic set is non-empty.  Therefore, there exists
            \[t_3 \in R(x,U_3) \cap R(V,U_3) \cap B_3.\]

            \item By \cref{lemma_return_time_algebra_one} and \cref{lemma_return_to_intersection} and the definition of $B_3$,
            \begin{align*}
                t_3 &\in R(x,U_3) \cap B_3 \\
                &= (s_1s_2)^{-1} (R(x,V) \cap B) \cap (s_1s_3)^{-1} (R(x,V) \cap B) \cap (s_2s_3)^{-1}(R(x,V) \cap B).
            \end{align*}
            Therefore, $s_1 s_2 t_3, s_1 s_3 t_3, s_2 s_3 t_3 \in R(x,V) \cap B$.  Therefore
            \begin{align}
                \label{eqn_delta_it_three}
                s_4 \defeq s_1s_2 s_3t_3  \in s_1 (R(x,V) \cap B) \cap s_2 (R(x,V) \cap B) \cap s_3 (R(x,V) \cap B).
            \end{align}

            \item Since $t_3 \in R(V,U_3)$, $V \cap t_3^{-1} U_3 \neq \emptyset$.  Since $s_1s_2s_3$ acts surjectively (\cref{lemma_minimal_comm_action_implies_surjective}),
            \begin{align*}
                U_4 &\defeq (s_1s_2s_3)^{-1} V \cap (s_1 s_2 s_4)^{-1} V \cap (s_1 s_3 s_4)^{-1} V \cap (s_2s_3s_4)^{-1} V \\
                &= (s_1s_2s_3)^{-1} \big( V \cap (s_1 s_2t_3)^{-1} V \cap (s_1 s_3 t_3)^{-1} V \cap (s_2s_3 t_3)^{-1}V \big)\\
                &= (s_1s_2s_3)^{-1} \big( V \cap t_3^{-1} U_3\big) \neq \emptyset.
            \end{align*}
            Note that by \cref{lemma_return_time_algebra_one} and \cref{lemma_return_to_intersection},
            \[R(x,U_4) = (s_1s_2s_3)^{-1} R(x,V) \cap (s_1s_2s_4)^{-1} R(x,V) \cap (s_1s_3s_4)^{-1}R(x,V) \cap (s_2s_3s_4)^{-1}R(x,V).\]
            Put
            \[B_4 \defeq (s_1s_2s_3)^{-1} B \cap (s_1s_2s_4)^{-1} B \cap (s_1s_3s_4)^{-1} B \cap (s_2s_3s_4)^{-1} B.\]
        \end{enumerate}
    \end{enumerate}
    
    Iterate ad infinitum: we get $s_1, s_2, \dots$ such that for all $m < n$, $s_n \in s_m (R(x,V) \cap B)$, as was to be shown.  The first instances of this are shown in \eqref{eqn_delta_it_one}, \eqref{eqn_delta_it_two}, and \eqref{eqn_delta_it_three}.
\end{proof}
%This comment shows how to get $s_1$ in R(x,V)
% By \cref{thm_delta_set_builder_prestep}, the set $R(x,V) \cap R(V,V)$ is syndetic.  By assumption, the set $B$ is thick. By \cref{lemma_synd_thick_dual}, the intersection of a thick set and a syndetic set is non-empty.  Therefore, there exists
% \[t_1 \in R(x,V) \cap R(V,V) \cap B.\]
% By \cref{lemma_return_time_algebra_three},
% \[t_1 \in R(V,V) = R(x,V)R(x,V)^{-1},\]
% so there exists $s_1 \in R(x,V)$ such that $s_2 \defeq s_1 t_1 \in R(x,V)$.

\subsection{Dynamical sets of Bohr recurrence are Delta}

A version of the following theorem can be found (for $\Z$-actions, or more generally?) in \cite{huang_shao_ye_2016}.

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_dcthickbohr_is_delta_for_rxu}
\leanok{}
    \lean{commURSetsOfBohrRecurrenceAreDelta}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, and let $B \subseteq S$ be an UR set.  If $B \in \dcthickbohr$, then for all thick $H \subseteq S$, the set $B \cap H \in \Delta$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_pr_family_concentration,lemma_dcthickbohr_is_pr,lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_dcthickbohr_dual,thm_rp_equals_s,thm_delta_from_kronecker}
    Since $B$ is such that $1_B$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent, there exists $x \in \{0,1\}^{M_S}$, and $U \subseteq \{0,1\}^{M_S}$ clopen so that $B = R(x,U)$.  Assume $B \in \dcthickbohr$.

    Since $R(x,U) \in \dcthickbohr$, by \cref{lemma_pr_family_concentration}, \cref{lemma_dcthickbohr_is_pr}, and the fact that $U$ is closed, there exists $y \in U$ such that for all non-empty, open $U' \ni y$, the set $R(x,U') \in \dcthickbohr$.
    
    Let $\pi: X \to K$ be any equicontinuous factor of $X$.  We will show that $\pi x = \pi y$ by showing that for all open neighborhoods $V$ of $\pi x$ and $W$ of $\pi y$, $V \cap W \neq \emptyset$.  Let $V$ and $W$ be open neighborhoods of $\pi x$ and $\pi y$ respectively.  Since $K$ is equicontinuous and $V \ni \pi x$, $R(\pi x, V) \in \dcsyndeticbohr$. Since $\pi^{-1} W \ni y$ and $R(\pi x, V) \in \dcsyndeticbohr$, we have by \cref{lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_dcthickbohr_dual} that $R(x,\pi^{-1} W) \cap R(\pi x, V) \neq \emptyset$.  Therefore, there exists $s \in S$ such that $sx \in \pi^{-1} W$ and $s \pi x \in V$.  Thus, $\pi s x = s \pi x \in W$ and $s \pi x \in V$, so $V \cap W \neq \emptyset$, as desired.

    We conclude by showing that for all thick $H \subseteq S$, the set $R(x,U) \cap H \in \Delta$. Let $\pi: X \to K$ be the maximal equicontinuous factor of $X$.  We showed in the previous paragraph that $y \in U$ is such that $\pi x = \pi y$.  Therefore, $(x,y) \in \seq$.  By \cref{thm_rp_equals_s}, $\seq = \RP$, so $(x,y) \in \RP$.  Therefore, by \cref{thm_delta_from_kronecker}, $R(x,U) \cap H \in \Delta$.
\end{proof}

% \dgg{The more robust version of \cref{thm_dcthickbohr_is_delta_for_rxu} can be found in the older version of this section at the bottom of this file.}

\subsection{Proof of \cref{thm_bfw_dual_abelian} and some corollaries}

If we only care about the combinatorics, the following is a weaker, but purely combinatorial and still highly-nontrivial-looking consequence:
\[\syndetic \classcapdual \Delta = \syndetic \classcapdual \Delta_0.\]

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_bfw_dual_abelian}
\lean{commSyndFamMeetDeltaIsSnydFamMeetSetOfBohrRecurrence}
    In a commutative semigroup $S$,
    \[\syndetic \classcapdual \Delta = \syndetic \classcapdual \dcthickbohr.\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_delta_in_deltazero,thm_delta_zero_set_of_bohr_rec,thm_classcapdual_is_monotonic,thm_lspwf_upgrade,thm_classcapdual_contained_in_intersection,thm_dcthickbohr_is_delta_for_rxu}
\leanok{}
    That
    \[\syndetic \classcapdual \Delta \subseteq \syndetic \classcapdual \Delta_0 \subseteq \syndetic \classcapdual \dcthickbohr\]
    follows from $\Delta \subseteq \Delta_0$ (\cref{thm_delta_in_deltazero}), $\Delta_0 \subseteq \dcthickbohr$ (\cref{thm_delta_zero_set_of_bohr_rec}), and the monotonicity of $\classcapdual$ (\cref{thm_classcapdual_is_monotonic}). So we have only left to show that
    \[\syndetic \classcapdual \dcthickbohr \subseteq \syndetic \classcapdual \Delta.\]
    According to \cref{thm_lspwf_upgrade}, it suffices to show this inclusion for sets whose indicators are $S$-uniformly recurrent.  Let $B \in \syndetic \classcapdual \dcthickbohr$ be such that $1_B$ is $S$-uniformly recurrent.  From \cref{thm_classcapdual_contained_in_intersection}, we have that
    \[\syndetic \classcapdual \dcthickbohr \subseteq \dcthickbohr,\]
    whence $B \in \dcthickbohr$.  We have from \cref{thm_dcthickbohr_is_delta_for_rxu} that since $B \in \dcthickbohr$, $B \in \syndetic \classcapdual \Delta$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_bfw_dual_abelian_v2}
\lean{commSyndFamMeetDeltaIsSnydFamMeetDeltaZero}
    In a commutative semigroup $S$,
    \[\syndetic \classcapdual \Delta = \syndetic \classcapdual \Delta_0.\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_delta_in_deltazero,thm_delta_zero_set_of_bohr_rec,thm_classcapdual_is_monotonic,thm_bfw_dual_abelian}
\leanok{}
    That
    \[\syndetic \classcapdual \Delta \subseteq \syndetic \classcapdual \Delta_0 \subseteq \syndetic \classcapdual \dcthickbohr\]
    follows from $\Delta \subseteq \Delta_0$ (\cref{thm_delta_in_deltazero}), $\Delta_0 \subseteq \dcthickbohr$ (\cref{thm_delta_zero_set_of_bohr_rec}), and the monotonicity of $\classcapdual$ (\cref{thm_classcapdual_is_monotonic}).  By \cref{thm_bfw_dual_abelian}, $\syndetic \classcapdual \Delta = \syndetic \classcapdual \dcthickbohr$, so $\syndetic \classcapdual \Delta = \syndetic \classcapdual \Delta_0$, as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_delta_star_locally_bohr}
\lean{commDeltaStarImpliesLocallyBohrZero}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup. Delta star sets are locally Bohr.  Actually:
    \[\Delta^* \subseteq \syndetic \classcapdual (\thick \classcap \dcsyndeticbohr).\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_bfw_dual_abelian,lemma_synd_thick_dual,lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_dcthickbohr_dual,thm_combo_algebra_statement_one}
\leanok{}
    From \cref{thm_bfw_dual_abelian}, we have that
    \[\syndetic \classcapdual \dcthickbohr \subseteq \syndetic \classcapdual \Delta.\]
    Since $\syndetic^* = \thick$ (\cref{lemma_synd_thick_dual}) and $\dcthickbohr^* = \dcsyndeticbohr$ (\cref{lemma_dcsyndeticbohr_dcthickbohr_dual}), we have the conclusion from \cref{thm_combo_algebra_statement_one}.
\end{proof}

\section{From semigroup actions to group actions}
\label{sec_nat_extension}

\dgg{No part of this has been put into Lean yet, as far as I know. Still working on updating all of the pointers. -- Aug. 12, 2026}

\subsection{Commuting actions}
\label{sec_proof_of_comm_actions_thm}

In this subsection, we prove \cref{mainthm_same_rp_for_comm_actions}, that the sets of regionally proximal pairs under two minimal, commuting actions of commutative semigroups are the same. This result exists for group actions; for example, see \cite[Thm. 1.1]{shao_xu_2026}.  The proof below follows their idea with necessary adaptations for semigroup actions.

% \begin{theorem}
% \label{thm_comm_actions_of_comm_semis_have_same_rp}
%     Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  Suppose $T$ is a commutative semigroup and $X$ is also a $T$-system.  If $S$ and $T$ both act minimally, and if the actions commute, then $\RP^-_S = \RP^-_T$.
% \end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{mainthm_same_rp_for_comm_actions}
    \lean{forTwoMinCommActionsRPsAreSame}
    Let $S$ and $T$ be commutative semigroups.  Let $X$ be both a minimal $S$-system and a minimal $T$-system.  If the actions of $S$ and $T$ commute, then $\RP_S = \RP_T$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_forward_equals_backward_rp}
    By \cref{thm_forward_equals_backward_rp}, we have $\RP_S = \RP_S^-$ and $\RP_T = \RP_T^-$, so it suffices to prove that $\RP_S^- = \RP_T^-$. By the symmetry of $S$ and $T$, it suffices to prove only that $\RP_S^- \subseteq \RP_T^-$.
    
    Suppose $(x,y) \in \RP_S^-$.  Let $\alpha \subseteq X^2$ be a neighborhood of the diagonal, and let $W \subseteq X^2$ be an open neighborhood of $(x,y)$.  We must show that $T^{-1} W \cap \alpha \neq \emptyset$.
    Since $(x,y) \in \RP_S^-$, there exists $s \in S$ such that $s^{-1} W \cap \alpha \neq \emptyset$.  Let $U, V \subseteq X$ be open and non-empty sets such that $U \times V \subseteq s^{-1} W \cap \alpha$.
    Since $S$ acts on $X$ minimally, there exists $\ell \in S$ such that
    \[Y \defeq U \cap \ell^{-1} V \neq \emptyset.\]
    Since $T$ acts on $X$ minimally, there exists  $t \in T$ such that
    \[s^{-1} Y \cap t^{-1} Y \neq \emptyset.\]
    It follows that
    \[
        Z \defeq Y \cap t^{-1} s Y \supseteq Y \cap s t^{-1} Y \neq \emptyset.
    \]
    We have
    \[
        Z \subseteq U \cap t^{-1} s U.
    \]
    Moreover, 
    \[
        \ell Z \subseteq \ell Y \cap \ell t^{-1} s Y \subseteq \ell Y \cap t^{-1} s \ell Y \subseteq V \cap t^{-1} s V.
    \]
    Therefore,
    \[\emptyset \neq Z \times \ell Z \subseteq (U \times V) \cap t^{-1} s (U \times V) \subseteq \alpha \cap t^{-1} W,\]
    as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Grothendieck group}
\label{sec_gro_gp}

It is well known that a commutative, cancellative semigroup $S$ embeds into its group of quotients.
The Grothendieck group, $\gro(S)$, generalizes this by omitting the cancellativity requirement at the cost of downgrading the embedding to a semigroup homomorphism $S \to \gro(S)$.
Since $\gro(S)$ is typically considered for monoids rather than semigroups, we briefly summarize the construction and properties we need in the following theorem.

%\dgg{In mathlib, we may need to do} \verb|Grothendieck (WithOne S)|

\begin{theorem}[The Grothendieck group]
\label{thm_gro_group_props}
\lean{theGrothendieckGroupExists}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup.  There exists an abelian group $\gro(S)$ and a semigroup homomorphism $\igro: S \to \gro(S)$ with the following properties:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item for all finite $F \subseteq \gro(S)$, there exists $s \in S$ such that $F \igro s \subseteq \igro S$;
        %($\igro S$ is a thick subset of $\gro(S)$)
        \item for all $s, t \in S$, $\igro s = \igro t$ if and only if there exists $r \in S$ such that $rs = rt$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Define a relation $\sim$ on $S^2$ by $(a,b) \sim (c,d)$ if there exists $s \in S$ such that $ads = bcs$.  It is a quick exercise to check that $\sim$ is a equivalence relation on $S^2$.
    
    Considering $S^2$ as a semigroup with coordinate-wise multiplication, the relation $\sim$ is multiplicatively invariant: if $(a,b) \sim (c,d)$, then $(s,t) (a,b) \sim (s,t) (c,d)$.
    The quotient $S^2 / \sim$ thus inherits a semigroup structure from $S^2$ via $[(a,b)] [(c,d)] = [(a,b) (c,d)] = [(ac,bd)]$.

    Define $\gro(S) = S^2 / \sim$.  We already have that it is a semigroup.  It is commutative because $S$ is commutative.  To see that it is a group, fix $r \in S$.  The class $[(r,r)]$ is the identity and every element has an inverse: indeed, $[(a,b)] [(b,a)] = [(r,r)]$.  Moreover, the map $\igro: S \to \gro(S)$ defined by $s \mapsto [(sr, r)]$ is a semigroup homomorphism.  We will check both of the properties asserted in the statement of the theorem.

    To see property 1., let $F = \{[(a_1, b_1)], \ldots, [(a_k, b_k)]\} \subseteq \gro(S)$ be finite. Let $s = b_1 \cdots b_k \in S$, and note that for all $i \in \{1, \ldots, k\}$,
    \[[(a_i, b_i)] \igro s = [(a_i b_1 \cdots b_k r, b_i r)] = [(a_i b_1 \cdots \hat b_i \cdots b_k r, r)] \in \igro(S),\]
    where $b_1 \cdots \hat b_i \cdots b_k$ denotes the product of all of the $b_j$'s except $b_i$.

    To see property 2., let $s, t \in S$.  Note that $\igro s = \igro t$ if and only if $(sr, r) \sim (tr, r)$, which happens if and only if there exists $p \in S$ such that $sr^2p = tr^2p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_igro_same_implies_elts_act_same}
\lean{surjectiveActionsFactorThroughGroGroupHom}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, let $X$ be an $S$-system, and let $s, t \in S$.  If $S$ acts by surjections and $\igro s = \igro t$, then for all $x \in X$, $sx = tx$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_gro_group_props}
\leanok{}
    For $s \in S$, denote by $f_s$ the element of $C(X,X)$ given by $s$.  Suppose $S$ acts by surjections and $\igro s = \igro t$. By \cref{thm_gro_group_props}, we have that there exists $r \in S$ such that $rs = rt$.  We wish to show that $f_s = f_t$.  
    % If $s = t$, this is immediate, so suppose there exists $r \in S$ such that $rs = rt$.

    Let $x \in X$. Since $S$ acts by surjections, there exists $y \in X$ such that $ry = x$.  Then
    \[tx = try = sry = sx.\]
    Since $x \in X$ was arbitrary, we see $f_s = f_t$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_extend_homeo_action_to_gr_group}
\lean{homDSofGroGroupDSIsOriginalSystem}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system.  If $S$ acts on $X$ by homeomorphisms, then there exists an action of $\gro(S)$ on $X$ by homeomorphisms such that the original $S$-action is the action via the homomorphism $\igro: S \to \gro(S)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_gro_group_props,thm_igro_same_implies_elts_act_same}
    For $s \in S$, denote by $f_s$ the homeomorphism of $X$ given by the action of $s$ on $X$. Note that since $S$ is commutative and $f_s$ is a homeomorphism, for all $s, t \in S$, $f_s \circ f_t = f_t \circ f_s$ and $f_s^{-1} \circ f_t^{-1} = f_t^{-1} \circ f_s^{-1}$.
    
    We wish to define an action of $\gro(S)$ on $X$ by homeomorphisms. Let $g \in \gro(S)$. By \cref{thm_gro_group_props}, there exists $s, t \in S$ such that $\igro(s) g = \igro(t)$, that is, $g = \igro(t) \igro(s)^{-1}$. Define $f_g: X \to X$ by $f_g = f_t \circ f_s^{-1}$.  To see that this is well-defined, suppose that $s_1, s_2, t_1, t_2 \in S$ are such that
    \[g = \igro(t_1) \igro(s_1)^{-1} = \igro(t_2) \igro(s_2)^{-1}.\]
    We have that $\igro(t_1 s_2) = \igro(t_2 s_1)$, whence by \cref{thm_igro_same_implies_elts_act_same},
    \[f_{t_1} \circ f_{s_2} = f_{t_1 s_2} = f_{t_2 s_1} = f_{t_2} \circ f_{s_1}.\]
    Composing with $f_{s_1}^{-1} \circ f_{s_2}^{-1}$, we see
    \[f_{t_1} \circ f_{s_1}^{-1} = f_{t_1} \circ f_{s_2} \circ f_{s_1}^{-1} \circ f_{s_2}^{-1} = f_{t_2} \circ f_{s_1} \circ f_{s_1}^{-1} \circ f_{s_2}^{-1} = f_{t_2} \circ f_{s_2}^{-1},\]
    as was to be shown.

    We claim that $\gro(S) \times X \to X$ given by $(g,x) \mapsto f_g x$ yields an action of $\gro(S)$ on $X$ by homeomorphisms.  Each $f_g$ is a homeomorphism by its definition. To see that it is an action, we need only check that for all $g, h \in \gro(S)$, $f_g \circ f_h = f_{gh}$.  Write $g = \igro(t_1) \igro(s_1)^{-1}$ and $h = \igro(t_2) \igro(s_2)^{-1}$.  Then $gh = \igro(t_1 t_2) \igro(s_1 s_2)^{-1}$, so we see
    \[f_{gh} = f_{t_1 t_2} \circ f_{s_1s_2}^{-1} = f_{t_1} \circ f_{t_2} \circ f_{s_1}^{-1} \circ f_{s_2}^{-1} = f_{t_1} \circ f_{s_1}^{-1} \circ f_{t_2} \circ f_{s_2}^{-1} = f_g \circ f_{h},\]
    as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{cor_S_and_GS_actions}
\lean{extendedActionRPisActionRP}
\leanok{}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, and let $X$ be a minimal $S$-system.  Suppose $S$ acts by homeomorphisms, and consider $X$ as a $\gro(S)$-system by \cref{thm_extend_homeo_action_to_gr_group}. We have $\RP_S = \RP_{\gro(S)}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\uses{mainthm_same_rp_for_comm_actions}
    Apply \cref{mainthm_same_rp_for_comm_actions}, noting that $S$ and $\gro(S)$ are commutative semigroups whose actions commute and are minimal.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The natural extension}
\label{sec_nat_ext}

In this section, we assume that $S$ is a commutative semigroup, that $X$ is an $S$-system, and that $S$ acts by surjections.  We show in \cref{lemma_nat_ext_props} that the natural extension of $X$ exists, and we show in \cref{lemma_nat_ext_univeral_prop} that it satisfies a universal property.
To see why the surjectivity assumption is necessary, note that if $\pi: W \to X$ is the natural extension of $S$-systems, then $\pi$ is a surjection and $S$ acts on $W$ by homeomorphisms.  For any $s \in S$, the map $\pi \circ s = s \circ \pi$ will be surjective, and hence it must be that $s : X \to X$ is surjective.

Let $\gro(S)$ be the Grothendieck group of $S$ from \cref{thm_gro_group_props}, and denote its identity element by $e_{\gro(S)}$.  The space of all functions $\gro(S) \to X$, that is, the product space $X^{\gro(S)}$, is a compact Hausdorff space with the product topology.  The group $\gro(S)$ acts on $X^{\gro(S)}$ by homeomorphisms:
\[
    \forall g, h \in \gro(S), \ \forall \varphi \in X^{\gro(S)}, \ (g \varphi) (h) = \varphi(hg).
\]
We treat $X^{\gro(S)}$ as an $S$-system via the homomorphism $\igro: S \to \gro(S)$, as described at the top of \cref{sec_dynamics}.

% \begin{definition}[The natural extension]
% \label{def_nat_ext}
%     Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system on which $S$ acts by surjections.  Define
%     \[W = \big\{ \varphi: \gro(S) \to X \mid \forall g \in \gro(S), \ \forall s \in S, \ \varphi((\igro s)g) = s \varphi (g) \big\} \subseteq X^{\gro(S)}.\]
%     Define $\pi: W \to X$ by $\pi (\varphi) = \varphi(e_{\gro(S)})$.
% \end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The natural extension]
\label{lemma_nat_ext_props}
Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system on which $S$ acts by surjections. The set
\[
    W \defeq \big\{ \varphi: \gro(S) \to X \mid \forall g \in \gro(S), \ \forall s \in S, \ \varphi((\igro s)g) = s (\varphi (g)) \big\} \subseteq X^{\gro(S)}
\]
is a non-empty, closed, $\gro(S)$-invariant subset of $X^{\gro(S)}$. Thus, $W$ is a $\gro(S)$-system, and hence, by way of the homomorphism $\igro: S \to \gro(S)$, an $S$-system. The map $\pi: W \to X$ defined by
\[\pi (\varphi) \defeq \varphi(e_{\gro(S)})\]
is a factor map of $S$-systems.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_gro_group_props,thm_igro_same_implies_elts_act_same}
    That $W$ is closed and $\pi$ is continuous follow easily from the definition of the product topology on $X^{\gro(S)}$.

    To see that $W$ is $\gro(S)$-invariant, for $\varphi \in W$ and $h \in \gro(S)$, we must show that $h \varphi \in W$.  We must check that for all $g \in \gro(S)$ and $s \in S$, we have
    \[
        (h \varphi) ((\igro s)g) = s \big((h \varphi) (g) \big).
    \]
    Let $g \in \gro(S)$ and $s \in S$.  We see
    \[
        (h \varphi) ((\igro s)g) = \varphi((\igro s)gh) = s \big(\varphi(gh)\big) = s \big((h \varphi) (g) \big),
    \]
    as desired.

    Now we show $W$ is non-empty and $\pi$ is surjective. For $F \subseteq \gro(S)$ finite, define
    \[
        W_F = \big\{ \varphi: \gro(S) \to X \mid \forall g \in F, \ \forall s \in S, \ \varphi((\igro s)g) = s \big(\varphi (g)\big) \big\} \subseteq X^{\gro(S)}.
    \]
    It is clear that $W = \bigcap_F W_F$, where the intersection is taken over all finite sets $F \subseteq \gro(S)$.  We will show that for all $x \in X$ and all finite $F \subseteq \gro(S)$, there exists $\varphi \in W_F$ so that $\varphi(e_{\gro(S)}) = x$.  This will show that $W$ is non-empty and that $\pi$ is surjective. 
    Indeed, for any $x \in X$,
    \[\big\{ W_F \cap \{\varphi \in X^{\gro(S)} \mid \varphi(e_{\gro(S)}) = x\} \mid F \subseteq \gro(S) \text{ finite} \big\}\]
    will be a family of non-empty, closed sets with the finite intersection property.  By compactness, there will be some function in every member of the family, and that function will belong to $W$ and map to $x$ under $\pi$.

    Let $F \subseteq \gro(S)$ be finite and $x \in X$.  Without loss of generality, we can assume $e_{\gro(S)} \in F$. By \cref{thm_gro_group_props}, there exists $h \in \gro(S)$ such that $F \subseteq \igro(S) h$.  In particular, there exists $s \in S$ such that $\igro(s) h = e_{\gro(S)}$.  By our standing assumptions, $s: X \to X$ is a surjection, so there exists $y \in X$ such that $sy = x$.

    Define $\varphi : \gro(S) \to X$ by
    \[
        \varphi(g) = \begin{cases} ty & \text{ if } \exists t \in S, \ g = \igro(t) h \\ y & \text{ else } \end{cases}.
    \]
    We must verify that $\varphi$ is well-defined. If there exists $t, r \in S$ such that $g = \igro(t) h = \igro(r) h$, then $\igro(t) = \igro(r)$, and so by \cref{thm_igro_same_implies_elts_act_same}, $ty = ry$.  Note that $\varphi(h) = y$.  Indeed, consider two cases: A) there is $t \in S$ such that $h = \igro(t) h$, and B) there is no such $t$.  In case A, $\igro(t) = e_{\gro(S)}$, so $\igro(t) = \igro(t^2)$. Since $t: X \to X$ is a surjection, there exists $z \in X$ such that $tz = y$.  By \cref{thm_igro_same_implies_elts_act_same}, we have that
    \[\varphi(h) = ty = t^2 z = tz = y,\]
    as claimed. In case B, by definition, $\varphi(h) = y$, and we are done.
    
    To see that $\varphi \in W_F$, suppose $g \in F$ and $t \in S$.  Write $g = \igro(r)h$ for some $r \in S$. By the definition of $\varphi$, we see that
    \[
        \varphi(\igro(t) g) = \varphi(\igro(t)\igro(r)h) = \varphi(\igro(tr)h) = (tr)y = t(ry) = t \big(\varphi(\igro(r)h)\big) = t\big(\varphi(g)\big).
    \]
    To see that $\varphi(e_{\gro(S)}) = x$, we see by the definition of $\varphi$ that
    \[\varphi(e_{\gro(S)}) = \varphi(\igro(s)h) = sy = x.\]

    We now prove the map $\pi$ intertwines with the action of $S$. (Recall that $W$ is an $S$-system by the homomorphism $\igro: S \to \gro(S)$, meaning that $s \varphi$ is defined as $\igro(s) \varphi$.)  For this, let $\varphi \in W$ and $s \in S$.  We see
    \[
        s \big(\pi (\varphi)\big) = s \big(\varphi(e_{\gro(S)})\big) = \varphi(\igro(s) e_{\gro(S)}) = (\igro(s) \varphi)(e_{\gro(S)}) = (s \varphi)(e_{\gro(S)}) = \pi (s \varphi),
    \]
    as was to be shown.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Universal property of the natural extension]
\label{lemma_nat_ext_univeral_prop}
Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be an $S$-system on which $S$ acts by surjections. The natural extension $\pi: W \to X$ is the smallest extension of the $S$-system $X$ on which $S$ acts by homeomorphisms: if $\rho: V \to X$ is an extension of $S$-systems and $S$ acts on $V$ by homeomorphisms, then there exists a factor map $\xi: V \to W$ of $S$-systems so that $\rho = \pi \circ \xi$.  In particular, if $S$ acts on $X$ by homeomorphisms, then $\pi$ is an isomorphism of $S$-systems.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_extend_homeo_action_to_gr_group,thm_gro_group_props}
    Suppose $\rho: V \to X$ is a factor map of $S$-systems and that $S$ acts on $V$ by homeomorphisms.  By \cref{thm_extend_homeo_action_to_gr_group}, since $S$ acts on $V$ by homeomorphisms, we get an action of $\gro(S)$ on $V$ that extends it. Note that this means that for all $v \in V$ and $s \in S$, we have
    \begin{align}
        \label{eqn_rho_factor_consequence}
        s (\rho v) = \rho( sv) = \rho ( (\igro s) v).
    \end{align}
    Define $\xi: V \to X^{\gro(S)}$ by
    \[\xi: v \mapsto \big( \xi_v : g \mapsto \rho (g v) \big).\]
    We will show that $\xi$ is continuous, that the image of $\xi$ is in $W$, that $\xi: V \to W$ is a factor map of $S$-systems, and that $\rho = \pi \circ \xi$.

    To see that $\xi$ is continuous, consider a cylinder set of $X^{\gro(S)}$ of the form
    \[[U]_{g} \defeq \{\varphi: \gro(S) \to X \mid \varphi(g) \in U \},\]
    where $g \in \gro(S)$ and $U \subseteq X$ is open. It is quick to check that
    \[\xi^{-1} [U]_g = g^{-1} \rho^{-1} U,\]
    which is an open subset of $V$. Finite intersections of such cylinders, which generate the topology on $X^{\gro(S)}$, will therefore have open pre-images under $\xi$, demonstrating that $\xi$ is continuous.
    
    Next we show that the image of $\xi$ is in $W$. Let $v \in V$, $g \in \gro(S)$, and $s \in S$.  We must show
    \begin{align*}
        \xi_v((\igro s) g) = s ( \xi_v(g)).
    \end{align*}
    We see that
    \[\xi_v((\igro s) g) = \rho \big( ((\igro s) g) v\big) = s \big( \rho (gv) \big) = s \big( \xi_v(g) \big),\]
    where the first and third equalities follows from the definition of $\xi_v$ and the second follows from \eqref{eqn_rho_factor_consequence}.

    To see that $\xi$ surjects onto $W$, we will show that the image of $\xi$ is dense in $W$.  This suffices since the image of $V$ under $\xi$ is closed, and hence equal to $W$.  Let $\varphi \in W$ and $g_1, \ldots, g_k \in \gro(S)$.  We will show that there exists $v \in V$ such that for all $j$, $\xi_v(g_j) = \varphi(g_j)$. By \cref{thm_gro_group_props}, there exists $h \in \gro(S)$ and $s_1, \ldots, s_k \in S$ such that $g_j = \igro(s_j) h$.  Since $h: V \to V$ is a homeomorphism and $\rho: V \to X$ is surjective, there exists $v \in h^{-1} \rho^{-1} \{ \varphi(h) \}$.  It follows that $\xi_v(h) = \rho ( hv) = \varphi(h)$. Then
    \[\varphi(g_j) = \varphi((\igro s_j) h) = s_j \big(\varphi (h)\big) = s_j \big(\xi_v(h)\big) = \xi_v((\igro s_j) h) = \xi_v(g_j),\]
    as was to be shown.

    We have only left to show that $\xi$ intertwines the action of $S$ on $V$ and $W$.  Let $v \in V$ and $s \in S$.  We must show that $\xi_{sv} = s \xi_v$.  Let $g \in \gro(S)$.  We see
    \begin{gather*}
        \xi_{sv}(g) = \rho(g(sv)) = \rho ((\igro(s) (gv)) = s \big(\rho (gv)\big) \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \\
        \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad = s \big(\xi_v(g)\big) = \xi_v( (\igro s) g) = \xi_v( g(\igro s)) = (s \xi_v) (g),
    \end{gather*}
    as was to be shown.

    It remains to prove if $S$ acts by homeomorphisms, then $\pi$ is injective, and hence is an isomorphism of $S$-systems. Note that this follows immediately from the universal property.  Indeed, the universal property gives that since $S$ acts on $X$ via homeomorphisms, we have factor maps $X \to W \to X$ that compose to give the identity.  But this means that $\pi: W \to X$ is injective.
\end{proof}
    %Direct proof of last part: Suppose $\pi \varphi = \pi \psi$, so that $\varphi (e_{\gro(S)}) = \psi (e_{\gro(S)})$.  We want to show that for all $g \in \gro(S)$, $\varphi(g) = \psi(g)$. Let $g \in \gro(S)$. By \cref{thm_gro_group_props}, $\igro(S)$ is thick in $\gro(S)$, so there exists $s, t \in S$ such that $\igro(s)g = \igro(t)$. We see then that
    % \[s \varphi(g) = \varphi(\igro(s) g) = \varphi(\igro(t)e_{\gro(S)}) = t\varphi(e_{\gro(S)}) = t\psi(e_{\gro(S)}) = \psi( \igro(s) g) = s \psi(g).\]
    % Since $S$ acts by homeomorphisms, it follows that $\varphi(g) = \psi(g)$, as desired.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{lemma_nat_ext_minimal}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, $X$ be an $S$-system on which $S$ acts by surjections, and $\pi: W \to X$ be its natural extension.  The system $X$ is a minimal $S$-system if and only if the system $W$ is a minimal $S$-system.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{thm_gro_group_props}
    Since $\pi: W \to X$ is a factor map of $S$-systems, if $W$ is a minimal $S$-system, then it is immediate that $X$ is a minimal $S$-system.

    Suppose conversely that $X$ is a minimal $S$-system. Let $\varphi, \psi \in W$, and let $U \subseteq W$ be an open neighborhood of $\psi$. We will show that there exists $t \in S$ such that $t \varphi \in U$.
    
    By the definition of product topology on $X^{\gro(S)}$, the set $U$ contains a ``cylinder set'' around $\psi$. This means there exists a finite set $F \subseteq \gro(S)$ and, for all $f \in F$, an open neighborhood $U_f \subseteq X$ of $\psi(f)$ satisfying the following: for any $\chi \in W$, if $\chi(f) \in U_f$ for all $f \in F$, then $\chi \in U$.

    By \cref{thm_gro_group_props}, there exists $h \in \gro(S)$ such that for all $f \in F$, there exists $s_f \in S$ such that $f = \igro(s_f) h$.  Thus, for all $f \in F$, we have that
    \[s_f \big(\psi (h)\big) = \psi( \igro(s_f) h) = \psi (f) \in U_f,\]
    whereby $\psi(h) \in \bigcap_{f \in F} s_f^{-1} U_f$.

    By the minimality of $X$, there exists $t \in S$ such that $t\varphi(h) \in \bigcap_{f \in F} s_f^{-1} U_f$; that is $s_f t \varphi(h) \in U_f$ for all $f \in F$. Moreover, for $f \in F$,
    \[
        s_f t \big(\varphi(h)\big) = \varphi( \igro(s_f) \igro(t) h) = (t \varphi) (\igro(s_f) h) = (t \varphi) (f),
    \]
    whereby $(t \varphi) (f) \in U_f$.  It follows that $t \varphi \in U$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{cor_nat_ext_for_min_comm_act}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup and $X$ be a minimal $S$-system.  The natural extension $\pi: W \to X$ as described in \cref{lemma_nat_ext_props} exists, satisfies the universal property described in \cref{lemma_nat_ext_univeral_prop}, and is a minimal $S$-system.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_minimal_comm_action_implies_surjective,lemma_nat_ext_props,lemma_nat_ext_univeral_prop,lemma_nat_ext_minimal}
    By \cref{lemma_minimal_comm_action_implies_surjective}, the semigroup $S$ acts by surjections.  Therefore, the hypotheses required for \cref{lemma_nat_ext_props}, \cref{lemma_nat_ext_univeral_prop}, and \cref{lemma_nat_ext_minimal} are met.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A proof of \cref{mainthm_kronecker_is_same_as_extension_kronecker}}
\label{sec_proof_of_same_kron}

In the statement of the following theorem, recall from \cref{cor_nat_ext_for_min_comm_act} that the natural extension for minimal actions by commutative semigroups exists.
The proof of \cref{thm_rp_of_natural_extension} relies crucially on \cref{thm_rp_strong_s_inv}, which we derive from \cref{mainthm_RP_is_eq_relation}.

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm_rp_of_natural_extension}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, $X$ be a minimal $S$-system, and $\pi: W \to X$ be its natural extension. We have that
    \[
        (\pi \times \pi)^{-1} \RP_X = \RP_W.
    \]
    As a result, the map $W/ \RP_W \to X / \RP_X$ given by 
    \[[w]_{\RP_W} \mapsto [\pi w]_{\RP_X}\]
    is an isomorphism of $S$-systems.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\uses{lemma_frp_functorial_property,thm_gro_group_props,lemma_frp_basics,thm_rp_strong_s_inv}
    That $\RP_W \subseteq (\pi \times \pi)^{-1} \RP_X$ follows from \cref{lemma_frp_functorial_property}.  To see that $(\pi \times \pi)^{-1} \RP_X \subseteq \RP_W$, let $(\varphi, \psi) \in W^2$, and suppose $(\pi \varphi, \pi \psi) \in \RP_X$.  We must show that $(\varphi, \psi) \in \RP_W$.
    
    First, we claim that $(\varphi(g), \psi(g)) \in \RP_X$ for all $g \in \gro(S)$.  Indeed, let $g \in \gro(S)$.  By \cref{thm_gro_group_props}, there exist $s, t \in S$ such that $(\igro s)g = \igro(t)$.  Since
    \[
        (\varphi(e_{\gro(S)}), \psi(e_{\gro(S)})) = (\pi \varphi, \pi \psi) \in \RP_X,
    \]
    by the $S$-invariance of $\RP_X$ (see \cref{lemma_frp_basics}), we have that
    \[
        s(\varphi(g), \psi(g)) = (\varphi((\igro s)g), \psi((\igro s)g)) = t (\varphi(e_{\gro(S)}), \psi(e_{\gro(S)})) \in t\RP_X \subseteq \RP_X.
    \]
    By \cref{thm_rp_strong_s_inv}, $s(\varphi(g), \psi(g)) \in \RP_X$ implies that $(\varphi(g), \psi(g)) \in \RP_X$, as desired.

    Now we will show that $(\varphi, \psi) \in \RP_W$.  Let $U, V \subseteq W$ and $\alpha \subseteq W^2$ be open neighborhoods of $\varphi$, $\psi$, and $\Delta_W$ respectively. By the definition of product topology on $W \subseteq X^{\gro(S)}$, there exists a finite set $F \subseteq \gro(S)$ and, for all $f \in F$, open sets $U_f, V_f \subseteq X, \alpha_f \subseteq X^2$ containing $\varphi(f)$, $\psi(f)$, and $\Delta_X$ respectively such that
    \begin{itemize}
        \item for $\chi \in W$, if $\chi(f) \in U_f$ for all $f \in F$, then $\chi \in U$,

        \item for $\chi \in W$, if $\chi (f) \in V_f$ for all $f \in F$, then $\chi \in V$,

        \item for $(\chi_1, \chi_2) \in W^2$, if $(\chi_1(f), \chi_2(f)) \in \alpha_f$ for all $f \in F$, then $(\chi_1, \chi_2) \in \alpha$.
    \end{itemize}
    
    By \cref{thm_gro_group_props}, there exists $h \in \gro(S)$ and, for all $f \in F$, $s_f \in S$ such that $f = (\igro s_f) h$.
    Then for all $f \in F$, we have 
    \[
        s_f \big(\varphi(h)\big) = \varphi((\igro s_f) h) = \varphi (f) \in U_f.
    \]
    It follows that $\bigcap_{f \in F} s_f^{-1} U_f$ is an open neighborhood of $\varphi(h)$.
    Similarly, $\bigcap_{f \in F} s_f^{-1} V_f$ and $\bigcap_{f \in F} s_f^{-1} \alpha_f$ are open neighborhoods of $\psi(h)$ and $\Delta_X$ respectively. By the first claim, $(\varphi(h), \psi(h)) \in \RP_X$, so there exists $s \in S$ such that
    \begin{align}
        \label{eqn_nonempty_subset_of_x2}
        \left( \bigcap_{f \in F} s_f^{-1} U_f \times \bigcap_{f \in F} s_f^{-1} V_f \right) \cap s^{-1} \bigcap_{f \in F} s_f^{-1} \alpha_f \neq \emptyset.
    \end{align}
    Since $\gro(S)$ acts on $W$ by homeomorphisms and $\pi$ is surjective, the map $\pi \circ h: W \to X$ defined by $\chi \mapsto \chi(h)$ is surjective.  Hence there exists $(\eta, \rho) \in W^2$ such that $(\eta(h), \rho(h))$ belongs to the set in \eqref{eqn_nonempty_subset_of_x2}. We see that for all $f \in F$,
    \[(\eta(f), \rho(f)) = s_f (\eta(h), \rho(h)) \in U_f \times V_f,\]
    whereby $(\eta,\rho) \in U \times V$. Moreover, for all $f \in F$,
    \[s (\eta(f), \rho(f)) = s s_f (\eta(h), \rho(h)) \in \alpha_f,\]
    whereby $(\eta, \rho) \in s^{-1} \alpha$.
    Therefore,
    \[(\eta, \rho) \in (U \times V) \cap s^{-1} \alpha,\]
    showing that $(\varphi,\psi) \in \RP_W$, as was to be shown.

    Finally, we will show that the map $W/ \RP_W \to X / \RP_X$ given by 
    \begin{align}
    \label{eqn_factor_quotient_map_def}
        [w]_{\RP_W} \mapsto [\pi w]_{\RP_X}
    \end{align}
    is an isomorphism of $S$-systems.  Since $(\pi \times \pi) \RP_W \subseteq \RP_X$, the surjective composition $W \to X \to X/\RP_X$ descends to the map $W / \RP_W \to X / \RP_X$ defined in \eqref{eqn_factor_quotient_map_def}, showing that the map in \eqref{eqn_factor_quotient_map_def} is well-defined, continuous (by a defining characteristic of the quotient topology), and surjective.
    It intertwines the actions of $S$ since $\RP_W$ and $\RP_X$ are $S$-invariant.

    We only have left to show that $\pi$ is injective.  Suppose $w, z \in W$ so that $[\pi w]_{\RP_X} = [\pi z]_{\RP_X}$.  Then $(\pi \times \pi) (w,z) \in \RP_X$.  Since $(\pi \times \pi)^{-1} \RP_X = \RP_W$, we have that $(w,z) \in \RP_W$, whereby $[w]_{\RP_W} = [z]_{\RP_W}$, as was to be shown.
\end{proof}

We conclude this section by proving \cref{mainthm_kronecker_is_same_as_extension_kronecker}.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{mainthm_kronecker_is_same_as_extension_kronecker}
    Let $S$ be a commutative semigroup, $X$ be a minimal $S$-system, and $\pi: W \to X$ be its natural extension. The following $S$-systems are the same up to isomorphism:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item \label{item_x_kron}
        $X/\RP_{X,S}$, the largest equicontinuous factor of the $S$-system $X$;
        \item \label{item_w_kron}
        $W / \RP_{W,S}$, the largest equicontinuous factor of the natural extension $W$ of $X$ as an $S$-system; and
        \item \label{item_w_kron_gp}
        $W / \RP_{W,\gro(S)}$, the largest equicontinuous factor of the natural extension $W$ of $X$ as a $\gro(S)$-system, made into an $S$-system via the homomorphism $\igro: S \to \gro(S)$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{mainthm_kronecker_is_same_as_extension_kronecker}]
\uses{thm_rp_of_natural_extension,lemma_nat_ext_minimal,mainthm_same_rp_for_comm_actions}
    That \eqref{item_x_kron} is isomorphic to \eqref{item_w_kron} follows from \cref{thm_rp_of_natural_extension}.
    
    To see that \eqref{item_w_kron} is isomorphic to \eqref{item_w_kron_gp}, note first that since $X$ is a minimal $S$-system,  so is $W$ (by \cref{lemma_nat_ext_minimal}). It follows that $W$ is a minimal $\gro(S)$-system. Since the actions of $S$ and $\gro(S)$ commute, \cref{mainthm_same_rp_for_comm_actions} gives that $\RP_{W,S} = \RP_{W,\gro(S)}$.  Therefore, as sets $W / \RP_{W,S} = W / \RP_{W,\gro(S)}$.  We need only show that $S$ acts on this set in the same way in both descriptions.  But this is immediate since both actions are induced from the action of $S$ on $W$.
\end{proof}
